**Dr. Arvind Mewada** is an Assistant Professor at Bennett University, Greater Noida, India. He holds an M.Tech. in Computer Science and Engineering from the Maulana Azad National Institute of Technology, Bhopal, and a B.E. in Information Technology from Rajiv Gandhi Proudyogiki Vishwavidyalaya, Bhopal, India. # General Introduction

Blockchain technology, introduced in 2008 as the foundation for Bitcoin, has since emerged as a transformative innovation with applications extending far beyond cryptocurrency. It provides a decentralized, transparent, and tamper-proof ledger system that addresses critical issues like data integrity, trust, and security in digital interactions. Blockchain’s potential to eliminate intermediaries and enable peer-to-peer transactions has implications for sectors such as finance, healthcare, supply chain management, and governance. For example, blockchain can enhance transparency in supply chains by enabling real-time tracking of goods, ensuring authenticity and ethical sourcing. Similarly, it can revolutionize governance systems through secure and transparent voting mechanisms, fostering trust in democratic processes.

Despite its promise, blockchain faces significant challenges that limit its broader adoption. Scalability remains a pressing concern, with many networks struggling to match the transaction speeds of traditional systems. In addition, issues like high energy consumption, regulatory uncertainty, and difficulties in integrating blockchain with existing infrastructures pose barriers.

This book aims to explore the dual nature of blockchain’s societal impact—its ability to drive progress while contending with persistent challenges. By critically examining its applications, limitations, and potential, this study seeks to provide actionable insights to guide stakeholders in maximizing blockchain’s benefits while addressing its risks. The findings will contribute to shaping policies and strategies that ensure blockchain becomes a powerful tool for societal advancement, promoting transparency, efficiency, and inclusivity across various domains.

This book offers readers the opportunity to engage with cutting-edge technologies in the blockchain. It consists of a collection of 20 chapters, grouped into five key themes:

*   Healthcare Transformation
*   Integration with Emerging Technologies
*   Revolutionizing Finance
*   Societal and Industrial Impacts
*   Security and Cryptography

This structure provides a comprehensive overview of the current state and future directions of block technologies in the context of society development.

xvi 

## Section 1: Healthcare Transformation

This section deals with possible healthcare transformation in blockchain. Blockchain’s role in the healthcare sector is revolutionary, as highlighted in several chapters. The technology enables secure data sharing, improves medical record analytics, and streamlines healthcare data management. These chapters emphasize blockchain’s ability to enhance patient care by addressing privacy concerns and introducing secure frameworks for maintaining and sharing sensitive patient information. Specific use cases include blockchain’s application in drug traceability, ensuring data integrity in clinical trials, and enabling interoperability among healthcare providers. The integration of blockchain with the Internet of Things (IoT) in healthcare is also discussed, demonstrating how intelligent systems can further optimize patient care and operational efficiency.

## Section 2: Integration with Emerging Technologies

This section includes topics related to the integration of blockchain with emerging technologies. Blockchain’s synergy with emerging technologies like the IoT and artificial intelligence (AI) is a recurring theme. Chapters in this section explore the architectural integration of blockchain with IoT to address challenges such as data scalability, security, and privacy. Intelligent learning models powered by AI are examined for their potential to enhance blockchain-enabled systems. These chapters also delve into how the convergence of these technologies facilitates efficient data analysis, secure decision-making, and the development of self-learning networks. The integration of blockchain and IoT is presented as a solution to long-standing challenges, creating systems that are secure, scalable, and efficient.

## Section 3: Revolutionizing Finance

In this section, blockchain concepts in finance are explored. The financial sector has been profoundly impacted by blockchain technology, as extensively analysed in this volume. The chapters in this section provide insights into blockchain’s disruptive influence on traditional financial frameworks. From streamlining international payments to enabling decentralized finance platforms (DeFi), blockchain is transforming financial operations. The chapters discuss how blockchain enhances transparency, reduces costs, and increases efficiency in financial transactions. In addition, the potential of blockchain to enable asset tokenization, smart contracts, and decentralized autonomous organizations is explored, showcasing its ability to modernize and democratize the financial sector. Regulatory challenges, scalability issues, and future research directions are also addressed, providing a balanced view of the opportunities and limitations in this domain.

xvii 

## Section 4: Societal and Industrial Impacts

In this section, the societal and industrial impacts of blockchain are explored. Three chapters are presented in this section. Blockchain’s applications extend beyond traditional sectors to address societal and industrial challenges. This section highlights blockchain’s role in fostering sustainable energy trading within microgrids, improving supply chain transparency, and revolutionizing biotechnology. Chapters examine the transformative impact of blockchain on social governance, emphasizing its potential to enhance accountability and equity in management practices. Use cases include blockchain’s application in seed supply chain management, which improves traceability and reduces inefficiencies, and its role in supporting corporate social responsibility and social impact investing. The volume underscores blockchain’s ability to drive sustainability initiatives and create ethical, transparent systems across industries.

## Section 5: Security and Cryptography

This section briefly explores the security concerns and cryptography used in blockchain. Security is a critical aspect of blockchain technology, and several chapters are dedicated to exploring its mechanisms and challenges. These chapters provide a detailed analysis of how blockchain ensures resilience against cyber threats through robust cryptographic techniques. Topics include the use of hash functions, digital signatures, and public key cryptography to maintain data integrity and trust fewer consensuses among network participants. The chapters also address challenges such as scalability, energy consumption, and the regulatory landscape, proposing solutions to enhance the security and efficiency of blockchain systems. Real-world applications, such as secure supply chain management and smart contract deployment, are discussed to illustrate the practical implications of blockchain’s security features.xviii

# Section 1 *Healthcare Transformation*

# 1 Harnessing Blockchain Technology for Enhanced Healthcare Data Management and Analytics

K. Lalitha, V. Megala, and Khalid Abdullah Fakeeh

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-2](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-2)

## 1.1 Introduction

Timely interchange of patient data among diverse healthcare services significantly reduces costs, improves treatment, and strengthens disease surveillance \[[1](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_1)\]. Transmitting medical images or patient data through a centralised data centre is inappropriate, compromising storage and security settings \[[2](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_2)\]. The lack of secure connections to integrate several independent health systems into an accessible network while protecting the privacy of healthcare professionals constitutes a significant healthcare challenge. The Ethereum blockchain platform was established to demonstrate the efficacy of blockchain in managing sensitive data across various sectors, including finance, information technology, healthcare, security, real estate, the Internet of Things, and public services \[[3](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_3)\]. Blockchain offers solutions to critical healthcare issues, including secure medical record interchange and adherence to data protection regulations. Blockchain is a technology developed in 2008 as part of the Bitcoin proposal to alter or, more precisely, supplant electronic transactions \[[4](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_4),[5](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_5)\]. Blockchain transactions are generated and preserved chronologically; participants can monitor digital money and user information without relying on centralised record-keeping. This technology’s key attribute is the capability to store a complete copy of the blockchain on the nodes participating in the network \[[6](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_6)\].

Moreover, each transaction is traceable, hence complicating the perpetration of fraud. The term “blockchain” lacks a broadly accepted definition due to its complex nature, which includes technological, operational, legal, and regulatory aspects \[[5](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_5),[7](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_7)\]. Blockchain is a technology developed in 2008 as a component of the Bitcoin proposal to alter or, more precisely, supplant electronic transactions \[[8](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_8)\]. Due to the historical creation and maintenance of blockchain transactions, users can monitor digital currency and patient information without the need for centralised record-keeping \[[9](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_9)\]. The fundamental attribute of blockchain is its ability to store a complete copy of the entire blockchain on the nodes participating in the network \[[10](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_10)\].

4Moreover, each transaction is traceable, hence complicating the perpetration of fraud. It is crucial to highlight that “blockchain” lacks a universally agreed definition because of its multifaceted nature, encompassing technological, operational, legal, and regulatory dimensions. The implementation of blockchain technology might significantly revolutionise the healthcare sector through crucial opportunities such as decentralised storage, which allows data to be transparent and accessible to third parties with the creator’s consent. Maintaining many copies of data across diverse places constitutes a decentralised approach to data storage \[[11](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_11)\]. Consent is the global consensus algorithm that will govern access, storage, and distribution. Data modification means that data alterations may only occur with the autonomous permission of all relevant parties. Immutability means that the modification of any patient data is prohibited. Data stored in a certain block is immutable and cannot be modified or amended at any time. Enhanced capacity means upholding data privacy using blockchain, which is cost-effective due to the absence of intermediaries and the streamlined process for obtaining permissions \[[12](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_12)\].

[Section 1.2](#C_004_c1.xhtml#h1_2) addresses the rationale for adopting blockchain technology in healthcare, while [Section 1.3](#C_004_c1.xhtml#h1_3) outlines the fundamental aspects of the blockchain deployment process. [Section 1.4](#C_004_c1.xhtml#h1_4) addresses potential blockchain implementation strategies for healthcare sub-domains. [Section 1.5](#C_004_c1.xhtml#h1_5) examines the issues associated with blockchain technology.

The main objective of this study is to investigate and analyse blockchain technology in connection to healthcare applications. This study also investigates the requirements of blockchain and the main difficulties in building blockchain- integrated healthcare systems. In addition, the importance of smart contracts in healthcare systems is explored. Blockchain is a network of authenticated users or nodes that manages a decentralised public ledger database. It has immutable data blocks that can be securely transferred without the help of third-party signatures and consensus in cryptography.

## 1.2 Blockchain Technology in Healthcare

### 1.2.1 Distributed Ledger Technology

Blockchain, a form of distributed ledger technology (DLT), is illustrated in [Figure 1.1](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_1). The fundamental concept of DLT is that all users have access to a shared database. Bitcoin introduced the notion of an open, universally accessible ledger, providing the initial solution to the difficulty of establishing confidence in an unsafe environment without reliance on a third party. DLT designates stockholders as nodes possessing two keys, with a hash generated by a cryptographic method that interconnects all blocks on the blockchain (e.g., SHA256). It guarantees that the block is anonymous, immutable, and concise. This employs a private key to authenticate a transaction digitally and guarantees its legitimacy. The first stipulation is that the transaction can solely be authenticated with a certain key, while the second condition is that an error during the transfer inhibits decoding.

![Figure 1.1 Distributed ledger technology. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig01_01.jpg)

Figure 1.1 Distributed ledger technology. [Return to text.⏎](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_1b)

The consensus process regulates the data incorporated in the block and the selection of miners. A peer-to-peer system is established, wherein each node is designated 5as a peer upon initiating communication and interaction with other nodes in the network; thus, it is referred to as a peer node. A full node in the network refers to any device that possesses the whole blockchain host state and is accountable for operating the entire blockchain system. When a client intends to utilise the blockchain, it connects to the network through a node. Blockchain technology in healthcare has numerous critical components that collectively facilitate its uses in securing, managing, and exchanging healthcare data.

### 1.2.2 Essential Components of Blockchain for Healthcare

The fundamental elements of blockchain in medical applications highlight key components and their specific functionalities. The decentralised ledger is the core of blockchain, storing transactions across a network securely and transparently. In healthcare, it encrypts and preserves patient records, treatment histories, and other sensitive data, with each transaction being timestamped, cryptographically linked, and distributed throughout the network. Cryptographic hashes transform data into unique, fixed-size strings, helping to identify and maintain data integrity within the blockchain. Any data alteration would result in a new hash, alerting the network to potential tampering. Smart contracts are self-executing agreements with predefined 6rules, useful for managing patient consent, insurance processing, and regulatory compliance in healthcare.

They facilitate transparency and enable trustless transactions without intermediaries. Consensus mechanisms, such as Proof of Work (PoW), Proof of Stake (PoS), and Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT), ensure that nodes across the blockchain network agree on transaction validity, enhancing the reliability and accuracy of medical records. Encryption transforms plain text into ciphertext, safeguarding healthcare data by allowing only authorised parties to access and decrypt sensitive information. The immutable audit trail ensures that it cannot be altered once data is verified. It creates an auditable history of medical transactions that strengthens data integrity, reduces fraud, and supports regulatory compliance. Permissioned access allows controlled entry to blockchain networks, ensuring that sensitive medical information remains secure. Patients can manage who has access to their data, aligning with privacy regulations such as Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA). Interoperability standards, such as HL7 and FHIR, define protocols for seamless data exchange, promoting blockchain integration with existing healthcare IT systems for improved data accessibility and usability. Addressing scalability and performance is vital, enabling blockchain solutions to handle large-scale data transactions without sacrificing efficiency or security.

Lastly, regulatory compliance ensures that blockchain applications in healthcare adhere to data protection regulations, preserve patient rights and privacy, and meet legal obligations for healthcare providers. Together, these components provide a secure, transparent, and efficient framework for blockchain applications in the medical field. [Figure 1.2](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_2) delineates the fundamental functions of a blockchain node. These essential functions ensure that blockchain networks are decentralised, secure, and reliable. The blockchain node, characterised by decentralisation, enhanced security, and transparency, is revolutionising finance, supply chain management, and 7healthcare sectors. Its ability to eradicate intermediaries, ensure data integrity, and provide tamper-proof records drives innovation across all sectors, offering more efficient, secure, and transparent solutions to traditional challenges. This transformative impact has established blockchain as a cornerstone for future developments across multiple domains \[[13](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_13),[14](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_14)\].

![Figure 1.2 Types of Block chain nodes. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig01_02.jpg)

Figure 1.2 Types of Block chain nodes. [Return to text.⏎](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_2b)

### 1.2.3 Blockchain Classification

Blockchain is categorised into four main types: public, private, hybrid, and consortium. A public blockchain enables all participants to access comprehensive information and engage in the consensus mechanism. A private blockchain is confined to a designated group. In a private blockchain, all nodes within a specific group are authorised to engage in the consensus process \[[15](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_15)\]. A hybrid blockchain environment amalgamates the advantages of both public and private blockchain networks. Consortium blockchains are managed by a collective of organisations rather than an individual entity. They provide shared control yet remain permissioned, indicating that only designated individuals can access the blockchain. This illustrates how the public blockchain integrates the security and confidentiality of private blockchains with the transparency of blockchain networks \[[16](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_16),[17](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_17)\]. In blockchain applications, we must resolve two challenges: double spending and the Byzantine Generals Problem. The double spending issue arises when a single currency is used in two concurrent transactions. Blockchain addresses this issue by enabling numerous decentralised nodes to authenticate transactions. This necessitates the development of a corresponding consensus algorithm.

### 1.2.4 Consensus Algorithms

*   **Proof-of-Work:** It is mostly linked to blockchain technology used in Bitcoin. Miners vie to address a computationally demanding task while employing the Proof-of-Work (PoW) protocol. The miner that assesses this hash value is compensated for initially validating the transactions or other elements within the block. The PoW protocol necessitates substantial energy consumption when implemented on a large blockchain.
*   **Proof-of-Stake:** The objective of developing Proof-of-Stake (PoS) is to address the issue of energy inefficiency. The fundamental premise is that nodes possessing a larger stake will exhibit an increased likelihood of contributing blocks to the network. A new block signer is selected randomly, with the selection procedure determined by the miner’s stake amount. As a result, the energy required to mine a block will be markedly reduced relative to the exhaustive computation in PoW.
*   **Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance:** Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT) was the inaugural proposed consensus mechanism for addressing Byzantine fault tolerance, achievable even in a hostile node within the system. Executing non-deterministic chain codes can be quite effective. Stellar, Hyperledger, and Ripple exemplify PBFT.
*   8**Delegated Proof of Stake and Proof of Stake:** Delegated Proof of Stake (DPoS) and PoS differ fundamentally in that PoS operates as a representative democratic system, whereas DPoS functions as a direct democratic system. Users construct and validate blocks. The reduced number of nodes for block validation enhances the probability of rapid verification and accelerates transaction confirmation.
*   **Proof of Authority:** Proof of Authority (PoA) integrates elements of PoW and PoS. It honours a stakeholder’s reputation and identity. A shareholder is thereby bolstered by their reputation rather than their equity stake. Consequently, the foundational elements of the blockchain are safeguarded by authentic and reliable persons.
*   **Proof of Vote algorithm:** The Proof of Vote (PoV) algorithm is unique compared with all other consensus mechanisms. To produce transaction blocks on the blockchain, a consortium of firms must participate in exchanging business data. As a result, they employ external personnel for assistance. The team will allocate the block to each firm inside the network to validate votes, ensuring the blockchain’s decentralised nature. Business owners sometimes expand the workforce by employing individuals. This method was explicitly designed for a consortium.

### 1.2.5 Application of Blockchain Technology in Healthcare

Examples of blockchain applications in the healthcare sector include medical management, medical development and research genetics, and e-healthcare. Blockchain offers a secure and dependable mechanism for information storage across all healthcare industry sectors, enabling physicians and healthcare providers to use recorded data for various transactions and analyses \[[18](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_18),[19](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_19)\]. In this section, we will examine alternative ways for implementing blockchain across different healthcare sub-domains, as seen in [Figure 1.3](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_3). Blockchain technology transforms healthcare by addressing numerous challenges, including data security, cooperation, and efficiency. These are some of the key areas where blockchain is making a significant impact on the healthcare sector.

![Figure 1.3 Blockchain technology implementation. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig01_03.jpg)

Figure 1.3 Blockchain technology implementation. [Return to text.⏎](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_3b)

*   **Medical Records Management:** It is challenged by the fragmented nature of healthcare data, which often spreads across multiple providers. This disjointed setup limits access, complicates data sharing, and heightens security risks. Blockchain addresses these issues by creating a secure, decentralised ledger where patient records are cryptographically stored, making them accessible only through authorised channels. This setup enhances security, supports interoperability, and gives patients greater control over their health information. The past decade has seen significant growth in the digitalisation of health data, with healthcare professionals increasingly requiring immediate access to patient information to make timely decisions. Electronic health records (EHRs), digital versions of medical records, facilitate this need by allowing healthcare providers to access a patient’s medical history, diagnoses, and treatment plans at any time, given proper authorisation. Blockchain’s primary applications in EHRs include secure 9storage of prescription information, treatment protocols, laboratory results, test outcomes, and similar critical health data, thereby supporting a more integrated and secure healthcare data management system \[[20](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_20)\].
*   **Supply Chain Management:** This requires strict maintenance of the authenticity and integrity of medical equipment throughout the supply chain to ensure patient safety and compliance with regulatory standards. Blockchain enables transparent oversight across the supply chain, providing traceability from producer to consumer. Through smart contracts, verification procedures can be automated, ensuring that items meet quality standards at each stage and reducing the risk of counterfeit pharmaceuticals.
*   **Clinical Trials:** It relies on the integrity of trial data, transparency, and the management of participant consent to ensure research validity and adherence to ethical standards. Blockchain can streamline the administration of consent forms, securely archive trial data, and provide verifiable records of trial outcomes. This enhances transparency, reduces risks of data tampering, and expedites regulatory approvals.
*   10**Healthcare Data Sharing:** It involves exchanging sensitive patient information among healthcare providers, researchers, and insurers, often complicated by privacy concerns and diverse IT systems. Blockchain allows patients to control access to medical information through encryption keys, enabling secure, patient-directed data sharing. By securely transmitting encrypted data, blockchain facilitates information flow for research and treatment while ensuring patient confidentiality.
*   **Billing Information and Medical Claim Processing:** Healthcare often involves multiple intermediaries, leading to inefficiencies, delays, and potential inaccuracies. Blockchain can streamline billing processes through smart contracts, reducing administrative costs, minimising fraud, and improving payment accuracy.
*   **Identity Management and Authentication:** Healthcare presents challenges in managing patient identities across different providers and systems, often resulting in inaccuracies and opportunities for fraudulent activities. Blockchain-based digital IDs provide a unified and secure approach to verifying patient identities, significantly enhancing treatment accuracy, billing, and patient engagement.
*   **Research and Intellectual Property Protection:** It is critical for ensuring innovation in healthcare. Safeguarding intellectual property and maintaining research integrity are essential components of this process. Blockchain enables the timestamping and authentication of research data and intellectual property rights, limiting unauthorised use. This technology fosters collaboration among researchers while protecting their rights and contributions.
*   **Internet of Things (IoT) Integration:** Healthcare involves securely transmitting real-time data to healthcare systems, which presents data integrity and confidentiality challenges. Blockchain provides a decentralised and secure platform for IoT devices to transmit this real-time data, ensuring its authenticity. In addition, it facilitates automated actions based on smart contracts, enhancing the efficiency and reliability of healthcare processes.
*   Various blockchain innovations in medical management highlight their applications, descriptions, and associated tools. In EHRs, blockchain creates a decentralised and immutable system that ensures patient data remain secure and tamper-proof using tools like MedRec, Patientory, and Guardtime Health. Blockchain enhances trial data transparency for clinical trials and research utilising platforms such as IBM Blockchain, FarmaTrust, and Embleema. In drug supply chain and management, blockchain technology tracks pharmaceuticals throughout the supply chain, effectively preventing counterfeit drugs, with tools like MediLedger, Blockpharma, and Chronicled. Regarding medical billing and claims, the blockchain validates the billing process, reducing fraud and errors; notable tools include PokitDok, Hashed Health, and SolveCare. Blockchain also plays a crucial role in patient consent management by securely recording and easily verifying patient consent for data sharing through tools like Consentium, Health Wizz, and Coral Health \[[21](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_21)\].

11Furthermore, it facilitates interoperability by enabling data sharing across different healthcare systems while maintaining data integrity using technologies such as Hyperledger, Ethereum, and IBM Blockchain. For wearables and IoT devices, blockchain secures data from wearable health devices, ensuring privacy and accuracy with tools like Factom, Medicalchain, and Chronicled. In the insurance sector, blockchain enhances the transparency of claims processing, exemplified by Aetna’s blockchain initiative and MetLife’s LumenLab. In addition, blockchain provides highly secured digital identities for patients and medical experts through platforms like SelfKey, Civic, and ShoCard. Finally, blockchain improves public health data collection and reporting accuracy and speed in public health surveillance utilising tools such as Hacera, IBM Blockchain, and SAP Blockchain.

## 1.3 Challenges in Blockchain Adoption

Blockchain technology faces challenges due to inherent defects and many architectural issues. As developed by various academics, blockchain technology has considerable technological problems and constraints, as illustrated in [Figure 1.4](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_4).

![Figure 1.4 Challenges in blockchain technology. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig01_04.jpg)

Figure 1.4 Challenges in blockchain technology. [Return to text.⏎](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_4b)

*   **The Problem of Throughput:** Its maximum transaction rate does not exceed five. Consequently, if conventional e-commerce companies implement blockchain for transactions, it is likely to fail due to transaction delays and escalating network communication expenses.
*   **Latency Issue:** Elevated latency constitutes a significant issue inside the Bitcoin network. Procedures require approximately 10 minutes to complete, with additional time needed to avert duplicate spending. For the dual assault to succeed, certain criteria must be met.
*   12**Limitation of Size and Bandwidth:** Blockchain scalability is essential for competing with conventional e-commerce platforms like Visa. The Bitcoin network can process approximately 500 transactions per second, facilitating scalability, as illustrated in [Figure 1.5](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_5).
    
    ![Figure 1.5 Scalability issue in blockchain technology. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig01_05.jpg)
    
    Figure 1.5 Scalability issue in blockchain technology. [Return to text.⏎](#C_004_c1.xhtml#f1_5b)
    
*   **Block Size:** Every node in a decentralised system processes each transaction, constituting a comprehensive replica of the ledger state. In the patient care system, only the essential ledgers for specific nodes are required, rather than the complete ledger.
*   **High Volume:** A million additional records may be replicated across all participants when this platform is employed to maintain patient profiles. Consequently, there would be an excess of information. Significantly, the creation of scaled-linked providers faces the difficulty of excessive data volume in healthcare facilities.
*   **Transactions:** Each transaction on the Ethereum blockchain requires verification by all validators inside the network. The data load adversely affects the network’s speed. Consequently, achieving consensus or authenticating digital identification requires time.
*   **Number of Nodes:** The complexity of operating blockchain-based applications escalated with a substantial growth in users or patients. An increase in the overall computational requirements of the blockchain system accompanies this.
*   **Protocol:** Blockchain technology cannot address certain patient issues, such as unforeseen access or emergencies. Moreover, access to personal information by a physician was first prohibited during the emergency.
*   **Resource Wastage:** The consensus process is essential to the Bitcoin system, providing security against adversaries, but it also results in significant wastage of computational resources, manifesting as elevated hardware costs and energy consumption.
*   13**Usability Problem:** Most blockchain research emphasises technical elements such as security, secrecy, data integrity, efficiency, and scalability, sometimes neglecting usability \[[22](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_22),[23](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_23)\].
*   **Fork Problem:** A division occurs in the blockchain when a modification is required. Forks are categorised into two types: soft forks and hard forks. Blockchain operates through participants, nodes, and miners. Nodes must accept all modifications. In addition, updated nodes persist in validating blocks.

## 1.4 Implementation of Blockchain in Medical Care

Implementing blockchain in medical care presents a transformative opportunity for healthcare institutions to enhance data security and transparency, ultimately improving patient care. The workflow commences with identifying specific use cases where blockchain can add value, such as medical records management, clinical trials, supply chain integrity, or insurance claims processing. After ascertaining these applications, the next step involves designing a blockchain network tailored to the organisation’s needs. This includes selecting from private, public, or hybrid blockchain options based on data sensitivity and regulatory obligations and elucidating the consensus mechanisms and governance frameworks that will be employed. Once the network design is complete, healthcare data is gathered from diverse sources, including hospitals, clinics, and laboratories. Critical information is then encrypted using sophisticated cryptographic methods to ensure confidentiality.

Smart contracts are created to automate patient consent management, insurance verification, and medicine traceability. These smart contracts implement predetermined regulations and autonomously conduct transactions. As transactions occur, encrypted healthcare data and transaction records are preserved as immutable blocks on the blockchain. Each transaction, whether a patient visit or laboratory test result, is timestamped and linked to the preceding block, establishing a visible and verifiable information chain. Cryptographic key techniques maintain access control and privacy, ensuring that only authorised entities, including patients, healthcare providers, and researchers, can access designated data. This approach safeguards patient confidentiality and adheres to data protection laws such as General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) and HIPAA.

Furthermore, interoperability is crucial for effective blockchain integration in healthcare; thus, standards and protocols must be developed to facilitate seamless data sharing among various healthcare systems and blockchain networks. Utilising Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) and data standards, such as HL7 and FHIR, enables smooth data interchange. Routine audits are essential to ensure data integrity and compliance with regulatory standards; the transparent and auditable nature of blockchain simplifies the auditing process and enhances regulatory compliance. Integrating blockchain with IoT devices and artificial intelligence algorithms further enhances the healthcare ecosystem by improving real-time data collection, analysis, and decision-making \[[24](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_24)\].

Continuous improvement is also vital; evaluating performance indicators like transaction velocity, scalability, and cost-efficiency allows for ongoing enhancements 14to the blockchain network and its protocols, thereby increasing efficiency and user experience \[[25](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_25)\]. Education and training for healthcare professionals, administrators, and patients regarding blockchain technology, its benefits, and the secure utilisation of blockchain-enabled applications are equally important \[[26](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_26),[27](#C_004_c1.xhtml#r1_27)\]. Finally, fostering community engagement through collaboration among stakeholders, including healthcare providers, technology vendors, and regulators, can help address challenges and improve the implementation of blockchain technology in the healthcare sector.

## 1.5 Conclusions and Future Scope

This chapter delineates the current state of blockchain in healthcare administration, emphasising its prospective advantages and uses. Blockchain offers the potential to create safe, decentralised systems that emphasise patient-centred care through improved data processing and management capabilities. The suggested applications of blockchain in health data management encompass enhancing privacy, security, accessibility, and comprehensive control over EHRs. Various prototypes and concepts have been investigated, including payment systems, permissioned blockchains, and offline storage. Nevertheless, additional research is required to comprehensively comprehend and assess the efficacy of blockchain in healthcare. Furthermore, current projects must tackle challenges associated with their implementation, encompassing dependability, latency, and privacy difficulties. This sophisticated technique should be employed wisely, contingent upon its relevance and requirement for particular clinical situations.

# 2 Towards a Unified Framework for Blockchain-IoT Integration Architectural Insights and Research Challenges

Reetu Singh, Greetta Pinheiro, Niharika Keshari, Arvind Mewada, and Mohd. Aquib Ansari

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-3](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-3)

## 2.1 Introduction

The Internet of Things (IoT) represents a new configuration that alters the way people interact with their environment. It is an incredibly complex network of internet- connected devices, ranging from simple appliances used in the home to highly complex industrial equipment. IoT devices collect data, exchange information, and utilise software, sensors, and connectivity to automate processes and enable real-time decisions to be made to improve efficiency and convenience in work and daily life \[[1](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_1)\]. IoT is creating intelligent environments. The smart thermostat can learn your preferences, and heavy industrial equipment is capable of sensing when it is about to break down. Every device in the network generates a huge amount of data, so we need systems that can store them efficiently for processing and analysing without sacrificing performance. In addition, as the data increases, so do concerns about security, immutability, privacy, and the need for reliable, scalable infrastructure. Blockchain technology with core attributes—security, immutability, and transparency—emerged as a feasible solution to the significant challenges of IoT \[[2](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_2)\]. First designed as the backbone of cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin, blockchain is a decentralised \[[3](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_3)\] ledger system that securely records and stores data over a distributed network. Blockchain works by recording the information and the hash of the previous information into encrypted “blocks,” each linked together to form an unalterable and secure chain. Any change to the blockchain requires consensus from the entire network, thus affording strong protection against forgery or unauthorised changes. This decentralised architecture lowers the risk of reliance on some central authority for transfers of data, thereby minimising the risk of a single point of failure, which is particularly beneficial for IoT networks where many devices exchange sensitive information \[[4](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_4)\].

18Integrating blockchain and IoT provides a revolutionary solution to some of the most significant hurdles against security and scalability. Each transaction in blockchain-based IoT networks is encrypted and validated by a distributed network, thus making the system highly secure against hacking or data corruption \[[4](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_4)\]. For this reason, blockchain-based IoT allows the independent and secure communication of devices because data can be shared with minimal centralised control \[[5](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_5)\]. This makes IoT networks more efficient and also resilient to tampering and unauthorised access \[[4](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_4)\]. Apart from security, blockchain addresses the scalability challenges IoT faces as it expands. The levels of data generated by IoT present a challenge to efficiently managing traditional centralised systems. This results in inefficiency and delay in such systems.

With a decentralised architecture, blockchain ensures that the workload is distributed across many nodes, thus making the system scalable. As IoT networks expand, blockchain offers them high performance and reliability without the data processing load that might be congested with it. Smart contracts present much more than increased security and scalability: they tremendously boost the potential offered by IoT in blockchain frameworks. As conditions are integrated directly into the blockchain, smart contracts automatically execute conditions while allowing IoT devices to automate transactions and processes without any human intervention. The smart contracts in a smart city, for example, control how the autonomous vehicles, traffic sensors, and traffic lights interact. They optimise the flow of traffic and reduce congestion in cities. They also automate the payment disbursement or inventory handling on some predefined criteria.

This streamlines operations. Hence, blockchain in supply chain management creates a transparent and secure system for tracing products from their source to the end customer, thereby reducing fraud and enhancing traceability and accountability. Monitoring of patient health can be done by IoT devices while ensuring that blocks within this system secure the integrity and privacy of the patient’s data, and access will only be granted to parties authorised to view it \[[6](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_6)\]. The blockchain-based innovation of IoT shapes future interaction between devices and systems, propelling sectors towards innovation, automation, and efficiency using trustworthy solutions for industries and consumers alike.

This chapter dives into IoT and blockchain architectures, their benefits when combined, as well as challenges involved. A rigorous literature review of existing research work will establish key points of gaps and suggest future directions to improve blockchain-based IoT. The chapter is organised as follows: In [Section 2.2](#C_005_c2.xhtml#h2_2), the basics of IoT and blockchain are dealt with, and then the entire architecture of IoT-blockchain with its applications and challenges are addressed in [Section 2.3](#C_005_c2.xhtml#h2_3). In [Sections 2.4](#C_005_c2.xhtml#h2_4)–[2.6](#C_005_c2.xhtml#h2_6), related research is reviewed, gaps are pointed out, future research directions are proposed, respectively, and a conclusion is included in [Section 2.7](#C_005_c2.xhtml#h2_7).

## 2.2 Preliminaries

### 2.2.1 Internet of Things (IoT)

It is described as the interconnection of numerous networks of devices with sensors, software, and internet tools, enabling those appliances to gather, share, and process information. The origin of IoT is in extending the internet beyond its traditional 19computing devices to a wide range of objects that make more interactions and automation in our daily lives. The IoT system consists of several components, such as sensors and actuators, communication protocols, data processing systems, and applications that allow users to access and remotely control these devices \[[7](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_7)\]. This network allows improvements in efficiency and innovation in many sectors. There are other industries, such as smart homes, health, manufacturing, and transport, which shall benefit from real-time monitoring and the control of processes by the IoT. Typically, three primary layers constitute the architecture of IoT:

1.  **Perception Layer:** Within that layer, IoT devices, such as smart appliances, cameras, and a wide variety of sensors, capture data about the physical environment. Its primary purpose is to sense, monitor, and obtain information from the environment.
2.  **Network Layer:** The network layer uses several protocols, such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, and cellular networks, to offer communication between devices and data processing systems that assure the secure transmission of information to allow collected information to be relayed efficiently to the proper applications or storage systems.
3.  **Application Layer:** This layer includes the software applications that analyse collected data so that meaningful insights or tools to control can be presented to the users. It supports automation and enables better decision-making.

### 2.2.2 Blockchain

Blockchain technology is a decentralised and distributed digital ledger, allowing the secure capture of transactions across several computers and providing assurance of data security, immutability, and transparency. While it was first designed for cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin, the primary idea of blockchain lies in its capacity to generate a secure, tamper-resistant record of transactions without needing a central authority \[[8](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_8),[9](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_9)\]. Transactions are grouped into blocks subsequently connected to previous blocks, resulting in a secure chain, as illustrated in [Figure 2.1](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_1). The transactions are hashed and combined in pairs to form a tree-like structure where each 20node represents the hash of its child nodes, as shown in [Figure 2.2](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_2). The block header includes the root hash, ensuring transaction integrity and security. This architecture promotes trust among participants, as any alteration in the data would require consensus from the entire network.

![Figure 2.1 Blockchain architecture: Each block contains a header with a previous block hash, nonce, timestamp, and Merkle root, forming a chain of blocks. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig02_01.jpg)

Figure 2.1 Blockchain architecture: Each block contains a header with a previous block hash, nonce, timestamp, and Merkle root, forming a chain of blocks. [Return to text.⏎](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_1b)

![Figure 2.2 Merkle tree and hashing in blockchain. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig02_02.jpg)

Figure 2.2 Merkle tree and hashing in blockchain. [Return to text.⏎](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_2b)

Blockchain architecture is defined by its decentralised and distributed characteristics, consisting of several key components:

1.  **Blocks:** Every block holds the transaction data, timestamp, and cryptographic hash of the previous block, effectively linking them into a secure chain (as shown in [Figure 2.1](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_1)). In addition, blocks utilise Merkle Trees for efficient and secure verification of transactions, ensuring data integrity (depicted in [Figure 2.2](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_2)). This structure ensures the integrity and immutability of the recorded data.
2.  **Nodes:** In a blockchain network, individual computers are called nodes; each holding a copy of the blockchain is involved in validating transactions. This decentralised model prevents any single entity from controlling the entire system, thereby improving the security and transparency of the system (illustrated in [Figure 2.3](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_3)). Each node verifies transactions and maintains the state of the Blockchain, contributing to the resilience and reliability of the network.
    
    ![Figure 2.3 Blockchain transaction flow: (1) Initiating a transaction, (2) Transaction broadcasting in the P2P network, (3) Transaction validation, and (4) Formation of a new block. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig02_03.jpg)
    
    Figure 2.3 Blockchain transaction flow: (1) Initiating a transaction, (2) Transaction broadcasting in the P2P network, (3) Transaction validation, and (4) Formation of a new block. [Return to text.⏎](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_3b)
    
3.  **Consensus Mechanisms:** Protocols are used to ensure agreement on transaction validity. Popular methods include Proof of Stake (POS) and Proof of Work (PoW), which help maintain data integrity, eliminating the need for a central authority. The consensus process and the formation of new blocks are depicted in [Figure 2.3](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_3).

## 2.3 IoT Using Blockchain: Architecture, Advantages, Applications, Challenges

### 2.3.1 IoT Using Blockchain: Architecture

Integrating IoT and blockchain requires a multi-layered architecture to ensure seamless operation, communication, security, and data integrity across devices, networks, and services. The following section describes the layered architecture of IoT systems 21using blockchain based on [Figures 2.3](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_3) and [2.4](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_4) illustrates how blocks are linked and how transactions are validated and added to the blockchain.

![Figure 2.4 Layered architecture of IoT using blockchain. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig02_04.jpg)

Figure 2.4 Layered architecture of IoT using blockchain. [Return to text.⏎](#C_005_c2.xhtml#f2_4b)

1.  **Perception Layer:** The perception layer, also known as the device layer, consists of the physical devices that collect data from the environment. These devices are embedded with sensors, actuators, and IoT technologies that capture real-time information.
    
    *   **Components:** Cameras, Smartphones, Humidity Sensors, Vibration Sensors, Laptops, PCs, Vehicles.
    *   **Function:** This layer acts as the interface that translates the real world into the digital system. This layer gleans environmental data, such as humidity, temperature, or video feeds and motion, and sends it to the communication layer.
2.  22**Communication Layer:** The communication layer is used for data transfer from IoT devices to the perception layer of the blockchain network. The sensor-to-the-gateway and gateway-to-the-processing unit communication is absolutely reliable and secure.
    
    *   **Components:** Micro Base Stations, Gateways, Small Base Stations, and Access Points.
    *   **Function:** The layer discusses the data transference protocols, which further states that in order to transfer information from the devices to the blockchain system, communication has to be wireless. It is a case of having network components involved, namely, base stations, gateways, and access points, which will facilitate electronic data communication by the electronic devices to the central servers or cloud platforms.
3.  **Blockchain Composition Layer:** Block chain layer for composition concerning blockchain is important for safe, immutable, and distributed data management. It includes processing and storing IoT data by using a decentralised ledger.
    
    *   **Components:** Blocks, Smart Contracts, Docker Containers, P2P (Peer-to- Peer) Networks.
    *   **Function:** This layer marks the area of application of blockchain’s decentralised ledger, smart contracts, and peer-to-peer networks. The blockchain composition layer ensures the protection of IoT data through encryption and decentralised storage across nodes, as well as automating transactions or data exchange through smart contracts and containerised applications, such as Docker, for efficiency and scalability.
4.  **Application Layer:** This layer elaborates on the specific use cases and services for which the IoT-blockchain system is being developed. It links an end user to various applications spread across different industries.
    
    *   **Components:** Internet of Vehicles (IoV), Supply Chain Management, Healthcare, Bitcoin Integration.
    *   **Function:** Blockchain-based IoT solutions specifically meet the needs of varied industries, such as traceability in supply chains, health monitoring, vehicle-to-vehicle communication, and cryptocurrency transactions.
5.  **Service Sublayer (from the right-hand portion of the diagram):** This service layer provides Blockchain-as-a-Service, which allows for the execution of smart contracts and chain code. It provides the basic services that are required to design and deploy blockchain applications.
    
    *   **Components:** Smart Contracts, BaaS, Chain Code.
    *   **Function:** It ensures the seamless working of the decentralised application with the BaaS frameworks given to the IoT developers who help develop blockchain technologies.
6.  **Incentive Sublayer:** The incentive sublayer provides an incentive sublayer, ensuring economical operations inside the IoT-blockchain system.
    
    *   **Components:** Distribution Mechanism Transaction Cost Reward Mechanism.
    *   **Function:** It deals with the incentive mechanisms by distributing rewards to the participants or nodes inside the blockchain network so that they can use their computing power to validate transactions.
7.  23**Consensus Sublayer:** This sublayer is crucial for upholding the integrity of the blockchain network through consensus algorithms.
    
    *   **Components**: Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance, PoS, PoW.
    *   **Function**: A consensus sublayer verifies that all parties agree about the legitimacy of transactions added to the blockchain. There are several consensus algorithms, including PoW, PoS, and pBFT, which serve to secure the validation of transactions and prevent fraudulent acts.
8.  **Network Sublayer:** A network sublayer supports secure routing and propagation of data across the Blockchain.
    
    *   **Components**: Verification Mechanisms, Overlay Routing, Propagation Protocols.
    *   **Function**: This layer emphasises secure forwarding and validation of data packets throughout the network. Overlay routing techniques allow effective movements of blockchain data through the IoT network. The verification of true data is guaranteed as the overlay ensures validation and back-checks of data while communicating between sources and destinations.
9.  **Data Sublayer:** This sublayer of the blockchain forms the data structure and encryption in the blockchain.
    
    *   **Components:** Blocks, Chain, Merkle Tree, Hash Functions, Cryptographic Algorithms,
    *   **Function:** This sublayer organises data into blocks, connects them, and locks the blocks using cryptographic methods by Merkle trees and hash functions. The result is that the data coming through the IoT is tamper-proof, verifiable, and will be secured on the distributed ledger.

### 2.3.2 Advantages of IoT Using Blockchain

The combination of IoT and blockchain combines the strengths of both to solve crucial challenges that exist in IoT systems, from security and scalability to trust. Here are the main benefits that come from combining IoT with blockchain.

#### 2.3.2.1 Enhanced Security

Blockchain provides a decentralised and tamper-proof framework for the securement of IoT data \[[10](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_10)\]. The critical dangers associated with IoT, as these devices generate and share huge amounts of sensitive information, are susceptibility to cyberattacks. Blockchain has mitigated the risks through data integrity, which guarantees that whenever the data from the IoT devices are written to the ledger, it cannot be altered. All the attempts at changing the data shall be rejected by the network, leaving that authenticity intact. Decentralised validation rather than only relying on a central authority to verify and record transactions, blockchain utilises a series of nodes within a network. This alone is more resistant to attacks such as a Distributed Denial of Service attack on IoT networks.

#### 2.3.2.2 Trustless Environment

In the traditional setups of IoT, data validation is carried out most of the time by a trusted central authority or third party. This, however, opens up vulnerabilities and complicates configurations. The application of blockchain in IoT systems allows the 24possibility of establishing a trustless environment where, theoretically, devices can self-governally interact and share data without intermediaries.

1.  **Decentralised Trust:** The blockchain consensus mechanism allows IoT devices to validate and confirm transactions and data independently. Therefore, a trusted intermediary is no longer required, and this enhances efficiency and security.
2.  **Machine-to-Machine (M2M) Trust:** Blockchain provides for secure and trustless communication between IoT devices so that M2M transactions can be effected safely, without any human intervention.

#### 2.3.2.3 Improved Transparency and Traceability

The most important benefit of blockchain is its transparent and verifiable record of all interactions and transactions in an IoT network, which is especially useful in supply chain management, healthcare, and agriculture.

1.  **Traceability:** The blockchain supply chain management system allows the stakeholders to trace the origin and journey and the current status of every product at each point. The entire transparency blockchain ensures that all stakeholders have the same information and fraud cannot occur.
2.  **Auditability:** All the actions from an IoT device are straightforwardly registered on blockchain and can be audited. This allows users to track everything and adhere to the regulations or standards in real-time.

#### 2.3.2.4 Automation Through Smart Contracts

Smart contracts are self-executing agreements that activate when predefined conditions written into their code are satisfied. Smart contracts further simplify IoT interactions amongst machines, ensuring transactions are possible without any human interface.

1.  **Autonomous Operations:** Through real-time information, IoT devices can automatically initiate actions. For instance, a smart contract may be activated to automatically turn on irrigation in agriculture if the moisture level falls below specified parameters in the soil.
2.  **Efficient Processes:** Smarter contracts are those that make payments, update records of inventory, or send orders automatically to be executed when pre-set conditions such as delivery confirmation are satisfied, thus reducing the time spent doing these things manually and hence making supply chains more efficient.
3.  **Cost Saving:** Smart contracts automate the transaction and leave no need for an intermediary or oversight in an endeavour. Thus, it saves costs and allows transactions to be performed quickly.

#### 2.3.2.5 Scalability and Resource Optimisation

Spreading out the workload to multiple nodes could be another way in which the decentralised architecture of blockchain would help improve the scalability of IoT systems because it would not bottle up with data.

1.  25**Efficient Resource Usage:** IoT systems create massive data sets, which must be processed and validated. Blockchain allows the network to distribute the task among various nodes so that no single point of the network is burdened by the same.
2.  **Scalable Networks:** With an increase in the number of IoT devices, blockchain will be able to handle more transactions without such bottlenecks as in centralised systems that collapse when given too much information.

### 2.3.3 Applications of IoT Using Blockchain

Combining IoT and blockchain establishes a robust technological framework to revolutionise multiple industries. Blockchain helps address key challenges in IoT, such as transparency, security, scalability, and data integrity, making it ideal for numerous applications. Below are some significant applications of IoT using blockchain across different sectors:

#### 2.3.3.1 Supply Chain Management

In supply chain management, IoT devices like sensors, Radio-Frequency Identification (RFID) tags, and GPS technologies are used to track goods and monitor conditions (temperature, humidity, etc.) throughout the complete life cycle of a product \[[11](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_11)\]. Incorporating blockchain into this process improves traceability and transparency.

1.  **Real-Time Tracking:** IoT devices monitor the location and condition of goods in real-time, while blockchain provides a secure, tamper-proof record of these transactions.
2.  **Tamper-Proof Records:** The use of blockchain ensures that each stage of the supply chain is documented on a secure, decentralised ledger. This allows stakeholders to verify and evaluate the condition of goods at every point, from their origin to their destination.
3.  **Fraud Reduction:** The use of transparency and immutability by blockchain will reduce the risk associated with fraud, enhancing authenticity and trust in the supply chain.

#### 2.3.3.2 Smart Cities

This makes it use IoT technology to control and maximise the efficiency of cities’ systems, from the level of energy usage and its flow up to the availability of water and public safety \[[12](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_12)\]. This makes the idea of using blockchain to improve the security and efficiency of following IoT applications.

1.  **Traffic Management:** The IoT sensors can track traffic conditions in real-time. The smart contracts on blockchain can alter the working of the traffic lights or usage of roads based on live data fed into it. Blockchain guarantees the genuineness of this data and automates actions.
2.  **Energy Management:** Blockchain enables decentralised peer-to-peer energy trading, in which smart contracts allow residents to either sell or acquire surplus energy coming from photovoltaic solar panels or other 26renewable energy sources. The byproduct is the powerful establishment of an efficient energy market within smart cities and limiting dependence on centralised utilities.
3.  **Public Safety:** The IoT-based surveillance and sensors track environmental conditions. Blockchain may record and manage these data to make sure public safety decisions are not based on erroneous or false information.

#### 2.3.3.3 Healthcare

IoT devices, such as smart medical equipment and surveillance systems at a distance, collect and transmit patient data in the health sector. Blockchain can then ensure that such sensitive information is secure, private, and accurate \[[13](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_13)\].

1.  **Medical Data Security:** Blockchain technology ensures the secure transferability of medical records among service providers, patients, and insurers. With IoT devices generating real-time health information on an ongoing basis, blockchain stores it safely and cannot be modified.
2.  **Remote Patient Monitoring:** Wearable IoT devices can enable remote patient monitoring by reporting patient vital signs such as heart rate, blood pressure, and glucose levels. Blockchain ensures safe data transmission; the data will only be accessible to authorised people, securing the privacy of the patient.
3.  **Drug Traceability:** Blockchain and IoT may interlink to track the medicines at each stage of the drug supply chain from manufacturing to the point of sale, thereby reducing counterfeit drugs and ensuring safe, authentic drugs to the patients.

### 2.3.4 Agriculture and Food Supply Chain

Blockchain and IoT are of considerable importance to agriculture, aiming to improve food safety while optimising the use of resources, thus reducing waste.

1.  **Precision Agriculture:** It is used to monitor soil, weather, and crop health conditions all through IoT sensors that store data safely in the blockchain. This offers better bases for farmer decisions on irrigation, planting, and harvesting that tend to increase yields and save resources.
2.  **Food Safety and Traceability:** IoT will track the product from farm to consumer. Blockchain will ensure the right information, to the consumer who is consuming it in order to verify the origin and quality of the food consumed.
3.  **Sustainability and Fair Trade:** Blockchain tracking at each step of production and distribution in an agricultural process helps to verify the practice of sustainability and fair trade in agriculture and that ethical standards are respected.

### 2.3.5 Automotive Industry and Autonomous Vehicles

Blockchain and IoT will have important effects on improving the functionality and security of connected and autonomous vehicles for higher efficiency.

1.  27**Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) Communication:** The IoT sensors enable vehicles to get in touch with other vehicles, infrastructures, and pedestrians. Blockchain ensures that these communications remain secure and unaltered. This would be a critical feature for vehicle safety and reliability in autonomous driving.
2.  **Ownership and Usage Tracking:** Blockchain can keep a record of ownership, maintenance, and usage of a vehicle in a safe manner. It is very useful for car-sharing and ride-hailing applications because user transparency is very critical in such scenarios.
3.  **Insurance and Smart Contracts:** Data on an accident can be automatically collected through IoT sensors in a vehicle, and smart contracts on the blockchain can be drafted to automate processing for an insurance claim.

## 2.4 Literature Survey

This literature survey provides an overview of key contributions, limitations, and future directions regarding integrating blockchain technology with the IoT, focusing on healthcare applications. The survey facilitates a clear comparison across the existing literature by synthesising findings from various studies. The integration of blockchain and IoT has emerged as a significant area of research, addressing various applications and challenges across multiple sectors. Article \[[8](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_8)\] examines this intersection within the healthcare sector, exploring applications such as remote patient monitoring, disease prediction, and secure medical record management. They highlight various blockchain types and associated tools that could enhance healthcare delivery. However, their review is confined to a specific timeframe (2018–2023) and focuses predominantly on healthcare, leading to a lack of practical implementations and reliance on conceptual rather than empirical solutions. They recommend developing more efficient blockchain algorithms to manage real-time healthcare data and enhance privacy. A comprehensive IoT and blockchain integration analysis using Latent Dirichlet Allocation to identify research themes across 4,455 journal articles \[[14](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_14)\]. They group those articles into 14 different research areas, but they are mainly theoretical, with very little emphasis on practical aspects. They have identified quite a number of relatively unexplored topics that may be included in future research, such as energy efficiency on wireless sensor networks and access control mechanisms in IoT systems. Article \[[15](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_15)\] analysed the IoT-blockchain architecture and challenges, focusing on several advantages, such as data integrity and security, with some potential future research challenges. Their work is mainly theoretical and not experimentally proven; the context is quite challenging in the development of robust frameworks to move forward with Blockchain Internet of Things (BIoT) security and overcome the challenge of scalability. In addition, \[[7](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_7)\] discuss various applications, opportunities, and challenges of the integration of IoT and blockchain over the diverse domains of healthcare, agriculture, and smart cities. Even though their framework is insightful, it is not yet equipped to address scalability concerns sufficiently, and only little empirical material supports their claims. They further remark that research in this area is necessary to boost the interoperability as well as security aspects in complex IoT-blockchain environments. Authors \[[16](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_16)\] take into consideration the way blockchain 28could ease IoT security issues considering the interaction of artificial intelligence (AI) with the two paradigms. They introduce the concept of blockchain as a service for IoT, but their work mainly presents theoretical models without empirical results or evaluations. Their future directions include investigating AI-based optimisation strategies for IoT and blockchain integration and further real-world deployments of blockchain as a service. Article \[[17](#C_005_c2.xhtml#r2_17)\] systematically reviews blockchain solutions for IoT, focusing on technical approaches for blockchain integration in IoT data and device management. However, much of the research remains at the conceptual stage, lacking real-world applications and facing high computational costs in large-scale IoT environments. They recommend developing scalable, lightweight blockchain frameworks to support large IoT systems better. The literature reveals a dynamic and evolving landscape for integrating blockchain and IoT. While significant theoretical contributions have been made, most studies emphasise conceptual frameworks with limited empirical validation. Future research should prioritise practical implementations, scalability solutions, and exploration of under-researched areas to harness the potential of blockchain and IoT integration fully.

## 2.5 Research Gaps in IoT Using Blockchain

### 2.5.1 Scalability Challenges

The low transaction throughput and latency in blockchain make it hard to handle the large amount of data generated by IoT devices in real-time. Scalability is very significant in IoT systems because this involves large numbers of devices and, thereby, results in an enormous quantity of data. With these scalability issues dealt with, using blockchain in large-scale IoT systems is feasible.

### 2.5.2 Storage and Data Management

In order to store large sets of data generated by IoT devices, blockchains must be developed efficiently. The requirement to carry all IoT data on-chain becomes unmanageable due to enormous storage requirements, which further causes problems such as blockchain bloat and cost augmentation. Thus, the development of efficient solutions for storing IoT data and handling it securely is of extreme importance without a significant load on blockchain systems.

### 2.5.3 Energy Consumption and Resource Constraints

However, traditional consensus mechanisms, such as PoW, require a lot of processing power and resources to work for IoT devices. Thus, the traditional blockchain consensus mechanisms are impractical for use on a massive scale in IoT networks. PoW is the most computer-intensive. Most IoT devices cannot provide such resource exhaustiveness due to their dependence on minimal power sources, like batteries. Hence, energy-friendly solutions become critical for the adoption of blockchain in IoT. It can extend blockchain adoption to even resource-constrained devices so long as the IoT devices have lightweight consensus mechanisms tailored for them, such as PoS or other low-power algorithms, improving security without losing efficiency.

29 

### 2.5.4 Latency and Real-Time Processing

The delay in blockchain technology is due to the time that is being taken for block validation and consensus. Hence, it cannot be applied directly to real-time IoT applications such as autonomous vehicles and smart healthcare. Slight delays in such an environment can lead to major issues such as accidents or delayed medical interventions; thus, latency should be minimised for the implementation of blockchain in such applications. Low-latency blockchain solutions would enable IoT systems to benefit from the security and transparency of blockchain while still fulfilling real-time requirements of critical operations.

### 2.5.5 Security and Privacy Concerns

IoT devices remain vulnerable to cyberattacks, whereas blockchain enhances the security aspect. It is certainly not the end; it leaves open Distributed Denial of Service attacks or vulnerabilities in smart contracts. IoT systems are bound to hold sensitive information often, so the right security and privacy mechanisms must be there. No matter what Blockchain promises, it cannot solely assure the absoluteness of safety for the ecosystems of IoT against emerging threats unless these gaps are filled.

## 2.6 Future Research Directions in IoT Using Blockchain

Future research into IoT using blockchain focuses on several key areas, taking into account the peculiar challenges in this convergence of two technologies. Scalable blockchain architectures, together with layer-2 solutions and sharding, are probably the most important directions; these are intended to deal with huge amounts of data and transactions generated by IoT devices. IoT systems generally require energy-efficient consensus mechanisms, such as the PoS protocols, as many of these devices are resource-constrained and carry out low-power operations. Privileged-preserving technologies, such as zero-knowledge proofs and homomorphic encryption, would ensure the confidentiality of sensitive IoT data while continuing to protect user privacy. Overarching solutions for interoperability will be identified and developed for the creation of cross-chain protocols and standardised frameworks that allow frictionless interaction across blockchain platforms. Lastly, the integration of AI in IoT automation will enhance smart contracts and predict security threats in the blockchain-enabled IoT to improve performance with a security profile. These three research directions together go harmoniously as approaches towards advancing the integration of blockchain technology in IoT.

## 2.7 Conclusion

The integration of blockchain technology into the IoT system has enormous potential to transform many sectors, overcoming most of the challenges facing IoT, which relate to security, transparency, and data integrity. This chapter has examined the architecture of IoT utilising blockchain, its advantages, and key applications for a comprehensive understanding of how both technologies can be aligned to solve key concerns in data management and system efficiency. The combination of IoT and 30blockchain is very promising. Currently, however, it is surrounded by various challenges, such as scalability, storage management, high energy consumption, and also real-time processing. This chapter, in view of an extensive review of the literature and a gap analysis of the current research, points out areas that would allow further exploration and development. Accordingly, the storage problem is one of the significant bottlenecks of IoT systems because, by nature, blockchains are not natively designed for storing large heaps of data from IoT sources. Therefore, lightweight and efficient blockchains of scalable architecture, along with lightweight consensus algorithms and storage solutions, are mandatory for future research. The integration of IoT and blockchain promises to unlock innovations that could lead towards secure, scalable, and efficient systems that may benefit industries and applications across many categories.
