### 6.1.4 Tokenisation of Assets

Tokenisation is the process of changing physical or digital assets into blockchain-based tokens. This invention allows fractional ownership, recovers liquidity, and streamlines transactions. According to recent studies, tokenisation is an indispensable element in forming the World Innovation Stock Exchange, where the revolution of tokenisation and the securitisation of project management are grave (source). This suggests that tokenisation democratises entree to investment opportunities and raises better transparency and competence in asset management. Furthermore, the request for decentralised exchanges is energetic for the seamless conversation of these digital tokens and cryptocurrencies on the blockchains. The author highlighted that mixing decentralised exchanges within the decentralised finance (DeFi) ecosystems is energetic for the efficient trading of assets, thereby improving market dynamics and liquidity \[[12](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_12)\].

### 6.1.5 Decentralised Autonomous Organisations

DAOs signify another transformative trend in blockchain finance. These organisations operate on smart contracts and are ruled by their members, eradicating the traditional hierarchical structures. According to the researcher, DAOs have the potential to disrupt traditional funding mechanisms in various industries, particularly entertainment. Through the utilisation of blockchain tokens, DAOs can enable direct funding from stakeholders, thereby offering a more democratised method of investment and project management \[[13](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_13)\].

### 6.1.6 Security Challenges and Trust in Blockchain

Even though it has benefits, blockchain technology is not deprived of challenges. He recognises several security concerns, including the vulnerabilities to cyber threats, which could deteriorate the truthfulness of the financial transactions \[[14](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_14)\]. The joint 74of blockchain with outdated financial security instruments grants an area ready for further research, particularly in developing robust cybersecurity summaries custom-made to the blockchain environments.

### 6.1.7 Blockchain's Impact on Auditing and Corporate Governance

The inferences of blockchain technology spread into auditing and corporate governance. He argues that the blockchain can alter auditing through transparency and accountability in financial reporting \[[15](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_15)\]. This transformation stances questions about the future parts of the auditors and the potential need for new skill sets that contribute to blockchain knowledge. As organisations progressively accept blockchain for financial record-keeping, additional research is desirable to understand how these changes will mark the corporate governance structures and the compliance requirements.

## 6.2 Review of Literature

Blockchain has emerged as a transformative potency in numerous industries, with finance as the lead of its adoption. Recent studies have discovered the likelihood of blockchain in finance \[[15](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_15)\]. Blockchain is a transformative technology that can improve the speed, correctness, and cost-efficiency of financial transactions, possibly leading to extensive fluctuations in how firms function and devise new ways of payment through cryptocurrencies. The financial industry has already been activated to discover and implement blockchain-based solutions, along with applications that range from cross-border payments to trade finance and asset management \[[6](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_6)\].

In addition, blockchain technology’s decentralised and safe nature opens up new possibilities for financial inclusion, particularly where traditional banking services are rare \[[16](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_16)\]. It seeks to evaluate the possibility of DeFi replacing traditional finance by using the Ethereum platform enabled by blockchain. The abolition of the intermediaries and the mechanisation of the keen contracts result in cost reductions. Market share is gained by fintech companies working with blockchain startups. Limitations include industry-specific differences and long-term data availability. Implications call for more investigation into long-term effects, research particular to subsectors, and comparative evaluations. Fintech companies are urged to deliberately implement blockchain with a focus on risk management and compliance \[[5](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_5)\].

### 6.2.1 Transparency, Privacy, and Security

The decentralised ledger of blockchain guarantees transparency by authenticating the transactions through computers, which allows all participants to view them in real-time. Every transaction is tightly encrypted and cannot be improved in the past without the participants’ agreement. This transparency nurtures trust among the parties and diminishes the risk of deceit and employment. Blockchain technology is transformative in reshaping society and industry because of the fintech revolution and web utilisation \[[17](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_17)\]. According to this research, blockchain execution has been accepted to foster transparency and trust across various sectors due to increased 75competition in the digital landscape \[[18](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_18)\]. Integrating blockchain and artificial intelligence has changed business processes, increased efficiency and transparency and provided security to businesses \[[19](#C_011_c6.xhtml#r6_19)\]. Moreover, blockchain improved financial inclusion through various solutions, particularly in developing countries, and it can be made imaginable through IoT.

### 6.2.2 Fast Settlements

Blockchain tolerates fast settlements, avoiding the need for intermediaries and clearinghouses commonly created in old-fashioned financial systems. Smart contracts allow transactions to be automatically verified and settled founded on predefined conditions, as the agreement terms have been inscribed into the code. This automation reduces the time and cost related to the manual verification and processing.

### 6.2.3 Risk Management

Blockchain recovers risk management in finance, assuming that it has transparent and indisputable records of transactions. Financial institutions can use custom blockchain-based platforms to display and scrutinise transactions in real-time by more competently identifying the possible risks and anomalies.

### 6.2.4 Auditing

Auditing has become more efficient and dependable with blockchain technology. Auditors can authenticate the truthfulness of financial records without trusting third parties to settle the records.

### 6.2.5 Cost Minimisation

Blockchain weakens the costs in finance by eliminating intermediaries and eliminating manual processes. Smart contracts systematise the transaction clearances and implement the contract terms without the essential intermediaries such as banks or clearinghouses, thereby dipping the transaction fees and operational costs.

## 6.3 Research Methodology

The process begins with the expression of the key research questions designed to influence the primary advantages of blockchain technology in the respective financial sector, categorising the difficulties and constraints related to its implementation, measuring its influence on the various financial services, and discovering the emerging trends. Subsequently, a complete search strategy is developed using academic resources such as IEEE Xplore, Google, Science Direct, SpringerLink, and JSTOR. Keywords such as “Blockchain,” “Machine Learning,” “Finance Industry,” and “Regulatory” are working to answer the search. Specific inclusion criteria (IC) are recognised to select the works for presence in the review. These standards comprise keywords such as “Blockchain,” “fintech,” “distributed ledger technology,” and 76“Blockchain technology adoption” for IC 1. Rational operators are applied to define the standards’ composition.

Furthermore, the studies published before December 31, 2019, are omitted (IC 2), although only the research publications in English are measured (IC 3). The study types are restricted to journal articles (IC 4), and studies based on abstracts are included (IC 5). Furthermore, studies based on full texts are incorporated (IC 6). To eliminate literature from consideration for this systematic literature review, exclusion criteria (EC) are applied. Duplicate studies with identical titles and/or digital object identifiers are removed, and studies are eliminated based on quality ratings as specified in the exclusion conditions. At this stage, 42 studies were identified, of which 27 were deemed relevant to this investigation. The selected papers considered for this study are itemised in [Table 6.1](#C_011_c6.xhtml#t6_1).

## 6.4 Results, Discussion, and Findings

Based on the above discussion and systematic literature review, it is apparent that most research employs both qualitative and quantitative analyses. The qualitative studies are analysed using the systematic literature review method. At the same time, the quantitative analyses use various statistical software and tools such as SPSS AMOS and methods such as descriptive and inferential analysis. Numerous important discoveries are highlighted in the comprehensive literature study on the influence of blockchain technology on the respective financial sector. Blockchain technology power facilitates increased efficiency and decreased expenses by removing the mediators and restructuringthe financial operations.

## 6.5 Implications of Blockchain in Finance

Blockchain technology can have a number of important repercussions for the financial sector. One of the most noteworthy aspects is the potential to revolutionise the payment systems. Blockchain technology permits individuals to conduct transactions directly with one another without the need for intermediaries. This results in a growth in transaction speed and a decrease in transaction costs. An additional benefit of this decentralised model is that it increases transparency and reduces the risk of fraud, which is an important concern in outdated financial systems.

Moreover, blockchain can justify the procedure of securities trading and settlement. By using keen contracts, the blockchain platforms allow the automatic execution of contracts once predefined conditions are met, eliminating the need for mediators such as clearing houses and dipping settlement times. This capability improves efficiency and drops the functioning risks and costs associated with the post-employment processes.

## 6.6 Conclusion

Blockchain technology has enhanced the financial sector by creating a secure and decentralised platform for various financial claims. There is an opportunity for the introduction of FinTech explanations based on blockchain technology to revolutionise 7778the distribution of financial services, which will result in improved accessibility, reduced expenses, and greater scrutiny. Various blockchain applications have emerged across numerous industries, including healthcare, the Internet of Things, security, and supply chain management. This is because blockchain has made it possible to construct a storage system that is decentralised, secure, and conspicuous. Blockchain technology has the potential to transform many different banking procedures, which means that it might significantly impact the financial sector. The power of blockchain technology has revolutionised the financial industry, giving rise to DeFi. This new method of financial services is shadowed by its decentralised countryside, innovative solutions, seamless interoperability, global reach, and supreme transparency. By approving DeFi, we can temporary financial inclusion, encourage open access, stimulate unrestricted novelty, and unlock the world of options for entrepreneurs and visionaries. From the industry perspective, blockchain technology significantly enhances efficiency by streamlining transactions, improving security through decentralisation, and fostering transparency with a tamper-proof ledger.

# 7 Significance of Cryptography Techniques in Blockchain

Satyam Omar, Namita Tiwari, Vinay Shukla, Manvi Prajapati, and Swati Rawal

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-10](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-10)

## 7.1 Introduction

Blockchain is a distributed ledger technology that enables decentralised data storage across several computers (nodes). Blockchain comprises a block’s series, and each block plays the role of a public ledger, having a list of transactions, a timestamp, and a link to the block before it. Because of this structure, it is very difficult to change the data without also changing all of the blocks that come after it, which would require agreement from most of the network. Since a network of nodes uses a consensus method to validate and record transactions, the decentralised nature of blockchain eliminates the need for central authority. This decentralisation guarantees the security, integrity, and transparency of the data recorded on the blockchain when combined with cryptographic methods \[[1](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_1)\].

For decades, the study and application of secure communication methods, or cryptography, has been a vital component of human civilisation. To be more precise, cryptography is a means of securely communicating via an unreliable channel \[[2](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_2)\]. Today, cryptography serves as the foundation for many different digital security systems, guaranteeing data integrity, confidentiality, and authenticity. Blockchain, which mostly depends on encryption, is one of the most important inventions of the 21st century. Cryptography has a historical background. For instance, the Egyptians used substitution ciphers to secure important data on papyrus scrolls. After that, the Greeks made a noticeable contribution to the initial development of cryptography \[[3](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_3)\].

Later, Julius Caesar was known for deploying Caesar’s cipher to encode conversations in the military. All these strategies were heavily based on complex decipherability and secrecy. In the Middle Ages, the creation of poly-alphabetic cypher advanced cryptography significantly. The 19th century was important from the point of view of cryptanalysis, a science of breaking codes \[[4](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_4)\]. Charles Babbage’s work on automated decryption apparatus and Friedrich Kasiski’s frequency analysis set the background for modern cryptanalysis techniques. In the modern age, symmetric ciphers such as **Data Encryption Standard** (DES) and **Advanced Encryption Standard** (AES) were designed by deploying fiestel and substitution-permutation networks, respectively. In 1976, Whitfield Diffie and Martin Hellman \[[1](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_1)\] developed 81a key exchange protocol that opened the gateway of public-key cryptography. Later, the **Rivest–Shamir–Adleman** (RSA) cryptosystem \[[5](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_5)\] was designed, which comprises two distinct keys, namely the public key for encryption and the private key for decryption. After that, the fields of information security were completely transformed by ElGamal \[[3](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_3)\], **Digital Signature Algorithm** (DSA), and **Elliptic Curve Digital Signature Algorithm** (ECDSA). These innovations revolutionised applications like digital signatures, secure email, and safe online transactions by enabling secure communication without the requirement for a shared secret key.

Blockchain, a decentralised and distributed ledger technology, contributes to various sectors by providing secure, transparent, and immutable transaction records. Cryptographic protocols are important tools in accomplishing this target for blockchains. This chapter investigates the significance of cryptography in securing blockchain, looking at the various methods and applications through which the blockchain’s integrity and security could be customised by cryptographic techniques \[[6](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_6)\].

## 7.2 Role of Cryptography in Blockchain Security

Several critical components of blockchain security depend on cryptography, such as data confidentiality, data integrity, data authenticity, non-repudiation, consensus mechanisms, digital signatures, hash functions, and zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs).

### 7.2.1 Data Confidentiality

Data confidentiality secures the data from unauthorised access \[[7](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_7)\]. To maintain confidentiality, blockchain-primarily depends on encryption techniques. Encryption is a cryptographic technique that changes the original message into an unintelligible text that can only be retrieved in the original format by a special keyholder. Depending on the number of keys, the encryption techniques can be classified into the following two categories.

1.  **Symmetric Encryption:** In this type of technique, only one key is used by the sender of the message and the recipient of the message. This means that the same key is used for both encryption and decryption. As we know, *n*(*n*−1)/2 keys will be required to be shared in a network of *n* members. Therefore, despite being very effective and quick, it is not frequently employed in public blockchains. However, it is occasionally used in private blockchains to maintain confidentiality.
2.  **Asymmetric Encryption:** In this type of encryption technique, two distinct keys are used by the sender and receiver of the message. The sender uses the recipient’s public key to encode the original message, and the recipient uses his private key to decrypt the encoded message. There are only 2*n* keys required in a network of *n* users. This is why asymmetric encryption is frequently employed in blockchain systems to protect participant communications and transactions. Sensitive information is kept secret because only the owner of the private key may decrypt the data.

82 

### 7.2.2 Data Integrity

Data integrity ensures that the data has not been changed in an unauthorised manner \[[8](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_8)\]. The cryptographic hash function (CHF) is the most essential tool for maintaining integrity in blockchain applications. Modern digital security tools such as signature schemes, password protection, data integrity checks, and blockchain technology deploy CHFs. A CHF takes the input of flexible length and converts it into a string of smaller fixed length. The following are the key features of CHFs that make them practically usable.

1.  **Deterministic Output:** A CHF always provides the deterministic output. It means that if we give the same inputs to a CHF, it will provide the same output again and again. Due to this property, CHFs are useful in data integrity verification.
2.  **Fixed-Length Output:** A CHF consistently generates an output (hash) of the same length regardless of the size of the input data set. For example, whether the input is a single letter or an entire paragraph, the SHA-256 hash function always returns a fixed bit, i.e., a 256-bit (32-byte) hash result. Comparison and hash value management are made simpler by this set length.
3.  **Efficiency:** Fast and effective in processing vast volumes of data to generate hashes, CHF is built to last. This performance is essential for applications such as blockchain technology and internet communication security, wherein massive volumes of data must be hashed in real-time.
4.  **Pre-Image Resistance:** This property is all about finding the pre-image of a given hash output. It should be computationally impossible to deduce the original input given only the hash result. For sensitive data (for example, passwords), this characteristic is crucial when only the hash is kept.
5.  **Collision Resistance:** When two distinct inputs provide the same hash output, this is known as a collision. The goal of CHFs is to reduce the possibility of collisions so that every unique input produces a unique hash. For blockchain technology and digital signatures to preserve data confidentiality and integrity, collision resistance is essential.
6.  **Avalanche Effect:** This characteristic of CHFs indicates that a slight modification in the input of CHF results in a significantly different hash output. Due to this feature, it is simple to identify unauthorised changes, as even small changes to the input data result in an entirely different hash.

CHFs are extensively employed in numerous digital security sectors. To improve security in the case of a data breach, they are employed in password storage to store hashed passwords rather than the actual passwords safely. Hashing functions make it possible to quickly compare data in data integrity verification to make sure it has not been altered. Because malware infections can be disinfected by checking file integrity, it is significant for software distribution \[[9](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_9)\].

For practical usability, we can employ iterated hash functions. The iterated hash function is an example of a CHF, which takes an input message of any length and 83converts it into fixed-size blocks known as the hash value or digest. It repeatedly applies a compression function to each block. An iterated hash function works as follows.

1.  **Message Padding:** Message padding ensures that the length of the input message exceeds the block size. The standard steps in padding are to add one 1 bit, enough 0 bits, and then the length of the original message. The objective of this phase is to ensure that the message can be appropriately divided into fixed-size blocks.
2.  **Initialisation Vector:** An initialisation vector (IV), which is a fixed, specified value, is used to begin the hash computation. It is essential to the security of the hash function because it ensures that two identical messages shall produce different hash values if different IVs are utilised.
3.  **Processing Blocks:** The padded message divided into fixed-size blocks is processed one block at a time. The compression function accepts the current block as input and outputs the hash of the block (or, in the case of the first block, the IV). The compression function combines these inputs to create a fixed-size output, which is then used as the input for processing the following block.
4.  **Finalisation:** The hash value of the entire message is the compression function’s final output once all blocks have been processed. The cryptographic digest that accurately captures the original message is this fixed-length output.

The following are well-known CHFs based on the iterated hash function model.

1.  **MD5:** It takes an input of 512-bit blocks and outputs a 128-bit hash value. It is currently regarded as insecure because collision attacks happen due to some flaws.
2.  **SHA-1:** It takes 512-bit input blocks and produces a 160-bit hash value as output. SHA-1 was once widely used, but because of similar security issues, specifically the possibility of collisions, it has been deprecated.
3.  **SHA-256:** SHA-256 is a member of the SHA-2 family. It takes 512-bit blocks and returns a 256-bit hash value. It is still a popular and safe hash function, particularly in applications involving blockchain technology and other cryptography.
4.  **SHA-3:** The structure of SHA-3 depends on the sponge construction so that it can take the input of variable length and produce the output of variable length.

### 7.2.3 Data Authenticity

The process of confirming that data originates from a reliable source is known as data authenticity \[[10](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_10)\]. In blockchain, digital signatures are the main means of ensuring authenticity. Public key cryptography uses two different keys: a private key and a public key. These are used to establish digital signatures. The public key is kept in 84the dictionary, and the private key is kept confidential. The following procedures are involved in the creation of a digital signature.

1.  **Hashing the Message:** The original data runs through a CHF to generate a fixed-length hash value (also known as a message digest) prior to signing a message. The hash function guarantees that even slight modifications to the message produce a significantly different hash.
2.  **Signature Process:** The signer’s private key is then used to encrypt the hash value. The outcome is a digital signature that is specific to the message and the private key that was utilised. The signature acts as evidence that the signer is the original creator of the communication because only the signer has access to the private key.
3.  **Verification Process:** A digital signature can be verified by the recipient by decrypting it with the signer’s public key and getting the original hash value. After that, the recipient independently hashes the message they received. The communication is regarded as genuine and unaltered, and the signature is confirmed if the independently generated hash and the decoded hash match.

These are the following key properties and benefits of digital signatures.

1.  **Authenticity:** You can get the assurance through the digital signature that a communication or document comes from a certain sender. The recipient can be sure that the communication is genuine because the signature can only be produced using the signer’s private key.
2.  **Integrity:** With digital signatures, you can be sure that a communication or document has not had its content changed after it was signed. The verification process will fail if the original message is altered in any way, as even a little change will provide a different hash value.
3.  **Non-Repudiation:** When a signer cannot retract their signature, the communication or document is considered non-repudiated. A digital signature binds the signer to the material once it is created, and in many places, this might have legal consequences. In situations such as contracts and financial transactions, this is essential.

A lot of contemporary technology and procedures depend on digital signatures. They confirm the identity of the sender and assure that the email content has not been changed in secure email communications. Digital signatures in software distribution prevent bad actors from tampering with software and, therefore, allow consumers not to download and install tampered software. Digital signatures protect and validate financial transactions by allowing only valid parties to start or approve transactions \[[11](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_11)\]. Digital signatures are used to authenticate transactions using blockchain technology, and as soon as a user initiates a transaction, it is signed with his private key. This block would then connect this transaction to the network nodes using the user’s public key to validate his signature. This prevents fraudulent and undue actions because only valid transactions can stay within the blockchain.

85 

### 7.2.4 Non-Repudiation

The assurance that something cannot be denied is called non-repudiation \[[12](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_12)\]. Digital signature and non-repudiation are ideas that go perfectly together with the use of blockchain. A digital signature provides proof that a sender caused and authorised a transaction because it is linked only to the sender’s private key. Once such a transaction has been signed and set in the blockchain, the sender cannot deny participation in the transaction. Digital signatures were already covered in the section before this one.

### 7.2.5 Consensus Mechanisms

Protocols known as consensus mechanisms make sure that every node is in agreement with the legitimacy of transactions and the ledger’s current state \[[13](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_13)\]. These systems depend on cryptographic approaches to ensure that consensus is obtained securely and equitably.

1.  **Proof of Work:** Proof of Work (PoW) \[[7](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_7)\] is the most well-known consensus algorithm employed by Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies. In essence, it asks players or miners to solve challenging mathematical puzzles that resemble CHFs. Miners solve the riddle in addition to receiving the payment in Bitcoin. Because of the riddles’ difficulty, it is hard for one party to control the blockchain because it takes a lot of computing power to create a new block. PoW depends on hash function security to thwart attacks such as double-spending, which is the practice of spending the same coin more than once.
2.  **Proof of Stake:** An alternative to PoW \[[8](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_8)\] is Proof of Stake (PoS), in which the person who creates the next block is selected according to their stake (amount of cryptocurrency) in the network. PoS employs cryptographic approaches to ensure the selection process is secure and random, lowering the risk of centralisation and 51% attacks, even though it does not rely on solving cryptographic problems.
3.  **Delegated PoS:** In a variant of PoS referred to as Delegated Proof-of-Stake (DPoS) \[[9](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_9)\], participants elect a limited number of delegates who are in charge of approving transactions and maintaining the blockchain. To protect the voting process and guarantee that the chosen delegates behave in the network’s best interest, DPoS employs cryptographic techniques.

### 7.2.6 Digital Signatures and Authentication

As previously stated, digital signatures play a critical role in guaranteeing the legitimacy of data in blockchain systems \[[6](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_6)\]. The sender’s private key is used to create a unique signature during the data or transaction signing process. The signature is thus stamped onto the data in such a manner that anyone would be able to verify using the sender’s public key. However, verification authenticates the sender and checks if any modification has been made to the data. Digital signatures are used for authentication in smart contracts and also for securing transactions. Smart contracts are 86self-executing contracts that have complex contract specifications programmed in their codes. These are more transparent and need fewer middlemen as they are executed and kept on the blockchain. Digital signatures assure both the legitimacy of its parties and the security of the execution of the smart contract.

### 7.2.7 Hash Functions in Blockchain

CHF is one of the leading cryptographic techniques regarding blockchain technology \[[6](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_6)\], which supports procedures towards consensus while giving unique IDs and ensuring data integrity. Merkle trees \[[13](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_13)\], sometimes called hash trees, are currently part of blockchain systems as the most effective way to sort and validate large amounts of data. A Merkle tree, sometimes referred to as a hash tree, is any form of data structure used to collate and verify humongous sizes of data. This structure, named after computer scientist Ralph Merkle, takes in data to hash and repeatedly hashes it in pairs until it produces a single root hash, called the Merkle roots. In principle, this means that the Merkle root can be used as a kind of fingerprint for the set without needing to access the overall data set, and it could be used to verify any given piece of data within it. The following layers are used in the design for a Merkle tree, in which leaves are the discrete pieces of information that require hashing. Non-leaf nodes are intermediate hashes, which stand for child node pairs, and the Root node is a component that summarises the whole collection of data. It is because Merkle trees can efficiently verify data integrity in a distributed decentralised setting without having each network member keep the complete data set, thereby gaining popularity in technology for blockchain. Blockchain is a decentralised ledger that keeps records and verifies information through distributed consensus. A blockchain’s Merkle tree facilitates the safe, effective record-keeping and verification of the transactions that are contained in each block. The following are the key significances of Merkle trees in blockchain:

*   **Efficient Data Verification:** Blockchain networks involve a lot of transactions, and it would take a lot of resources to verify the integrity of each transaction independently. Merkle trees enable nodes to check only a subset of the data, rather than the entire blockchain, to confirm the integrity of every transaction. This process is referred to as “Merkle proof” or “Merkle verification.”
*   **Space Efficiency:** Each node in a blockchain network does not have to keep track of every transaction to ensure the chain’s integrity. Alternatively, nodes can retain the Merkle root and use Merkle proofs to validate transactions when necessary. As a result, participating nodes use a significantly smaller amount of storage space.
*   **Faster Verification:** By only requiring a logarithmic number of hashes to be examined (i.e., the height of the tree) instead of the complete list of transactions, a Merkle tree enables speedy confirmation of whether a specific transaction is part of a block. This significantly cuts down on the amount of time required for transaction verification.
*   **Data Integrity and Immutability:** Hash functions employed in Merkle trees have cryptographic qualities that are crucial to the blockchain’s security. 87Since every level of the tree builds off the hashed outcomes of the one before it, changing even a single transaction would produce a distinct Merkle root. The tamper-evident nature of the data guarantees that any alterations are promptly detected, hence adding to the immutability of the blockchain.

### 7.2.8 Zero-Knowledge Proofs

ZKPs allow one party to persuade another that a statement is true without providing any secret information. ZKPs hold an interesting position in blockchain as they can address the dual issues of privacy and security. In addition, ZKPs can also support transactions where only certain details are concealed, and the parties involved are enabled to verify the validity of the transaction even if the parties’ identities, along with the amount transferred, are hidden. This is an area of interest. Zcash \[[12](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_12)\] is a privacy-focused cryptocurrency that uses a flavour of ZKPs called Zero-Knowledge Suffix Non-Interactive Arguments of Knowledge (zk-SNARKs). ZKPs have also been applied in identity management systems and have made improvements to blockchain technology’s privacy and security to help prevent potential fraud and identity theft risks.

## 7.3 Cryptographic Challenges in Blockchain Security

Blockchains have a significant amount of protection due to cryptography, but they also face many challenges. Major cryptographic issues, such as quantum computing, are advancing technologies that threaten all the encryption methods used in current blockchain technology \[[14](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_14)\]. This advanced type of computing can solve some specific mathematical problems much quicker than supercomputers by using the properties of quantum mechanics. This advanced type of computing can solve certain specific mathematical problems much quicker than supercomputers by relying on properties of quantum mechanics, including breaking well-known cryptographic algorithms such as RSA and **Elliptic Curve Cryptography** (ECC) that are currently used in blockchain platforms. Because the quantum computer could break them, researchers are, therefore, starting to take a look at **Post-Quantum Cryptography** (PQC), which is resistant to quantum attacks. The long-term security of blockchain networks will thus depend on the adoption of PQC techniques in the advancements in quantum technology. Although highly secure, totally transparent, and decentralised, blockchain technology poses considerable challenges to scalability and efficiency.

The processing and validation of large amounts of transactions form a significant challenge to the growth of blockchain networks. While maintaining high-security systems for blockchain technology, the role of cryptography may indirectly affect the scalability and efficiency of the network systems. In previous discussions, we discussed the interrelationship of blockchain scalability and cryptography, the challenges faced by this problem, and how they can be overcome with advancements in cryptographic techniques. Key management is an essential component of blockchain security. The confidentiality of private keys is essential to the security of the system in asymmetric encryption. A lost or stolen private key can compromise related assets or data, resulting in large-scale financial losses or data breaches. Secure key storage solutions, multi-signature wallets, hardware security modules, and other effective 88key management techniques are crucial for protecting private keys and guaranteeing the security of blockchain systems.

## 7.4 Future Trends in Cryptography and Blockchain Security

As blockchain technology develops further, new ideas and cryptographic techniques are appearing to improve security and tackle the issues confronting the sector. The following are future trends in cryptography and blockchain security. The emergence of quantum computers is driving post-quantum cryptography research. Blockchain systems will require new encryption algorithms that can withstand quantum attacks in the future. The following post-quantum methods \[[10](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_10)\] can be applied in place of traditional cryptographic primitives: lattice-based cryptography, where the security depends on lattice problems such as the closest vector problem and the shortest vector problem; hash-based cryptography, where the security depends on the pre-image resistance of the hash function; code-based cryptography, where the security depends on coding theory problems such as syndrome decoding; multivariate cryptography, where the security depends on algebraic geometrical problems such as multivariate quadratic problems and isomorphism of polynomial problems; and isogeny-based cryptography, whose security relies on finding the isogenies within elliptic curves. Such algorithm development and standardisation will constitute one of the hottest topics in the coming years for both cryptography and blockchain communities.

In homomorphic encryption \[[11](#C_012_c7.xhtml#r7_11)\], calculations can be performed on encrypted data without first decrypting it. In this way, private, secure data processing within blockchain systems becomes possible. This approach is particularly useful for SMPC and privacy-preserving smart contracts. Enhanced privacy-conserving methodologies that go beyond ZKPs, such as differential privacy and secure multiparty computation (SMPC), are also being investigated for application within blockchain frameworks. These methodologies improve security and confidentiality for transactions, data exchange, and decentralised applications. The ever-increasing demand for privacy in the blockchain environment makes these approaches increasingly appealing.

Interoperability and cross-chain security are critical goals of cryptography, including enabling the seamless and secure transfer of both assets and information across diverse blockchains. This provides greater cross-chain communication and transaction possibilities. However, vulnerabilities in one blockchain can potentially affect others, making cross-chain security a pressing concern. Advancements in cryptographic protocols, including cross-chain bridges, atomic swaps, and others, are expected to lead to further improvements in both the security and interoperability of blockchain networks.

## 7.5 Conclusion

Blockchain technology uses the foundation of cryptography in terms of security. Cryptographic algorithms secure and decentralise the blockchain systems, which ensures data integrity, secrecy, authenticity, and non-repudiation. However, as the blockchain industry expands and advances, new risks and challenges like scalability 89issues and quantum computing are emerging. To solve these problems, cryptography research and innovation must continue. Post-quantum cryptography, homomorphic encryption, and advanced privacy-preserving techniques will be necessary to ensure the long-term security and viability of blockchain technology.

To sum up, cryptography is essential to blockchain security. As blockchain technology continues to transform industries and the digital world, cryptographic techniques will remain at the forefront of protecting the future of decentralised systems.

# 8 Leveraging the Internet of Things and Blockchain for Developing Intelligent Learning Models

Shahnawaz Ahmad, Mohammed Wasid, Shahadat Hussain, Sana Shahab, and Jiyaul Mustafa

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-11](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-11)

## 8.1 Introduction

In recent years, Internet of Things (IoT) and blockchain technology have altered the digital world by presenting new solutions across many industries. The IoT defines the integration of a contemporary device over the World Wide Web as interactant, aggregative, and cooperative via Internet-inculcated sensors and actors \[[1](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_1),[2](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_2)\]. This revolution presents a smart environment where home appliances, machines, and all sorts of objects can communicate and self-regulate their activities, enhancing automation’s performance, health, and scope in almost all aspects, including health, farming, production, and many others \[[3](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_3)\].

Blockchain, an open and distributed database, and IoT are the two technologies being embraced globally by organisations due to their profound capabilities in addressing some critical issues affecting IoT, such as security, privacy, and trust \[[4](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_4)\]. This problem becomes more challenging because more authenticated users join the IoT networks, hence there is an increased need to check the authenticity and integrity of data being exchanged by the devices in the system. Blockchain presents a strong base that reduces these factors’ effects by enhancing secure data management, preventing the alteration of records, and using decentralised consensus techniques \[[5](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_5)\]. This makes it appropriate for use in main applications such as smart contracts, supply chains, and other decentralised finance applications because they require high- security measures as well as more visibility.

In parallel with such development, intelligent learning models (ILMs) serve as helpful tools for dealing with the huge amount of data generated by IoT devices. Artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML), and deep learning (DL) build models so that the systems can learn the patterns and further make correctly targeted estimations and decisions. AI is the general concept of the capacity of a computer to perform work intelligently \[[6](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_6)\]. AutoAI, a subcategory of AI, is an executive-aiding application of algorithms that adapt in response to specific data inputs. DL is a subcategory of ML and it employs artificial neural networks with added layers to mine 91complex data and provide improved accuracy \[[7](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_7)\]. These four systems, which include IoT, blockchain, and ILMs, work harmoniously to enable the collection, sharing, and analysis of big data. This chapter explores how these technologies can be leveraged to develop ILMs that enhance decision-making and operational efficiency across various sectors, thereby driving innovation and solving contemporary challenges.

## 8.2 Background

### 8.2.1 Internet of Things

The IoT refers to the interconnection via the Internet of computing devices embedded in everyday objects that enable them to send and receive data. This is possible because almost all “things” or objects can now be equipped with various sensors and technologies for collecting and exchanging data. Sensors can obtain environmental data by collecting information on light, heat, sound, chemical composition, or other similar factors. Embedded systems use this sensory information to interact with their external environment by taking appropriate actions. IoT technology allows multiple devices to communicate using web services and embedded software. Web services are intermediaries between different processes or applications within a single system or between different systems on the same platform or across multiple platforms \[[1](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_1)\].

The IoT has many applications, such as tracking animal behaviours, identifying soil moisture levels in farms, monitoring diabetes patients, improving personal healthcare management, developing greater self-assistance tools for older people, and enhancing environmental monitoring functions. It was estimated that there would be nearly 20 billion IoT devices worldwide by 2020, and global spending on IoT technology is projected to reach somewhere near $1.3 trillion. The blockchain market is projected to reach $3 trillion by 2024. Many companies, including Google, Amazon, Cisco Systems, Intel, General Electric, IBM, Dell, Microsoft, HP, and Bosch, have adopted IoT technology to enhance their operations. For example, in 2015, General Electric established GE Digital to transform industrial machines into smart machines, launching Predix.io as an IoT platform that provides machine monitoring tools for industries such as hospitality, aviation, and utilities.

Typically, IoT architecture comprises five primary layers, as shown in [Figure 8.1](#C_013_c8.xhtml#f8_1). The first layer, perception layer, is responsible for sensing the information (data/actuators) from the surrounding physical world. It uses different technologies, such as Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, and 5G, to connect and communicate with the device machines/objects. The second layer, known as the network layer, transfers all information the perception layer collects to other layers for further processing. The third layer, 92processing layer, contains cloud components or middleware, which store, filter, and analyse all transferred data from the network layer. The fourth layer, application layer, provides actual data (information), i.e., it provides services to the user by creating Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) using data received from the processing layer. The fifth layer, security and management layer, handles the security and management of the entire architecture. It includes various activities such as securing data during transmission over several networks or using a time security policy so no one can use that device within the period mentioned.

![Figure 8.1 Layers of the IoT architecture. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig08_01.jpg)

Figure 8.1 Layers of the IoT architecture. [Return to text.⏎](#C_013_c8.xhtml#f8_1b)

A device is considered “smart” when it has two essential capabilities. First, it must be able to interact with the physical world, which involves processing collected data and communicating with its environment through wireless technologies such as Bluetooth, RFID, and Wi-Fi. Second, it must be capable of collecting data through sensors or actuators that gather information from its surroundings. These two capabilities allow a smart device to effectively monitor and respond to environmental changes, enhancing its utility and functionality. A prime example of a smart device is a smartwatch. When an ordinary watch is connected to a smartphone via Bluetooth, it can become a smartwatch capable of tracking various metrics such as calories burned, heart rate, steps taken, messages, weather forecasts, and sleep patterns, in addition to providing basic time and date functions. Smart devices generate vast amounts of data that can be stored in the cloud, processed, and analysed using advanced techniques such as genetic algorithms, neural networks, and AI. This analysis enables devices to make informed decisions. Transparency and reliable devices are essential to ensure data security and privacy \[[8](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_8)\].

As shown in [Figure 8.2](#C_013_c8.xhtml#f8_2), IoT applications are transforming numerous industries, including smart homes, healthcare, smart cities, and agriculture. In smart homes, IoT allows users to control devices such as lights and appliances remotely, while in healthcare, it supports remote patient monitoring and wearable device tracking. IoT enhances traffic management, waste collection, and infrastructure maintenance in urban settings. In agriculture, it contributes to precision farming and livestock tracking. Manufacturing benefits from IoT through automation and optimised supply chains, and in transportation, it advances connected vehicles, fleet management, and smart parking. These examples highlight how IoT improves efficiency, convenience, and sustainability across sectors \[[9](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_9)\].

![Figure 8.2 Applications of Internet of Things. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig08_02.jpg)

Figure 8.2 Applications of Internet of Things. [Return to text.⏎](#C_013_c8.xhtml#f8_2b)

IoT has transformed numerous industries by enabling optimisation through data- driven insights. For example, automakers can use data from vehicle sensors to monitor and improve the efficiency and performance of their designs \[[10](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_10)\]. In addition to being able to access more data than would be possible using conventional data collection strategies, the real-time nature of IoT allows for immediate feedback. Although dealing with such large amounts of data can be problematic, decision-making can be better informed, and resources can be more effectively deployed thanks to the immediacy offered by IoT, which can also reduce waste and improve overall system performance.

IoT systems provide many benefits, but there are also important challenges and risks that arise due to (i) insecure/insufficient authentication and access controls; (ii) non-transparent/inadequate information usage consent and revocation mechanisms; (iii) shortcomings in the dependability of the IoT system service functionality; (iv) integration with other systems—(a) interaction of the IoT system with other 93independently developed smart space applications, i.e., coexistence of independently managed objects in a common environment, and (b) interaction of other systems with IoT systems e.g., integration with existing legacy services into an IoT-enhanced enterprise/organisational IT infrastructure; (v) lifecycle issues—management of increasingly dynamic information including sensing, mining, and processing proprietary enterprise data resources/devices that do not have well defined or annotated public interfaces; (vi) their compliance to regulatory context-specific usage constraints, for example, in eHealth environments \[[11](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_11)\].

### 8.2.2 Blockchain

Blockchain is an Internet technology that uses the private key cryptography and incentive mechanisms to construct a decentralised, distributed, and shared immutable ledger of transaction blocks in chronological order, such that it ensures security in transactions \[[11](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_11)\]. On the contrary, issues exist with traditional bank systems, such 94as double spending, centralisation, and vulnerability to hacking activities, especially in the P2P paradigm of IoT. Against this context, innovation blockchain provides a highly secure decentralised solution for peer-to-peer transactions. In the IoT world, trust among devices and networks is extremely important. Blockchain builds trust by introducing novel authorisation methods to block unauthorised accesses and data manipulations. Its immutability makes altering recorded transactions challenging, enhancing security and making it suitable for payment systems. To ensure data security and integrity, blockchain uses elliptic curve cryptography and SHA-256 hashing algorithms to safeguard against unauthorised access and tampering \[[2](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_2)\].

Blockchain technology is a new alternative to traditional payment systems such as online wallets, banks, and third-party services. Let me explain this with an example: four friends (Jack, Ted, Sam, and Phil) decide to have dinner together and share the expenses. So, Phil can directly transfer money to Jack using an online payment service. At the same time, for Ted and Sam, it is impossible because of their bank issues, such as technical problems, account hacked issues, or transaction limits exceeding for the day. Cryptocurrencies will remedy this whole difficulty. They are digital currencies based on blockchain technology that works in a decentralised system without the mediation of banks \[[12](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_12)\].

Decentralisation has many advantages, such as enhanced security, reduced transaction fees, and increased control over users’ financial activities \[[3](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_3)\]. Cryptocurrencies provide many advantages over normal money; they are impossible to counterfeit, do not rely on a central authority, and are protected by strong encryption \[[13](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_13)\]. Out of the thousands of cryptocurrencies, Bitcoin is the most well-known \[[14](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_14)\].

Cryptocurrencies are based on blockchain technology. Whenever a cryptocurrency transaction occurs (e.g., when one person sends money to another), a transaction record is created and added to a block linked to previous blocks in a chain. This chain is known as a ledger. All network participants keep their copy of this ledger, increasing transparency and security. Since the ledger is distributed across the network, it becomes difficult for hackers to manipulate information because others will see any modification to a transaction: their ledgers will not match. Data can also be hidden from unauthorised individuals using encryption algorithms.

[Table 8.1](#C_013_c8.xhtml#t8_1) compares permissioned and permissionless blockchains. Permissioned blockchains restrict block creation for authorised nodes, require permissions for changes, and have high privacy. Instead, permissionless blockchains allow anyone to create blocks without changing permissions and having low privacy \[[4](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_4)\].

Table 8.1 Permissioned vs. Permissionless Blockchain [Return to text.⏎](#C_013_c8.xhtml#t8_1b)

 
| Feature || Permissioned Blockchain || Permissionless Blockchain |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Node participation || Only authorised nodes can create blocks. || Anyone can create blocks. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Permission || Requires permission to make changes. || No permission is required to make changes. |
| Access || Typically, private. || Publicly accessible. |
| Privacy || High. || Low. |

95[Table 8.2](#C_013_c8.xhtml#t8_2) compares public and private blockchains with respect to their permissions, access, and privacy. A public blockchain is accessible to everyone, allowing anyone to participate in the network and view its transactions. Public blockchains are also permissionless (i.e., no special authorisation is required to create new blocks). A private blockchain is private for authorised users. Only one can create a block or access the network if they have permission to do so. This model can provide better privacy than public blockchains \[[4](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_4),[15](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_15)\].

Table 8.2 Public vs. Private Blockchain [Return to text.⏎](#C_013_c8.xhtml#t8_2b)

 
| Feature || Public Blockchain || Private Blockchain |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Permission || Permissionless || Permissioned |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Access || Open to everyone || Restricted to authorised participants |
| Privacy || Low || High |

Bitcoin transactions use a dual-key system: a public key, which functions like an email address, and a private key, like a password. When sending Bitcoin, the user includes the recipient’s address and amount and signs the transaction with their private key. A cryptographic algorithm generates a digital signature that verifies the sender’s identity \[[7](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_7),[16](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_16)\]. The signed transaction is then published on the Bitcoin network using the recipient’s public key; however, only their private key can unlock and utilise the transaction. The first miner to solve the puzzle is rewarded with new Bitcoins. Blockchain is increasingly used across industries to improve security and transparency. For instance, Walmart employed blockchain to monitor product quality along its supply chain, identifying failures and ensuring better product outcomes. This illustrates blockchain’s practical applications in solving real-world issues.

## 8.3 Intelligent Learning Models

ILMs are advanced computing models that use learning techniques such as ML and DL algorithms to analyse large data sets. These models can uncover and learn intricate patterns and relationships that traditional analytics might not reveal. Also, the more varied the types of data ILMs can take in, the richer their insights or predictions will produce. Moreover, ILMs can adapt their performance over time as they learn from newer data, a defining feature in most IoT and blockchain use cases.

[Table 8.3](#C_013_c8.xhtml#t8_3) summarises emerging trends and technologies in the blockchain-AI-IoT environment. It includes edge computing, federated learning, hybrid blockchains, secure hardware, data marketplaces, AI-powered blockchain analytics, self-sovereign identity, quantum-resistant cryptography, and energy-efficient blockchain solutions. These technologies offer various benefits, such as improved security, privacy, efficiency, scalability, and sustainability in blockchain-based systems.

## 8.4 Observations

Blockchain technology is certainly best suited for cryptocurrencies, but it has its uses in other fields. Thus, the fact of its activities in digital currencies became a valuable experience for its application in healthcare, economy, and governmental social benefits programs. Therefore, to unleash the potential of advanced technology, organisations need to gain a better understanding of the context and find use cases that can benefit from the elimination of intermediaries. However, the IoT has still not overcome security and privacy challenges because of its non-centralised structure and resource constraints, but it has become popular lately. Maybe that is why traditional security solutions cannot help secure IoT: millions of devices remain under a hacker’s attack and open to data leaks.

It is, therefore, commensurate to focus on securing interaction between devices and their surroundings to enhance IoT security \[[29](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_29)\]. The combination with IoT, which is known as Blockchain-enabled Internet of Things (BIoT), can solve some of the problems present with the use of blockchain. The BIoT concept works with these technologies combined in order to lower the reliance on computationally intense mining algorithms, which could be more appropriate for lightweight IoT. To benefit fully from BIoT, a new framework needs to be defined in order to accommodate various distributed IoT applications \[[30](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_30)\]. Therefore, for BIoT to work, there is a need to address the scalability issue and reduce overhead traffic. The authors believe that 99blockchain database designers should strive to improve the speed of transactions and the number of transactions due to the computational burden that a large number of transactions place on IoT devices with limited power.

For the same reason that the internet exists, blockchain technology goes on changing and exists as well. Its IoT integration can create new standards of data openness and enhance interactions between similar devices. As these technologies progress, they could open many new markets of trillion-dollar worth.

## 8.5 Future Research Directions for Blockchain-IoT Integration

Blockchain combined with IoT has certain issues that need further discussion to point directions to successful integration. Altogether, these challenges cannot be solved with one solution, but adequate and innovative solutions must be integrated.

*   **Scalability**: This is alarming, mainly given the amount of data on IoT, and the problem is worsening given the use of rapid 5G networks. Regarding this problem, some solutions can be used to enhance scalability. Sidechains and rollups, which are scaling solutions, are used above layer 2 to enhance throughput by reducing the load of the main blockchain through off-chain transactions. Sharding, the other approach, splits the main blockchain network into multiple sub-networks, thereby minimising traffic congestion and improving transaction rates. Also, other complex consensus algorithms, such as Proof of Stake and Delegated Proof of Stake, require fewer computational resources to back up scalability \[[22](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_22)\]. A combination of the features of public and private blockchains, known as hybrid blockchains, are characterised by their ability to have faster and more effective processes of transactions’ execution while providing for data confidentiality and protection. In combination, these strategies appear to herald scalable handling of IoT within the constraints of a 5G setting.

### 8.5.1 Security and Privacy

These are some of the fundamental issues that are central to the use of blockchain and IoT. Future research in this area will focus on several perspectives. The PRIV-enhancing technologies are zero-knowledge proofs and homomorphic encryption. They provide means for protecting confidential information, and at the same time, they can be used to work with and transform in unsecured environments. Decentralised identity solutions ensure that users are in charge of their identity, so keeping your identity safe from fraudsters or hackers is not a big issue. Moreover, security created through smart contracts, encryption, and digital signatures can improve IoT devices or related transactions when based on blockchain technology. These advancements seek to produce a secure, privacy-preserving blockchain IoT environment \[[23](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_23)\].

*   100**Interoperability:** This is a unique issue when it comes to IoT and blockchain integration because of the numerous protocols and standards implemented in both fields. Several important areas should be addressed in future research activities. First, the creation of standardised protocols is going to become a critical issue in the interactions between IoT devices and blockchain networks. It can also help create a single middleware framework that facilitates more efficient data exchange between IoT objects and blockchain platforms, eliminating incompatibility problems. Specific issues can also be addressed with reference to existing IEEE standards that include the use of blockchain with IoT: IEEE P2418.1, IEEE P2418.8, and IEEE P2418.9. It surfaced in an attempt to enhance the integration processes and make blockchain-powered IoT environments more integrated \[[24](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_24)\].
*   **Edge Computing**: This can, in turn, improve the performance and efficiency of blockchain-IoT systems since machine data processing will be done close to IoT devices. The above strategy brings the following benefits. First of all, the method minimises latency, which provides more opportunities for making decisions in real-time, and its importance is obvious for many applications of IoT. Second, edge computing promotes privacy and security due to handling sensitive data and not sending it online for processing. Finally, it aids in governance, where data handling reflects regulatory compliance at both regional and international levels. They make edge computing a useful element when it comes to improving and optimising blockchain IoT interfaces \[[25](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_25)\].
*   **Federated Learning**: This presents a useful way through which blockchain-IoT systems can be made even more effective since IoT devices can jointly train AI models locally. This approach results in two major advantages. First, it keeps data private, which are personal; the data do not go to central servers but remain on the users’ devices. Second, federated learning provides enhanced efficiency because it avoids the transmitting and analysis of large volumes of data that are otherwise generative. This isolation of nodes suits the decentralised model well and adds to the security and functionality of IoT networks underpinning blockchain \[[25](#C_013_c8.xhtml#r8_25)\].

## 8.6 Conclusion

In this chapter, the combination of IoT and blockchain to build ILMs for optimising the performance and security of smart systems has been discussed. We began with the principles of IoT and how devices can convey and exchange particular information, as well as blockchain, which guarantees the integrity and authenticity of such shared information in a peer-to-peer network. More important is the discussion on the integration of the two technologies. The integration is referred to as BIoT, and this paper aims to explore it and look at its positives and negatives. We also explored how ILMs such as AI, ML, and DL can be used to analyse the large volumes of data created by IoT systems. These models contain superior analysis functions that 101support predictions on preventive maintenance, decision-making, and security. AI, ML, and DL, when incorporated into IoT environments, make the BIoT systems even more powerful because they have become smart systems. In the course of the chapter, we discussed some issues related to BIoT integration, including scalability, security, and privacy. We described several challenges within the context of actual applications, and we suggested potential solutions based on new consensus algorithms, anonymity solutions, and edge computing for decentralised data processing. Finally, we discussed research areas that are important for enhancing the use of IoT, blockchain, and ILM in the future. These are enhancing the applicability of blockchain for IoT for better scalability at large, enhancing privacy for the same by using enhanced cryptographic measures and analysing the use of federated learning to form a decentralised setup for the maintenance of AI. Mitigating these can fully unlock BIoT systems’ opportunity for smarter, safer, and autonomous applications across various industries.

# 9 Blockchain Innovation in IOT-Based Healthcare Issues and Future Opportunities

Kedar Nath Singh, Arvind Kumar Upadhyay, and Arvind Mewada

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-12](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-12)

## 9.1 Introduction

Internet of Things (IoT) devices, bulges, and systems must be able to collaborate despite their differences and provide various health services under Healthcare 4.0 to continuously check the health of patients \[[1](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_1)\]. Healthcare workflows incorporate services from several nodes. These include patient login information, medical consultations, real-time health record monitoring, and payments. All services are handled sequentially on platforms for cloud and fog computing \[[2](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_2)\]. The nodes are identical and exchange data according to a trust agreement. Hospital branches can share patient data, allowing convenient access anywhere. These hospitals also use services from payment processors, insurance providers, and others, which share secure data for processing. Blockchain has recently become a major technology with uses extending beyond Bitcoin. For example, the resource limits of IoT devices and the ledger-based structure of Blockchain protocols provide difficulties that necessitate future research. However, these advantages may expose potential attackers to security weaknesses and privacy issues.

The IoT is quickly increasing, with devices primarily used to monitor and control environmental changes, reduce dangers, and provide a wide range of useful services. Blockchain and IoT integration is still in its infancy because of resource limitations and ledger-centric architecture. Blockchain gives flexibility but also has the potential to generate privacy problems and security flaws that adversaries might exploit \[[3](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_3)\].

Blockchain technology has evolved dramatically since its inception, with each iteration bringing in innovations and discoveries, as shown in [Table 9.1](#C_014_c9.xhtml#t9_1). The transition from cryptocurrencies to modern blockchain-based industrial applications represents a movement in using blockchain technology beyond digital currencies \[[4](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_4)\]. Blockchain applications are becoming increasingly popular in industries ranging from 1.0 to 10.0.

Table 9.1 Types of Blockchain Technology Required in Industry [Return to text.⏎](#C_014_c9.xhtml#t9_1b)

 
| Types of Blockchain || Application Description |
| --- | --- |
| Blockchain 1.0 || Bitcoin, introduced in 2009, was the first implementation of blockchain technology. It focused on peer-to-peer electronic cash transactions. This allowed safe, decentralised digital payments without intermediaries like banks. |
| --- | --- |
| Blockchain 2.0 || It is operated by Ethereum, which was founded in 2015. Smart contracts, or contracts that can be programmed to execute themselves, were introduced. Developers can construct both decentralised applications (DApps) and decentralised autonomous organisations. This expanded blockchain’s applications beyond transactions. It now contains Decentralized Finance (DeFi), supply chain management, and other features. |
| Blockchain 3.0 || It sought to address scalability difficulties that limited transaction throughput and speed. Sharing, sidechains, and layer two scaling (similar to Bitcoin’s Lightning Network) are proposed. Interoperability standards promoted communication and data sharing between blockchain networks. |
| Blockchain 4.0 || The purpose is to enhance the security, privacy, and governance mechanisms. New consensus algorithms have been launched, including Proof of Stake. Zero-knowledge proofs and privacy-focused cryptocurrencies like Zcash and Monero increased privacy. Governance systems have emerged to support decentralised decision-making and network enhancements. |
| Blockchain 5.0 || Blockchain 5.0 intends to combine blockchain, AI, IoT, and big data. It emphasises sustainability and energy efficiency. Eco-friendly consensus procedures and renewable energy for mining are being developed. Enhanced regulatory compliance systems handle legal issues. These criteria promote mainstream adoption. |
| Blockchain 6.0 || Blockchain 6.0 might put user experience first, making the technology more accessible. This involves improving user interfaces and minimising transaction prices. Improved scalability would allow for more users while maintaining speed. Blockchain-based reputation systems may enable less trustful interactions in various industries, including e-commerce and social networks. Environmentally friendly consensus processes should be prioritised. Energy-efficient protocols and carbon-neutral infrastructure can potentially lessen blockchain technology’s environmental impact. |
| Blockchain 7.0 || Scalability could overcome this issue through improved consensus mechanisms and sharing approaches. Other scalability options support more transactions per second. Standardised protocols and bridges enable seamless connectivity across various blockchain ecosystems. Advanced cryptography technologies enable users to transact without revealing sensitive information. Energy-efficient consensus methods can be implemented in the blockchain architecture. |
| Blockchain 8.0 || Blockchain 8.0 may employ quantum-resistant cryptography to safeguard data against quantum attacks. Quantum computing poses a danger to present cryptography algorithms. It may allow users to establish and maintain reputations depending on their interactions. Immutable reputation systems may be introduced. These systems could be used to establish decentralised governance, verify identities, and foster trust. This would improve accountability and openness in online interactions. |
| Blockchain 9.0 || Blockchain 9.0, despite being theoretical, has the potential to transform blockchain technology. It could include advancements in numerous sectors. Future technological breakthroughs will decide its specific features. The blockchain community’s efforts, as well as societal requirements, will shape its potential. |
| Blockchain 10.0 || It might fully integrate quantum computing capabilities. This encompasses quantum-resistant cryptography and quantum consensus techniques. These features would provide unsurpassed security and performance. |

Blockchain technology to safely and dependably manage healthcare data is becoming increasingly popular. Blockchain revolutionises healthcare by 105106facilitating secure data sharing and enhancing diagnosis and treatment efficiency. By integrating all real-time clinical data about a patient’s health into an already-existing, safe healthcare framework, blockchain has the potential to play a significant role in the future provision of tailored, genuine, and secure healthcare. This study uses blockchain technology to look at recent and upcoming developments in the health sector business \[[5](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_5)\]. A blockchain network has been utilised by healthcare providers to confidentially store and share patient data between pharmacies, doctors’ offices, hospitals, and diagnostic labs. Blockchain-based applications can identify serious and potentially harmful faults in healthcare.

Consequently, it enhances the healthcare system’s medical data interchange’s efficiency, safety, and transparency. Medical organisations benefit from this technology since it provides valuable information and improves the analysis of medical records \[[6](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_6)\]. Blockchain technology provides a distributed, secure database that may function without management or central authority. Blockchain continuously creates a growing list of consecutive items called blocks via a distributed, peer-to-peer network combined to build a digital ledger. Every transaction is included in a cryptographically signed block that is automatically verified by the network. Due to its potential to enhance data transparency and authenticity through various applications, including limiting access to electronic health records (EHRs) and streamlining the claims process, blockchain is gaining popularity in the healthcare sector \[[7](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_7)\]. A robust and productive healthcare system depends on interoperability since it enables software and technical platforms to use and share data between various health organisations and application providers securely and efficiently. The healthcare sector is currently dealing with problems including fragmented and compartmentalised data, delayed communications, and disjointed workflow tools due to a lack of interoperability. Blockchain provides a solution by facilitating the access of longitudinal, tamper- evident, and comprehensive medical records stored in disparate systems while preserving pseudo-anonymity and security \[[8](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_8)\].

Blockchain technology is a distributed, transparent, and safe numerical transaction scheme that uses blocks to store data, with transactions being the fundamental units. Consensus techniques ensure data security and integrity through cryptographic hashing. Nodes, computers, or servers participate in the peer-to-peer network design, allowing direct communication without a central authority. The details of the primary elements of blockchain technology are provided in [Table 9.2](#C_014_c9.xhtml#t9_2).

Table 9.2 Primary Components of Blockchain Technology [Return to text.⏎](#C_014_c9.xhtml#t9_2b)

 
| Primary Components || Components Description |
| --- | --- |
| Distributed ledger || A digital ledger distributed across numerous computers or nodes. Each participant received an identical copy. This ensures transparency while also lowering the danger of fraud. |
| --- | --- |
| Cryptography || Used to protect data and maintain transaction integrity. Public and private keys allow for safe data transfer and user authentication. Hashing creates a unique digital fingerprint for each block, assuring data integrity. |
| Consensus mechanism || Nodes utilise protocols to determine transaction validity and blockchain state. Proof of Work and Proof of Stake are two common consensus techniques. Other options are Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance and Delegated Proof of Stake. |
| Blocks || Data structures store transaction information. Each block contains a header containing metadata. This includes a date, a hash value of the preceding block, and a nonce (in PoW systems). The block also includes transaction details. |
| Smart contracts || Contracts that execute themselves and have their terms written in code. These contracts automatically enforce obligations depending on predetermined criteria. They remove the need for intermediaries. |
| Decentralisation || The network runs on a peer-to-peer basis, with no central authority. Each node possesses equal authority. This approach is censorship-resistant and avoids centralised points of failure. |
| Immutability || The information is recorded in a block and uploaded to the blockchain, and it is unchangeable or unavoidable. Cryptographic hashing and block linking ensure this. Tampering with previous records becomes incredibly tough. |
| Transparency and anonymity || Transactions are transparent and accessible to anyone on the network. The participants’ real names are concealed. Public addresses replace personal information. This improves privacy while remaining transparent. |

A digital ledger is crucial for financial services and operations, and blockchain technology ensures data encryption and reduces data breaches. It allows for multiple shared copies of a single database, reducing the risk of attacks. Blockchain is transforming the banking industry by efficiently tracking and documenting tangible and intangible assets \[[9](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_9)\]. Consensus methods, access control, and purpose categorise blockchain technology. The network consists of nodes, each with a shared ledger, updated regularly and subject to verification processes. The classification of blockchain technology is depicted in [Figure 9.1](#C_014_c9.xhtml#f9_1).

Figure 9.1 Classification of blockchain technology. [Return to text.⏎](#C_014_c9.xhtml#f9_1b)
