107 

## 9.3 Important IoT-Based Healthcare Issues

IoT-based healthcare provides numerous advantages, including enhanced patient monitoring, data collection, and operational efficiency, but it also has several significant drawbacks. Healthcare systems heavily trust data collected by IoT sensors, which must be protected from unauthorised modifications due to security and privacy concerns.

*   **Privacy and Data Security:** IoT applications in healthcare can boost system effectiveness, promote patient well-being, and facilitate quick emergency reactions. Data processing poses significant privacy security risks, as well as documentation, monitoring, and data transfer of these systems. Patients’ lives may be in danger if treatment and diagnosis are delayed due to worries about security, privacy, and healthcare data protection. IoT implementation improves patient outcomes, increases system performance, and speeds emergency response \[[10](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_10)\]. However, there are substantial threats to the security and privacy of data when it comes to these systems throughout processing, documentation, monitoring, and transfer. Problems with the 108109110111confidentiality, privacy, and protection of medical data may put patients in danger by delaying diagnosis and treatment. Healthcare IoT technologies can significantly enhance emergency response times, system performance, and patient care. However, they also present significant privacy and security issues regarding the processing, documentation, monitoring, and data transfer. These worries about privacy, security, and data protection may cause delays in diagnosis and treatment, which could endanger patients’ lives \[[11](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_11)\].
*   **Cooperation:** Collaborative features in IoT-based healthcare systems must be implemented in a decentralised environment using a distributed algorithm that ensures efficient resource sharing and equitable distribution of rewards among involved users. The simulation results indicate that this approach consistently improves system-wide and user-specific performance. In addition, the strategy ensures fair distribution of resources \[[12](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_12)\].
*   **The Ability to Scale Devices:** Recent advancements in computing and smart device technology have led to the generation of massive amounts of “Big Data” that are crucial for forecasting future trends. However, some technologies struggle to handle this complexity, causing concerns and issues that require attention \[[13](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_13)\]. Healthcare providers require scalable infrastructure to manage this inflow of data without sacrificing efficiency.
*   **Integration and Management of Data:** IoT network for e-health systems, aiming to improve security, safety, and data confidentiality. It suggests collecting data from smart health devices, and filtering and analysing it for efficient and cost-effective diagnosis and treatment of various illnesses. Using smart fitness and health devices connected to the IoT blockchain network to collect and analyse health data enables efficient and cost-effective diagnosis and treatment for various illnesses and ensures data confidentiality.
*   **Commitment to Regulations:** The IoT is revolutionising various sectors, including healthcare, by enabling real-time data collection and analysis despite numerous technological challenges. Healthcare IoT solutions must adhere to laws regulating patient privacy and data protection, which can be challenging to navigate and require constant attention \[[14](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_14)\].
*   **User Training and Adoption:** Exploring the use of IoT-based wearable devices in ageing healthcare records and examining the effects of factors promoting and inhibiting their use. Wearables with IoT technology offer real-time health monitoring, increased independence, and improved communication with healthcare professionals, enabling quick responses to abnormalities. Older individuals may face challenges in adopting wearables with IoT capabilities, such as data security, privacy, cost, and stigma concerns. They may fear becoming dependent on digital devices \[[13](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_13)\]. Healthcare workers need to get the proper training to use IoT devices and analyse the data they provide.
*   **Network Connectivity:** IoT-based healthcare systems require stable network connectivity for real-time data, especially for wearable monitors and smart sensors. Disruptions can lead to data loss or delays, so backup connectivity options are crucial for emergencies. Encrypting data transmitted over networks, implementing strong authentication and authorisation mechanisms, 112and preventing unauthorised access to sensitive health data are crucial measures to protect patient confidentiality. The growing number of IoT devices in healthcare necessitates a scalable network infrastructure to handle the significant data volume generated by these devices without compromising performance. Inadequate network infrastructure might hinder the adoption and efficacy of IoT-based healthcare solutions, especially in rural or disadvantaged areas.
*   **Ethical and Legal Issues:** IoT technology, including smart devices and applications, collects and monitors data, resulting in big data. Protecting this data from cyber threats requires advanced encryption techniques, secure communication protocols, and robust authentication systems. Ethical and legal concerns arise from collecting and using big data, as personal information can lead to privacy violations. Regulations such as **General Data Protection Regulation** (GDPR) and **Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act** (HIPAA) provide guidelines for enforcement, but this is complicated due to the global nature of the Internet and IoT. To overcome these challenges, coordinated efforts from technology developers, policymakers, and international bodies are needed to ensure IoT technology’s safe and ethical use \[[14](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_14)\].
*   **Battery Life and Power Management:** The IoT comprises sensor nodes that monitor physiological signals like **Electroencephalogram** (EEG), **Electrocardiogram** (ECG), blood pressure, and temperature using cellular networks, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, etc. In the health sector, the Internet of Medical Things consists of resource-constrained wearable devices. The foremost task is energy drain and battery consumption in these used devices. Numerous IoT devices are battery-operated, and it can be inconvenient to constantly change or recharge batteries, particularly for wearable or implanted devices \[[15](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_15)\].

## 9.4 Blockchain Technology Solved Internet of Things-Related Health Problems

Blockchain technology can completely transform IoT-based healthcare systems, offering revolutionary possibilities for IoT-enabled healthcare. Blockchain technology and IoT can significantly improve healthcare services, innovate, and drastically alter IoT-enabled healthcare systems.

### 9.4.1 Improved Privacy and Security of Data

The IoT has led to the development of distributed ledgers and blockchains, offering enhanced security, anonymity, and capacity. However, these technologies face limited storage, high resource use, transaction rate scalability, predictability, and legal issues. The latest approaches aim to resolve these issues by integrating blockchain technology with IoT. Despite these challenges, innovative solutions are being explored to merge blockchain technology with IoT to improve security and privacy in IoT-driven healthcare systems. Blockchain has the potential to revolutionise IoT-based healthcare solutions \[[16](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_16)\]. The decentralised and unchangeable ledger of blockchain guarantees the security and imperviousness of health data transferred and stored via IoT devices. 113To protect their privacy and ensure that only authorised parties see their data, patients can securely and openly give and withdraw access to their health records.

### 9.4.2 Enhanced Interoperability and Data Integrity

Advancements in information technology and blockchain technology have transformed the healthcare industry, enabling miniaturised sensors for monitoring vital signs and portable health devices for self-health tracking. However, inconsistent security policies and access control structures hinder these advancements. Blockchain-based platform for monitoring patient vital signs, utilising Hyperledger Fabric and Libelium e-Health toolkit. This innovative approach provides patients with an immutable history log and global access to medical information, enhancing data security and user comfort in medical sharing \[[17](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_17)\]. Blockchain enhances medical record integrity by creating an immutable patient data history, reducing errors due to corrupted or altered data, and ensuring accurate diagnosis and treatment. Blockchain technology enhances clinical trial credibility by securely collecting and verifying data from IoT devices, reducing the risk of manipulation and fraud.

### 9.4.3 Effective Clinical Trials and Investigations

Blockchain technology enables a transparent, traceable record of the entire supply chain, enhancing the management of medical supplies and pharmaceutical authenticity. IoT devices in clinical trials collect patients’ health data in real-time, utilising blockchain technology for secure recording, efficient monitoring, and prompt protocol modifications. The detailed taxonomy of blockchain technology in clinical trials highlights its potential for comprehensive, secure, and transparent coverage. It also addresses open research challenges in technical, security, and organisational aspects. Blockchain technology can securely and transparently handle clinical trials but faces technical, security, and organisational challenges \[[18](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_18)\]. Blockchain technology enables real-time health data collection from clinical trial patients, enhancing efficiency and facilitating quicker protocol adjustments.

### 9.4.4 Supply Chain Management

Integrating blockchain with the IoT can develop a smart health supply chain management system. In addition to preventing forged medications and harm to medical components reported, this strategy ensures authentication, lowers costs, and monitors the status of products during their shipment from manufacturers to end-users. It also offers decentralised tracking and tracing of medicinal items, privacy, security, trust, and visibility. A smart supply chain requires a secure infrastructure to ensure the timely and accurate delivery of goods to the right person. At the same time, IoT devices collect real-time health data for supply chain network control, but incomplete information and conflicting stakeholder behaviour make tracking medical components challenging \[[19](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_19)\]. Blockchain ensures patient safety by preventing the entry of counterfeit medications into the market by tracking every stage of the supply chain on an immutable ledger.

114 

### 9.4.5 Automated Smart Contracts

Insurance claim handling can be automated with smart contracts, which lowers administrative costs and expedites claim settlements. This can be especially helpful in managing the vast amounts of information that IoT healthcare equipment generates. The smart contract is a section of programming that is added to a blockchain and starts running automatically when certain requirements are satisfied. Blockchain is a distributed, mutual record where exchanges are meticulously documented and linked to each other to give the whole provenance or history of the benefit. Smart contracts apply to a number of businesses, such as financial services and banking, wherever they enable numerous investors to offer finances to multiple borrowers at the same terms. Smart contracts are used in the energy industry to distribute energy generally. The Digital Right Management System allows the entertainment sector to handle movie and music rights more easily through the use of smart contracts \[[20](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_20)\]. Insurance claim handling can be automated with smart contracts, which lowers administrative costs and expedites claim settlements. This can be especially helpful in managing the vast amounts of information that IoT healthcare equipment generates. Automatic payments for medical services are made under predetermined guidelines. Smart contracts have the ability to initiate payments, for example, when a patient’s IoT device signals that a health check-up is finished.

### 9.4.6 Improved Telemedicine Patient Monitoring

Blockchain can help IoT devices monitor patients remotely by guaranteeing that the data collected is reliable and unchangeable. This is necessary to give patients trustworthy care outside of conventional hospital settings. Medical IoT devices collect bio-signals for personalised healthcare, enhancing individual health-related data management. The Individual Health Records System ensures privacy, individual control, data integration, safety, and flexibility. Blockchain technology is emerging as a potential selection aimed at managing health information. The healthcare industry is transitioning from a hospital-centric to a person-centric approach, promoting national engagement in disease prevention and early detection \[[21](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_21)\]. Thanks to blockchain technology, paramedics and emergency services can instantly access a patient’s medical information in an emergency, which can be essential for prompt and efficient treatment.

### 9.4.7 Decentralised Health Information

Healthcare organisations are adopting innovative technologies to improve patient and provider control over health information. Blockchain technology offers a decentralised structure for smooth international services and enables medical professionals to record and oversee patient care without a central authority. However, privacy concerns and competitive edge concerns prevent institutional adoption. Cloud-based systems and blockchain technology enhance record security, providing patients with a dedicated area for organising and safeguarding their health records and allowing access at any time and from multiple facilities 115\[[22](#C_014_c9.xhtml#r9_22)\]. Blockchain enables the construction of decentralised medical records that people may take to other institutions and healthcare providers. This guarantees full, up-to-date medical histories and continuity of service.

## 9.5 Conclusion

Blockchain technology, originally developed for Bitcoin, has gained considerable recognition since its inception. This chapter examines several kinds of blockchain technology and their uses in various fields, including IoT-based healthcare. Applications in the music business, financial services, IoT, edge computing, smart grids, cybersecurity, and healthcare have all expressed great interest in it due to its potential. Blockchain integration improves interoperability, security, and efficiency in IoT-based healthcare systems, propelling digital transformation and bettering patients’ health. Patients always wish to protect their medical records and not give them to any healthcare organisation, whether through phones or other technological means. Today’s research on IoT-based healthcare offers a novel way to employ IoT resources that are underutilised safely and securely using blockchain technology.

# 10 Blockchain-Driven Supply Chain Management in Industry 5.0 Enhancing Efficiency and Sustainability

Harsh Raj, Garima Jain, Sanat Jain, Prafull Farswal, Vartika Singh, and Rajasekaran Selvaraju

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-13](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-13)

## 10.1 Introduction

The objective of the forthcoming fifth industrial revolution, or Industry 5.0, is to integrate human creativity with the accuracy, velocity, and intelligence of robotics. It is reinstating individuals to the manufacturing floor. Potential advantages of Industry 5.0 include advancements in cloud manufacturing, intelligent healthcare, manufacturing production, and supply chain management. There are various supporting approaches that help in the functioning of Industry 5.0. Blockchain technology is one of them \[[1](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_1)\].

Industry 5.0 has been derived from Industry 4.0. The shift from Industry 4.0 to Industry 5.0 is not too complex, but blockchain technology is seen to play a vital role in various sectors. In Industry 4.0, blockchain technology was used to make supply chains more secure and transparent \[[2](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_2)\]. In Industry 5.0, this is taken to the next level. For example, it facilitates the assurance of high-quality, genuine products. It allows companies to place custom orders through special apps, and transactions are managed using smart contracts. This makes things such as payments and logistics much smoother and cost-effective. In manufacturing, blockchain plays a massive role. From procuring raw materials and using automated processes in manufacturing to delivering the finished product, blockchain helps ensure that operations are efficient, secure, and transparent \[[3](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_3)\]. It aids in storing records of students in such a manner that it is secure, hard to modify by unauthorised sources, and easy to share.

Furthermore, it caters to innovative experiences such as remote labs and holographic teaching. For the Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV)/drone operations and disaster management domains, blockchain will keep sensitive data safe while 119ensuring on-time and secure communications in drone networks or vehicle systems. Blockchain is, in simple words, one of the most imperative tools moving into Industry 5.0 to make our industries more secure, efficient, and connected. Below is [Table 10.1](#C_015_c10.xhtml#t10_1), which uses various parameters to differentiate between Industry 4.0 and Industry 5.0. Think of blockchain as a super-secure, worldwide digital ledger. This is immutable, unlike regular databases, and lots of people can see it. The result of this is that an organisation cannot control it. Thus, if you make a transaction, then the system will check that, and everybody can see what happened. In light of the emergence of Industry 5.0 and blockchain technology, this study aims to achieve the following objectives:

Table 10.1 Transition from Industry 4.0 and Industry 5.0 \[[4](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_4)\] [Return to text.⏎](#C_015_c10.xhtml#t10_1b)

 
| Aspect || Industry 4.0 (Human-Out-of-the-Loop) || Industry 5.0 (Human-Back-into-the-Loop) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Focus || Technology-driven: Automation, smart factories, cyber-physical systems, and machine autonomy || Value-driven: Human-centricity, sustainability, resilience, and well-being of workers and society |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Role of human || Humans are largely removed from industrial processes; systems and robots handle most tasks autonomously. || Humans play a significant role in collaboration with robots and systems, focusing on human–machine cooperation. |
| Technology || Dominated by AI, IoT, cloud computing, machine learning, and cognitive computing. || Integrates weak AI for human control, focusing on transparent human–machine cooperation. |
| Communication || Machine-to-machine communication || Human–machine interaction with collaborative robots |
| Decision-making || Autonomous decision-making by machines || Humans participate in critical decision-making alongside machines |
| Goal || Efficiency and productivity, data-driven automation || Human-centric, sustainable, and resilient industrial processes that align with societal and ecological needs |
| Impact on jobs || Some job loss due to automation and autonomous systems || Industry aims to create new jobs by incorporating humans into more collaborative, innovative, and decision-making processes. |
| Ethical considerations || Largely ignored or secondary, “Black Box AI” with little human control || Focus on ethical decisions and fostering transparent human–machine cooperation, especially during system failures or degraded modes. |

*   **RO1:** To analyse the principal benefits of blockchain within the supply chain management domain in Industry 5.0.
*   **RO2:** To identify and assess the essential enablers of blockchain adoption for supply chain management in Industry 5.0.

120 

## 10.2 Literature Review

Innovations or processes enhancing efficiency through blockchain technologies or processes that augment the efficiency of systems for supply chain management in Industry 5.0 and the role of blockchain therein.

Achieving this involves several technologies and processes, with the Internet of Things (IoT) being particularly significant. The IoT constitutes an extensive network of intelligent devices—gadgets, vehicles, and structures—all interconnected via the Internet. These gadgets possess electronics, software, and sensors that enable data collection and sharing. This link facilitates remote monitoring and control, enhancing overall efficiency. Both real and virtual entities within the IoT ecosystem have unique characteristics that allow them to interconnect and become part of a larger information network. Various technologies, including barcodes, wireless sensor networks, smart sensing, Near Field Communication (NFC), Radio Frequency Identification (RFID), low-energy cloud computing, and wireless communications, play essential roles in IoT \[[5](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_5)\].

The IoT is revolutionising different industries by bringing smart applications like smart homes, cities, self-driving cars, and wearable devices. IoT’s role in the supply chain system is to improve inventory management through technologies like RFID and wireless networks for a real-time tracking and automation system. Industry 4.0 brings new information into inventory models—a movement from supplier-driven production to customer-demand-driven. It would involve time-varying production rates as well as policies regarding inventory replenishments in a manner that fully enables the warehouse to prepare and set up for changed demand in a highly effective manner \[[6](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_6)\].

Cooperation is the new gold standard for human-smart system relationships in the age of Industry 5.0, which includes robots. When it comes to improving this partnership, the IoT is king. Envision a smart factory where machines share data about manufacturing processes and make choices in real time through IoT communication. This connectivity boosts efficiency because machines can work together seamlessly. It also improves the operations that take place in the warehouse, where one can calculate the fastest route, which, in turn, speeds up product delivery and improves inventory management. Industry 5.0 places more emphasis on a balanced interaction between humans and machines, as compared with Industry 4.0, which aims to minimise human involvement. This version allows humans to take up creative roles while robots handle repetitive tasks. The combination of both further improves production quality. Industry 5.0 uses the IoT in such a way that industries transform into a collaborative environment, where combining human creativity with the capabilities of robots creates more agile and scalable operations \[[7](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_7)\].

The IoT network is much more strengthened and secure by combining new cutting-edge technology with it. To better understand the implications of blockchain, think of it as an extra safeguard that prohibits unauthorised parties from intercepting data transmissions between smart home devices. Blockchain technology enables simple and highly secure communication between various devices, like thermometers, refrigerators, and webcams. Distributed ledger technology (blockchain) makes it far more difficult to corrupt or manipulate the system than storing data in a single 121vulnerable area \[[8](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_8)\]. Secure digital commitments made across several devices enable smart contracts to facilitate interactions between parties. Many vulnerabilities exist in traditional security systems because they rely on a single authority. Contracts with intelligence, however, are reliable, independently executed, and impenetrable. They ensure that only authorised devices have access to the correct information, thereby maintaining the integrity of both the data and the overall IoT system. Therefore, smart contracts on blockchain enhance IoT security without requiring a complete overhaul of existing systems \[[9](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_9)\]. This technology also empowers IoT devices to process transactions, making it incredibly useful for autonomous machines that need to make quick, secure payments for services \[[10](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_10)\].

It is evident from the above that blockchain records each entry of data collected from IoT on a distributed ledger, thus verifying the integrity of IoT data. This transparent recording, making it tamper-proof, will increase the level of reliance among stakeholders. Predictive analytics can rely on this secure data to not only give forecasts in demand patterns but also detect anomalies in operation, such as failures of machinery or failure of the supply chain and allocate resources optimally. Integrate blockchain’s security with the analytical capabilities of artificial intelligence (AI) to unlock a whole new avenue of making more data-driven decisions by businesses with more confidence for improving both the efficiency and the ethical practices of global supply chains \[[11](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_11)\].

Information sharing is critical for businesses in supply chain systems to thrive in the context of Industry 5.0. Technological advancements have made it easier to share data across different levels of the chain, enhancing coordination and cooperation among suppliers, producers, retailers, and distributors. Without sufficient information sharing, organisations cannot operate effectively \[[12](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_12)\]. It can increase a company’s competitive advantage and improve responsiveness to market changes while ensuring better alignment of products with consumer demand. Although sharing information has associated costs, the benefits, particularly through advancements in Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) and web-based systems, are significant. Therefore, data sharing in the supply chain is a key factor in achieving better coordination, integration, and efficiency throughout the entire supply chain. Blockchain technology enforces trust and secures shared data by storing information in encrypted form.

It gives data owners complete control over who can access their information and under what conditions. The transparency and immutability of blockchain records also instil trust among users, making it a powerful solution for industries dealing with high volumes of sensitive data \[[13](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_13)\]. Thus, blockchain emerges as a vital component for ensuring secure transactions and protecting personal and sensitive information. Traditional methods of verifying the authenticity of shared documents often require time-consuming offline processes involving contacting issuing authorities. The dispersed and irreversible characteristics of blockchain computing resolve this issue by offering a visible, collective ledger of activities that can promptly authenticate the validity of shared information. Blockchain’s cryptographic strength prevents unauthorised entities from editing records, further enhancing security \[[14](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_14)\].

Blockchain ensures that ethical sourcing is maintained in a global supply chain by providing transparent and immutable records of every step in the sourcing and 122production process. Regarding the certifications for ethically sourced materials, blockchain authenticates the certifications that comply with fair trade or standards of sustainable practices. Traceability gives a confident assessment of the origins of products both by consumers and companies, holding suppliers accountable to ethical and environmental standards. This will create a better, responsible, and transparent supply chain ecosystem because blockchain eliminates and prevents fraud and risks of unethical conduct within the supply chain \[[15](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_15)\].

Because of the mentioned features, blockchain computing is characterised as a transformational solution for different sectors, especially for the health and energy sectors. Specifically, in the health sector, mainly in oncology, blockchain can significantly enhance data sharing with security, personalised care, and medical research through the features of transparency, traceability, and immutability. However, it should be noted that no amount of technology can protect data privacy or security and can be merged with other cryptographic methods. Legal, social, and technological barriers to adoption have kept large-scale real-world deployment at bay thus far. However, future research can overcome all of these through the development of privacy-preserving systems that will be interoperable with other systems and that are compliant with international regulations \[[16](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_16)\].

In the energy area, blockchain is driving change from centralised to decentralised energy systems, focusing currently on smart grids, microgrids, and peer-to-peer energy markets. China has led the basic blockchain research for energy; however, most real-world applications arise in developed countries. Future direction and research and development in this sector should be directed at integrating the two sectors so that basic research and practical applications are bridged \[[17](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_17)\].

Emerging trends such as quantum blockchain and the addition of AI in blockchain are going to revolutionise Industry 5.0, with both aspects related to decentralised systems and human-centric production. Quantum AI can greatly scale up efficiencies in blockchain by speeding up transaction validation and maintaining data safety while coupling AI algorithms with the computational power that comes from quantum computing. This integration will ensure efficient healthcare, financial services, and supply chain management through better prediction and analysis, as well as fraud detection. These technologies will look towards improving automation and decision-making to make industrial solutions sustainable and intelligent when applied in Industry 5.0. For this context, the combination of blockchain systems aligns with the requirement of Industry 5.0, which aims to improve the way humans and machines work together, as well as make manufacturing efficient. [Figure 10.1](#C_015_c10.xhtml#f10_1) shows the framework of the proposed methodology.

Figure 10.1 Proposed framework. [Return to text.⏎](#C_015_c10.xhtml#f10_1b)

## 10.3 Issues Industries May Face in Industry 5.0

*   **Security:** In this context, increasing digitalisation and connectivity through the IoT increases the risk of cyberattacks against factories and industrial processes. This has made security issues a top priority in Industry 5.0. Such attacks can cause humongous financial losses. Security breaches have the potential to disrupt important industrial productions, delivery, and customer satisfaction timelines. To overcome this, secure identity management, 123constant system monitoring, and advanced encryption are essential security attributes. To improve the reliability of digital ecosystems and prevent unauthorised access, businesses can use blockchain technology in conjunction with the IoT to securely exchange data with one another \[[18](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_18)\].
*   **Privacy:** Industry 5.0 aims to meet human needs through customised product production, relying heavily on user data to achieve such personalisation. Consequently, securing user data is vital. If user data falls into unauthorised hands, it can be exploited for identity theft, financial fraud, or unauthorised surveillance. As data sharing increases among multiple parties, controlling access becomes increasingly complex. A lack of control can easily lead to misuse, highlighting the need for a privacy-by-design approach, where privacy is embedded in the technology and business processes to strengthen overall system security. The unique feature of blockchain lies in its decentralisation, which grants individuals control over their data. By limiting access to certain users in accordance with permissions specified in intelligent agreements, blockchain technology helps participants more effectively handle their personal information \[[18](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_18)\].
*   **Data Sharing:** Despite its importance in Industry 5.0, data sharing does have some drawbacks. Complicating factors in information sharing include a lack of trust between organisations and different policies and standards. As a result, it is critical to establish an ecosystem that promotes data sharing among different players. Data ownership concerns and the fear of competitors gaining access to sensitive information can discourage organisations from sharing their data.
*   **Centralisation:** This parameter appears to be one major obstacle that could stop Industry 5.0 from achieving its goal. This exposes them to cyber-attacks and malfunctions. The compromise of centralised structures could lead to serious consequences not just for the organisation but for everyone who is a part of the supply chain. Also, flexibility and response are two terms that are crucial requirements for Industry 5.0, where consumer expectations and marketplace circumstances are always changing. ## 10.4 Role of Blockchain in Addressing the Challenges of Industry 5.0

It is important to address the potential challenges that all the organisations that are a part of some supply chain may encounter as Industry 5.0 develops. Blockchain technology is key to solving these problems. If exploited correctly, it has great potential to facilitate the safe integration of new technology, thereby fostering digital transformation in Industry 5.0. The decentralised, transparent, and secure nature of blockchain technology makes it ideal for solving many problems that are seething in Industry 5.0, including the issues that are related to data sharing, privacy, and centralisation. For example, blockchain improves reliability by spreading data that has to be stored across a decentralised network, eliminating single points of failure. Crucial for preserving the authenticity of the supply chain, the invincibility of the data on a blockchain guarantees that information remains tamper-proof.

The use of effective encryption techniques and access controls that are based on smart contracts further enhances blockchain’s privacy features. It gives users more control over their personal information by allowing them to set specific permission for data access. This control helps build trust between participants, allowing organisations and people on a personal level to share information while protecting their privacy. Blockchain removes common issues that occur while working together by securely sharing data between organisations. The fact that it could hold records permanently and also provide a shared view makes it easier for stakeholders to trust each other. This is particularly helpful in handling supply chains because many people rely on accurate data to make decisions.

Blockchain and gamification blend in a perfect synergy, which could be drawn on the supply chains. The stakeholders involved in the supply chain can be motivated to collaborate to increase involvement and transparency using blockchain-based tokens or rewards. For instance, participants receive tokens once they have met 125their particular performance markers or in case of timely deliveries, and the blockchain will handle secure, immutable records of their progress. This thereby integrates gamification through blockchain: it motivates stakeholders and strengthens accountability and trust by reducing the problem of transparency to decentralised supply chains \[[19](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_19)\].

Blockchain technology enables every user to have control over their own data in the chain and contribute to data sovereignty. The decentralised and open nature of the ledger used in blockchain ensures that data is not controlled by a single entity but distributed across a highly secure network. This allows individuals to manage access to their private data through the use of smart contracts, where they can determine the type of people allowed to access or use their information and the conditions under which such people should access them. Data is also cryptographically locked down to trace the movement along the supply chain while allowing users to maintain their privacy. This allows users to trust that personal data can be accessed only by authorised parties, thereby improving transparency without taking away user control.

The availability of the decentralised nature of blockchain will give individuals all sovereignty over data, and by doing this, it will minimise its reliance on third parties that were central to the risks of misuse of data \[[20](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_20),[21](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_21)\]. Blockchain provides privacy because of the transparent and trustworthy chain of information. [Figure 10.3](#C_015_c10.xhtml#f10_3) shows the research in Industry 5.0.

![Figure 10.3 Analysis of opportunities, constraints, and prospects for future research in industry 5.0. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig10_03.jpg)

Figure 10.3 Analysis of opportunities, constraints, and prospects for future research in industry 5.0. [Return to text.⏎](#C_015_c10.xhtml#f10_3b)

The stored data in the blockchain are secure, and third-party access is only possible with proper permissions and, thus, completely private \[[22](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_22)\]. One of its excellent features is its transparency and reliability, which makes it an apt platform for shared data across several systems. This offers secure, efficient data exchange in business, government, and logistics systems. Hence, it acts as a very trustable method for any real-time or other kind of data-sharing process. Blockchain is a technology through which peer-to-peer networks can be created. Through these remarkably diverse production units, machines, devices, or even a single manufacturer, they can be connected without any intermediaries or centralised control. It is further in a position to provide end-to-end transparency across the supply chain and thus allow 126production units to trace raw materials, components, and finished products. This way, transparency gives each unit involved in the production process a clear view of the whole supply chain, thus enhancing coordination and trust \[[23](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_23)\]. It also provides real-time inventory tracking, where firms can trace the flow of merchandise within the supply chain. This transparency ensures that levels of inventory are current and accurate, thereby averting situations wherein businesses have a stockout or overstock situation \[[24](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_24)\].

## 10.5 Issues with the Implication of Blockchain Technology

One of the key drawbacks of blockchain technology, for instance, is high energy consumption in Proof of Work models. In this model, miners must rely on solving extremely resource-hungry cryptographical puzzles to reach a consensus. As a result, electricity usage is quite high and provokes more environment-related concerns. The network, of course, cannot scale indefinitely in its ability to deal with the number of transactions since the amount of time and computational power necessary to validate will increase. Other limitations include regulatory challenges and interoperability issues among the many blockchain networks that exist in the world today \[[25](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_25)\]. Further, the integration of blockchain technology with legacy systems also poses a big challenge, including architectural incompatibility due to the centralised nature of legacy systems versus the decentralised nature of blockchain. Data synchronisation is complex, as legacy systems often use different formats that must be aligned with the blockchain’s structure. The cost and complexity of modifying outdated infrastructure can be prohibitive, while scalability and transaction speed limitations of blockchain may not meet the demands of high-volume legacy systems. Concerns regarding security and privacy emerge from blockchain’s transparency, potentially conflicting with the privacy standards of legacy systems \[[26](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_26)\].

To address the difficulty of seamless interoperability, standardisation of data exchange protocols is necessary to facilitate successful information transfer among diverse platforms. Legacy systems can connect to the blockchain using Application Programming Interfaces (APIs), thereby reconciling architectural discrepancies to facilitate data exchange in a safe environment. A decentralised methodology, coupled with cryptographic protections, can enhance data privacy and integrity. Addressing stakeholder integration and scalability is essential for management to attain efficient and safe blockchain interoperability throughout the network \[[27](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_27)\].

## 10.6 Case Investigations of Enterprises Effectively Incorporating Blockchain Technology to Enhance Their Supply Chain

1.  **Walmart:** Walmart has partnered with IBM to incorporate blockchain technology into its supply chain to enhance the monitoring of food goods’ provenance. Using IBM’s Food Trust blockchain, Walmart can trace a 127product in seconds, from the farm to the store shelf, improving transparency, security, and productivity. As a result, companies may respond more rapidly to food recalls, which helps to identify and remove any tainted products swiftly \[[8](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_8)\].
2.  **De Beers (Tracr):** To monitor the movement of diamonds across their supply chain, De Beers, the diamond corporation, introduced a blockchain platform named Tracr. This promotes the ethical sourcing of diamonds and helps stop the circulation of diamonds that have been in conflict (often called “blood diamonds”). According to Ahmed and Abdurrahman \[[9](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_9)\], blockchain technology enables more openness in monitoring the whole lifecycle of a diamond, from mining to storage.
3.  **Maersk (TradeLens):** With the help of IBM’s TradeLens platform, Maersk—one of the world’s leading shipping companies—has integrated blockchain technology. By digitalising and simplifying logistics and shipping paperwork, this technology enables efficient worldwide trade. Zhan and Wang \[[20](#C_015_c10.xhtml#r10_20)\] state that TradeLens employs blockchain technology to provide real-time shipping transaction updates, which reduces paperwork, enhances supply chain transparency, and minimises fraud.

## 10.7 Conclusion

Industry 5.0 signifies the latest industrial revolution centred on the collaboration between human ingenuity and machine productivity. It was founded on Industry 4.0, with the incorporation of blockchain technology to enhance security and transparency in supply chains. Industry 5.0 utilises blockchain technology to guarantee the authenticity and superior quality of products. It also works on a distributed ledger system, which is used to remove middlemen such as banks by creating a secure record of transactions. When it comes to the IoT, data interchange, inventory management, and decision-making processes, this technology is vital for many different types of enterprises. Here, the term “Internet of Things” refers to a system of interconnected Internet-enabled smart devices. It is important in Industry 5.0 because it allows remote monitoring and control. Integrating blockchain with IoT would enhance both reliability and security across these systems, allowing secure communication between sensors. Blockchain also ensures secure data sharing through encryption and access control, which is vital for efficient supply chain management.

The handling of inventory will become more efficient using blockchain as it transforms supply chain logistics. Furthermore, the platform’s transparency and immutability facilitate trustless collaboration, which in turn benefits the decision-making process. The primary component of blockchain in the new era of Industry is the integration of the IoT with the standards for interoperability techniques such as Proof of Stake and Proof of Work. An essential need in Industry 5.0, given the massive amounts of data produced by IoT devices, this integration guarantees the security of information, integrity, and overall automated transactions via smart contracts among devices, encouraging smooth and secure operations.

# 11 Healthcare Security and Privacy Concerns in the Blockchain Network A Review

KM Manisha and J Sathish Kumar

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-15](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-15)

## 11.1 Introduction

In Machine Learning (ML)/Deep Learning (DL)-based healthcare applications, there is an absence of actual medical datasets. Doctors and healthcare providers find it challenging to disclose sensitive data for the purpose of developing strong and efficient machine learning and deep learning models that can quickly and accurately diagnose diseases, which is why these medical datasets remain private. In light of the exponential growth of healthcare data and the growing desire for data-driven medical innovations, the ethical and safe sharing of private patient data is a critical concern. A fine balance must be struck by healthcare organisations, researchers, and patients between the critical requirement to protect patient privacy and data security and the usefulness of aggregated medical data for scientific advancement.

The problem has sparked the development of cutting-edge technologies that have the potential to change the way that healthcare data is shared completely. When multiple medical systems are used, there is a risk to patient information security, privacy, and accessibility. If security is compromised, there is also a risk to availability, data integrity, and privacy. Hackers can use medical records to find prescriptions and commit fraud or identity theft by ordering medications online. They can also use extortion and blackmail to force victims to declare private medical information.

Finally, there is a risk to the mobility, privacy, and security of IoMT-enabled health tools such as fitness trackers \[[1](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_1)\]. Single points of failure, interoperability problems, and concerns about data privacy and security are some of the drawbacks of centralised data-sharing systems. Decentralised data sharing, which disperses data among numerous nodes or peers without the need for a central authority or middleman, has emerged as a viable solution to these problems. Decentralised data sharing also has a number of advantages, including better data ownership and control, higher privacy and security, and increased responsibility and openness \[[2](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_2)\].

*   **Blockchain:** Blockchain is an immutable, decentralised, append-only public ledger. The blockchain is a public ledger, as anyone can participate in the transaction, and every node will keep a copy of it and maintain its copy. 134Everybody acts as a node and processes data locally; there is no central database to hold the chain’s data. This is called decentralisation \[[3](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_3)\]. Block is a container data structure that contains a series of transactions. Each block is linked together through its hash values. By using cryptographic hashes to link blocks together, a blockchain’s integrity is preserved while guaranteeing decentralisation and tamper resistance. A block is divided into two parts—block header and block body. Block headers contain version, timestamp, hash, difficulty level, nonce, and Merkle root. Versions define the version of the block structure. The amount of time to create a block is called a timestamp. A hash function takes input of any length and produces fixed-length output.

The difficulty level indicates the level of complexity and processing power needed to mine the network. Nonce is a counter used for the proof of work algorithm \[[4](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_4)\]. The Merkle root serves as a blockchain’s unique identifier for each transaction within a block. The block body contains the list of transactions. Larger blocks can help in processing a large number of transactions in one go. However, verification and propagation take longer. Blockchains start with a single block, called Block 0 or the Genesis Block. All other blocks are appended to this block, which acts as the ancestor to whom all other blocks may practically trace their genealogy because each block references the one before it. A cryptographic algorithm is used to link blocks, and a consensus architecture is employed to control and digitally sign transactions. New blocks are added to the network, carrying new information. Blockchain makes sure that everyone always sees the blockchain from the same perspective so that transparency is maintained. Since the information is open to all, anyone can confirm and validate it \[[5](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_5)\].

Blockchain enables immutable transactions, consensus, verification and validation. It is easily shared among several nodes without the requirement for any central authority because of its decentralised nature. The benefits of decentralised sharing of data include accountability, openness, validation and verification. Blockchain is more secure due to its immutability. Federated learning (FL) combined with blockchain offers a fresh method for decentralised data exchange. Consensus algorithms underpin blockchain’s decentralised nature by ensuring that all users may trust the system without the need for a central authority. Multiple entities are involved in the management of blockchain. Such entities include nodes, validators, transactions, blocks, consensus, and service providers. Every entity has contributed to the decentralisation, security, functionality, and governance of the network. Blockchains are inherently decentralised networks comprised of multiple individuals who behave according to their available knowledge and incentives \[[6](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_6)\].

Consensus is attained when a single state is accepted by a large majority of the agents that comprise the network. The blockchain’s security and integrity are upheld via a consensus algorithm. A consensus algorithm is a technique that enables all nodes in a distributed network to agree upon a single reality interpretation \[[7](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_7)\]. Consensus methods ensure uniform data viewing for all blockchain network participants and transaction validation in a secure, untrusted environment. If an algorithm meets both of the following two requirements, that is, agreement and termination, 135it is said to reach a consensus. In agreement, all non-faulty nodes choose the same output value. In termination, each non-faulty node selects an output value at some time \[[8](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_8)\]. Various consensus algorithms are Proof of Work, Proof of Stake, Proof of History, Proof of Activity, proof of authority, Proof of Burn, and Proof of Capacity. The choice of algorithm can have a significant impact on the network’s scalability, security, and performance \[[9](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_9)\].

### 11.1.1 Blockchain Is Divided into Four Parts-Public, Private, Hybrid and Consortium Which Are Explained Below

*   **Public Blockchain:** It is also called the permissionless blockchain. Anyone can join and conduct operations without needing any permission from a central authority. Its characteristics include decentralisation, immutability, and transparency. Its disadvantages are scalability issues, more energy consumption and vulnerability to attacks. For example, Bitcoin and Ethereum.
*   **Private Blockchain:** It is also called permissioned blockchain. Work is conducted within a closed network. When developing the private blockchain application, the relevant authorities and chain developers selected the entities for this network. There are some limitations on who can take part in validations and transactions. Its characteristics are high performance due to fewer nodes, high throughput, scalability can be increased, speed is fast, etc. Its limitation is centralised, limited network effects, interoperability issues, etc. \[[10](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_10)\]. For example, Hyperledger.
*   **Hybrid Blockchain:** This technology blends public and private blockchain with permission-based and permissionless systems. It is designed to remove all the disadvantages of both blockchains. Members have the authority to determine who is allowed to use the blockchain and whose transactions are visible to the public. It is highly flexible while preserving integrity, security, and transparency. Its advantages are high transaction speed, reduced transaction cost, flexible infrastructure, etc. For example, Energy Web Foundation, Dragonchain, and R3.
*   **Consortium Blockchain:** Also known as federated blockchain. It is a type of private blockchain where the consensus process is controlled by a pre-selected group of nodes or organisations \[[11](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_11)\]. Consortium blockchains are operated and maintained by several groups or entities. Consortium blockchains are useful for use cases where several reliable and well-known parties need to work together on a similar platform because they strike a compromise between control and decentralisation \[[12](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_12)\].
*   Data cannot be altered once it is written on a blockchain due to immutability. Due to the decentralised structure of the blockchain network, a breach on a single node does not compromise the system. By facilitating standardised, secure, and efficient data transfer between diverse healthcare systems and providers, blockchain solves scalability and interoperability issues. The decentralised structure of blockchain eliminates single points of failure \[[3](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_3)\]. Blockchain can be used in healthcare to provide security and privacy to healthcare data and provide transparency to the healthcare.

136 

### 11.1.2 Advantages of Blockchain in Healthcare

*   **Collaboration and Information Exchange:** Blockchain can increase interoperability by reducing the standard exchange of health data between multiple systems and organisations. Authorised healthcare provider keeps and access the full patient record, guaranteeing that all pertinent data is available for improved care coordination.
*   **Better Privacy and Security for Data:** Improved data privacy and security in the healthcare industry is essential for safeguarding private information, building patient trust, averting data breaches, and guaranteeing legal compliance. It not only safeguards patient data but also improves the general efficacy, efficiency, and reputation of healthcare providers, opening the door to better patient care and innovative medical practices since blockchain uses immutability to secure data.
*   **Openness and Trackability:** Due to its decentralised nature, data is available to all nodes. So, it provides openness and trackability \[[13](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_13)\]. Every transaction is documented on the blockchain, which maintains a public, auditable record of all data access and modification operations. Blockchain technology ensures transparency and traceability throughout the pharmaceutical and medical supply lifecycle, from manufacturing to patient delivery.
*   **Effectiveness and Economic Downsizing:** Smart contracts can be used to automate administrative tasks, including billing, processing claims, and managing patient records, which can save on overhead and administrative workloads. Blockchain can improve the efficiency of healthcare operations by streamlining a number of processes and reducing the number of delays.
*   **Improved Studies and Clinical Investigations:** For regulatory compliance and public trust, it is essential to guarantee the integrity of clinical trial data. Trial data can be safely recorded and verified using blockchain. Blockchain technology may accelerate the processes of recruiting and obtaining consent from clinical trial participants, ensuring efficiency and transparency.
*   **Preventing Fraud:** Blockchain technology can help in the identification and verification of patients and providers, decreasing the possibility of fraud in healthcare billing and services. The risk of counterfeit drugs can be decreased by tracking pharmaceuticals on the blockchain and verifying their validity.
*   **Empowerment of Patients:** Patients own and manage their health data, deciding when and to whom it can be accessed. Increased patient involvement and engagement in their care can result from having more control over their health data.
*   **Management of Population Health and Public Health:** Blockchain technology has the ability to instantly compile health data from several sources, offering insightful information for public health response and monitoring. While preserving individual privacy, blockchain technology can make it easier to use anonymised health data for research and population health studies.
*   137**Security of Medical Equipment:** Blockchain technology can be used to safely record and transfer medical device data, guaranteeing its integrity and security. Blockchain technology can be used to track and guarantee that medical devices are adhering to regulations.

### 11.1.3 Privacy and Security Concerns in Healthcare

Security and privacy are essential in the healthcare sector to protect sensitive information, build trust, and stop fraud and identity theft. Healthcare providers collect and keep vast amounts of sensitive data, including financial records, medical histories, and personally identifiable information. Safeguarding these data is necessary to ensure the confidentiality of patients. The reputations of healthcare providers are determined by their policies on the safeguarding of patient data. Breaches can severely damage relationships and trust in the workplace. In the healthcare sector, blockchain technology enhances data security, privacy, interoperability, and sharing. The privacy, security, and accessibility of patient information are all compromised when numerous medical systems are employed; apart from these, privacy and security have some disadvantages, like data leakage, complexity, possible delays in data access, and difficulties in finding a balance between security and use. The potential consequences of a security issue include availability, data integrity, and privacy \[[14](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_14)\].

Healthcare systems are still vulnerable to sophisticated cyberattacks like ransomware, advanced persistent threats and insider threats, even with strong security measures in place. Vulnerabilities that are not yet patched or newly identified can still be exploited. Ransomware attacks have the ability to encrypt important data, halting medical systems and affecting patient care. There is no assurance that access will be fully restored or that the data hasn’t been altered even after attackers demand substantial ransoms to be paid in exchange for allowing access to be restored \[[15](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_15)\]. A replay attack is used to repeat the same request multiple times and pretend like a new request. It can be prevented by using nonce, timestamp, token expiration, encryption, etc.

## 11.2 Problems in Healthcare

### 11.2.1 Data Sharing Between Hospitals

Inadequate implementation of access control measures could lead to unauthorised access to private patient information. Employees or insiders who have been given access to examine data may misuse it, such as accessing the data modify the data, whether intentionally or accidentally, for their benefit. Due to the frequent compartmentalisation of healthcare data across multiple systems and organisations, information sharing is difficult and secure. Inconsistent data formats and standards may make it more difficult to integrate and exchange secure data \[[16](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_16)\]. When patient data is shared between hospitals for better diagnosis, a patient’s data is encrypted using the receiver’s public key and decrypted using the receiver’s private key. After that, digital signature is used to identify users and protect information in digital messages. The sender signs the data using his private key, and the receiver authenticates using the sender’s public key \[[17](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_17)\]. In this way, data is protected from unauthorised access and changes.

138**The above problem can be addressed as follows:**

When data is shared from Hospital A to Hospital B, a malicious third party, such as Hospital C, is an unauthorised entity. It can attack the data, compromising system security, as shown in [Figure 11.1](#C_017_c11.xhtml#f11_1). The following are the possible attacks:

![Figure 11.1 Data sharing in healthcare. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig11_01.jpg)

Figure 11.1 Data sharing in healthcare. [Return to text.⏎](#C_017_c11.xhtml#f11_1b)

![Figure 11.2 Healthcare system architecture. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig11_02.jpg)

Figure 11.2 Healthcare system architecture. [Return to text.⏎](#C_017_c11.xhtml#f11_2b)

*   **Man-in-the-Middle Attack:** Hospital C puts itself in the way of Hospital A and Hospital B in order to intercept and maybe manipulate the data that is being transferred.
*   **Replay Attack:** Hospital C listens in on the conversations that take place between Hospital A and Hospital B. In an attempt to reinsert data or start unsanctioned data requests, it attempts to resend intercepted transactions.
*   **Identify Masquerade:** By faking signatures or changing its IP address, Hospital C tries to pass for Hospital A or Hospital B.

To prevent these attacks, several mechanisms can be proposed:

*   **End-to-End Encryption:** The public key of the recipient is used to encrypt data when it is transferred between hospitals. This guarantees that Hospital C will not be able to decode or alter data without matching private keys, even in the event of a man-in-the-middle attack.
*   **Hash and Nonce Verification:** A nonce value is included in each block to guarantee that the hash of the block corresponds to a specific pattern. As the blockchain checks the nonce and hash values, replay attacks are identified. A reused nonce value indicates a replay attempt.
*   **IP Verification and Digital Signature:** If Hospital C poses as either Hospital A or Hospital B, the digital signature verification will not succeed. IP address verifications were also added as an additional layer of security to better foil identity masquerade attempts \[[2](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_2)\].

139 

### 11.2.2 Share Sensitive Data

It is challenging for physicians and other healthcare professionals to communicate sensitive patient data because of the privacy-related characteristics of medical information. Healthcare models based on machine learning and deep learning are still not widely used due to a lack of realistic and up-to-date medical data.

**The above problem can be addressed as follows:**

Blockchain technology and FL allow for distributed learning between several physician collaborators while maintaining patient privacy. When a combination of FL and blockchain is used, it provides an extra layer of security. FL is also called collaborative learning. It is a machine learning technique that trains a global model across multiple decentralised servers holding local data samples without exchanging data \[[18](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_18)\]. Initially, weight is assigned by the global model to the local model. It trains the local models using a local dataset of that healthcare and sends the weights of all local models to the global model. All local model weights are encrypted and sent to the aggregator to aggregate weights using Secure Multiparty Computation (SMPC) and train the global model, as shown in [Figure 11. 2](#C_017_c11.xhtml#f11_2).

SMPC, also known as MPC (Multiparty Computation), allows several parties to work together to compute a function using confidential data, allowing for precise “Blackbox” calculations that protect the privacy of individual inputs. SMPC, also known as privacy-preserving computation, safe computation, or MPC, is a transformational cryptography technique. Without ever disclosing their inputs to one another, it allows several people to compute a function using private inputs and display a public output.

140Encryption and secret sharing are employed to guarantee confidentiality and privacy during the processing phase. Because it allows users to safeguard their data while still using shared systems, SMPC is crucial \[[19](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_19)\]. Without exchanging data, it allows numerous actors to construct a single, resilient machine-learning model, and it updates the weights of all local models. Again, the local model will be trained, and the global model will be trained without sharing the raw data. So, the privacy of healthcare data is preserved.

Therefore, it is possible to solve important challenges like access to heterogeneous data, data security, privacy and access rights. Suppose weights are directly sent to the global model from all local models without encryption. In that case, attacks that are possible using reverse engineering can obtain sensitive data from only locally generated updates, which is a problem \[[20](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_20)\]. So, to avoid this problem, the weight is encrypted and then sent to the global model. Many encryption algorithms (like homomorphic algorithms) can be used to encrypt the weight. Data encrypted with homomorphic encryption can be computed without the requirement first to decrypt it. Throughout the aggregation process, this feature is essential for preserving data privacy \[[21](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_21)\]. In this way, the global model is updated without sharing raw data. SMPC aims to train on heterogeneous datasets \[[22](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_22)\].

### 11.2.3 Healthcare Fraud

Healthcare fraud is an organised criminal activity in which false claims are filed with the help of peers, providers, doctors and beneficiaries. A comprehensive analysis of Medicare data has exposed a large number of fraudulent physicians. They use ambiguous diagnosis designations to support the priciest medications and therapies. Insurance companies are among the most vulnerable entities impacted by these unethical actions \[[23](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_23)\].

As a result, insurance companies raised their premiums and daily increases in healthcare costs. In the healthcare system, fraud and abuse can take many different forms. Some of the most common forms of provider fraud include service charges are never paid, Making false claims for identical service, Fabricating details on the services provided, charging for a more expensive or sophisticated service than was actually rendered, and Charging for a service that is covered even though it was not rendered. Hackers can utilise the medical information to locate the prescriptions while buying prescription medications online, which they can then use to commit fraud or identity theft \[[24](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_24)\]. In order to force victims to provide healthcare data, they would prefer to keep it secret, and hackers may also employ extortion and blackmail.

**The above problem can be addressed as follows:**

FL fraud enables FL-enabled policies through adaptive training and testing, which trains the model to detect fraud tendencies. The model can adjust to novel and unforeseen fraud patterns since it is self-learning. The fraud pattern was divided into two categories by the study: known patterns and unknown patterns. Patterns that have already been recognised and recorded are referred to as known patterns. They are comprehensible and frequently applied in forecasting and decision-making. 141These trends can be utilised to guide expectations for the future because they have been seen in historical data. Patterns that have not yet been recognised or recorded are known as unknown patterns. To be useful, they need to be discovered and analysed because they are not yet understood. Machine learning and sophisticated data mining techniques are frequently used to find these patterns.

Initially, a pattern is given to every node. Before being executed by the global node, the applications transfer their workloads to the locally accessible fog nodes for additional processing (such as testing and training). The suggested approach first allocated a certain pattern to every task and then transferred them to the corresponding fog nodes that were accessible. If a new workload follows the same previous pattern, then it is not considered fraud. If it does not follow the same pattern, i.e., an unknown pattern, then send workload to another node. If any node matches the pattern, then it is not fraud. If the pattern does not match, that is an unknown pattern, then inform all nodes that this pattern is considered fraud. In this way, static fraud is detected.

The smart contract rule is used by the current proof of work and proof of stake consensus systems to conduct data fraud and validation amongst nodes in a blockchain-enabled network. The blockchain features machine learning-enabled random-forest fraud detection integrated to tackle dynamic fraud in the network. Dynamic and runtime unknown attacks can happen \[[25](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_25)\].

### 11.2.4 Single Factor Authentication

Additional crucial components of security include authentication and access control. Many healthcare systems continue to utilise password-only access and other single-factor authentication methods despite their susceptibility to theft and guesswork. Bad password practices that compromise security include using the same password over and over again and changing it infrequently \[[26](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_26)\].

**The above problem can be addressed as follows:**

To avoid this problem, blockchain uses FL, Internet of Things (IoT), cloud computing, etc. The single-factor authentication problem can be solved by implementing multifactor authentication (MFA). Due to MFA, the risk of unwanted access is considerably reduced. MFA, a multi-step account login process, requires users to provide more information than just a password that is used for OTP. Passwords protect digital assets, but they are not enough because proficient fraudsters look for passwords on a regular basis. Someone could be able to access multiple accounts that you may have used your password for if they figure out what it is. MFA offers an additional layer of security in the event that the password is compromised, preventing unauthorised individuals from accessing these accounts. Many healthcare facilities use MFA to verify user identities and provide authorised users with convenient access \[[27](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_27)\].

## 11.3 Use Case and Applications

Application of privacy and security in blockchain

142 

### 11.3.1 Electronic Health Record

The patient creates his ID and password. They submit their report on the portal, and available doctors will see the report. After consulting the doctor, the hospital will update the patient’s profile. The doctor takes permission to get the patient’s private key to access the patient’s record, and the patient will provide his private key \[[1](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_1)\]. Only then will the doctor access the patient’s data. In this way, the people who know the private key will access the data. So, privacy and security will be maintained.

### 11.3.2 Clinical Trails and Research

An immutable ledger created by blockchain technology can capture all clinical trial data, guaranteeing data integrity and regulatory compliance. In addition, it has the ability to manage patient consent efficiently and transparently \[[28](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_28)\]. Due to immutability, data privacy and security are maintained.

### 11.3.3 Medical Billing and Claim Processing

Through smart contracts, blockchain can automate the processing of invoicing and claims, lowering errors and administrative expenses while avoiding fraudulent claims \[[25](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_25)\]. The content or condition written in a smart contract will only execute in a sequence. Due to smart contracts, privacy and security are maintained.

### 11.3.4 Prescription Management

Blockchain technology can record every prescription transaction, ensuring that medications are authentic and given as prescribed. Consequently, there is a reduced probability of medication errors and prescription fraud \[[29](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_29)\] due to its immutability nature.

### 11.3.5 Challenges

The delicate nature of medical data, the complex nature of healthcare systems, and the constantly changing environment of cyber threats provide several obstacles to privacy and security in the industry. Some of the challenges are:

### 11.3.6 Cyberattacks and Data Breach

Ransomware: Attacks using ransomware, in which cybercriminals encrypt data and demand money in order to decrypt it, are especially frequent targets of healthcare organisations. Phishing: Phishing attempts deceive workers into exposing login details or downloading harmful software, which results in leaks of confidential information. Advanced persistent threats advanced persistent threats: long-lasting, highly skilled attacks are designed to steal confidential data slowly.

143 

### 11.3.7 Problems with Interoperability

Interoperability is the ability of different blockchain networks and systems to communicate, share data and work together as a single entity. It is essential to allow various blockchain platforms to interact with and benefit from one another’s features, which raises the general effectiveness, scalability, and usefulness of blockchain technology. Many issues pertaining to patient participation, security, privacy, consent, and governance arise with the move towards patient-centred interoperability. It is difficult to ensure smooth, safe data sharing between various healthcare systems and providers while maintaining individual privacy. Risks and costs may result from the absence of established protocols and formats for data interchange \[[30](#C_017_c11.xhtml#r11_30)\].

### 11.3.8 Insider Dangers

Workers who have access to private information may abuse it or do harm with it. Data breaches can result from employees’ accidental behaviour, such as handling data improperly or falling for phishing attacks.

### 11.3.9 A Complex Regulatory Framework

Internationally functioning medical organisations are subject to a variety of administrative regulations, which can be difficult to manage and contradicting.

### 11.3.10 Outdated Systems

Many healthcare institutions still use outdated technologies, which are more difficult to secure and more open to attack. It can be difficult to integrate the latest, secure technology with older systems, being technically complex and costly.

### 11.3.11 The Volume and Complexity of Data

Healthcare systems create huge quantities of data, which makes effective security and monitoring challenging. Both structured and unstructured data—such as lab results, pictures and medical records—are included in healthcare data, and they all need various safety measures.

## 11.4 Conclusions and Future

In this Chapter, the security and privacy measures provided to healthcare data are explained. At first, several mechanisms to prevent the attacks that are possible in sharing data between hospitals are defined. By using several mechanisms to prevent possible attacks, data are easily shared between hospitals. Next, we have described how to train the global model without sharing the raw data to protect the privacy and security of data. In this, the local model is trained using the local dataset, weights are encrypted, and then the weights are aggregated via SMPC using homomorphic 144encryption and sent to the global model. The global model will be trained with the help of these weights, and aggregated values will be sent to the local model. Various strategies are discussed to prevent healthcare fraud by known and unknown patterns. If the upcoming pattern matches the existing pattern, then fraud has not occurred; if not, fraud has occurred.

Furthermore, MFA is performed to prevent single-factor authentication problems by adding an OTP facility along with an ID and password. In this way, privacy and security are maintained in healthcare. This book chapter explores the possibilities of integrating blockchain, FL, and cyber-security technologies for healthcare data management and security. The FL mechanism can be used in disease prediction models, personalised medicine, and novel therapeutics. Patients can benefit from more precise and customised diagnostic tools without having to worry about their privacy being compromised by using FL to train models using local hospital data.

The resilient and flexible fraud detection systems can constantly learn from a larger variety of data sources without disclosing private information. By enabling real-time fraud, cyber-attack and unauthorised access detection and prevention, advanced models may strengthen the healthcare system’s defences against ever-changing threats. Along with more conventional techniques like an ID and password, future authentication systems may use advanced biometrics like fingerprints, face recognition, and others.
