# 15 Harnessing Blockchain for Biotechnology Current Applications, Challenges, and Future Opportunities

Asad Khan, Mohd. Aquib Ansari, Arvind Mewada, Greetta Pinheiro, and Amrendra Singh Yadav

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-19](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-19)

## 15.1 Introduction

Blockchain \[[1](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_1), [2](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_2), [3](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_3)\], a decentralised and immutable ledger technology, has gained attention far beyond its initial application in cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin. By enabling secure, transparent, and tamper-resistant record-keeping, blockchain has the potential to transform various industries, including finance, supply chain management, and healthcare. The biotechnology sector has also begun exploring blockchain to solve some of its most pressing challenges in recent years. Biotechnology \[[4](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_4),[5](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_5),[6](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_6),[7](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_7)\], a field that applies biological systems and organisms to develop products and technologies, is central to advancements in medicine, agriculture, and environmental sustainability.

Supply chains have indeed a very complicated structure, coupled with huge amounts of data generated and intellectual property (IP) protection in high demand. With biotechnology blossoming, managing and securing data, the integrity of research, and transparency along the supply chain also grow challenges. The inherent challenges push one into the adoption of blockchain technology, especially in biotechnology. As it is evident from the fact that biotechnology requires huge volumes of genomic data as well as clinical trial results or research findings, data management indeed becomes an overdue priority along with security matters. [Figure 15.1](#C_021_c15.xhtml#f15_1) illustrates the blockchain’s overall architecture for data-sharing systems in bioinformatics. Traditional systems often fail to ensure such countermeasures against data breaches, unauthorised access, and manipulation of research data.

![Figure 15.1 Blockchain architecture for data-sharing systems in bioinformatics. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig15_01.jpg)

Figure 15.1 Blockchain architecture for data-sharing systems in bioinformatics. [Return to text.⏎](#C_021_c15.xhtml#f15_1b)

Blockchain technology indeed offers an impressive way out by providing such a decentralised, secure, and immutable record of transactions. In addition, the biotechnology industry encounters great challenges in IP management. The patenting processes of biotechnological innovations are often contested and complex, leading to litigation and disputes among the stakeholders. In this regard, blockchain can 184ease IP management by providing a transparent and immutable record of innovations, enabling the tracking and management of patents and licenses granted to the record owner.

Ultimately, the biotechnology retail chain, which comprises different agents including manufacturers, analysts, controllers, and, at last, retailers, has emerged as a prominent player. An important feature of these supply chains usually guarantees their security along with quality, hence remaining highly transparent and traceable throughout. In addition to preventing counterfeiting and adhering to regulatory requirements, the capacity of blockchain to provide a verifiable record of each step in the supply chain can contribute to the resolution of these issues. This review chapter aims to provide a far-reaching outline of the ongoing biotechnology applications for blockchain innovation.

In this chapter, [Section 15.2](#C_021_c15.xhtml#h15_2) provides a synopsis of the principal thoughts behind blockchain innovation, including its most significant highlights and types. In section 3, 185the utilisation of blockchain in biotechnology, for example, information the board, protected innovation the executives, store network straightforwardness, and customised medication, are analysed. [Section 15.4](#C_021_c15.xhtml#h15_4) discusses the troubles, impediments, and specialised, administrative, and reception hardships of carrying out blockchain in biotechnology. [Section 15.5](#C_021_c15.xhtml#h15_5) comprehensively examines existing arrangements, including various blockchain stages and a review of contextual studies. [Section 15.6](#C_021_c15.xhtml#h15_6) frames open doors and future exploration headings for blockchain and biotechnology combination. In the last section, the chapter comes to a close with a summary of the most significant findings and some concluding thoughts on the significance of blockchain in biotechnology in the future.

## 15.2 Blockchain Fundamentals

Most of the time, blockchain technology \[[8](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_8)\] is a distributed record framework that records transactions between various organisational computers. While traditional databases store data in a centralised location, blockchain operates on a decentralised network. All participants (nodes) have instant access to the same data as a result of this. This decentralisation is necessary for the transparency and security of the blockchain. The blockchain works by recording every transaction in a “block” linked to the previous block. Thus, a chain of blocks is formed, which is called a “blockchain.” Data in these blocks is secured by cryptographic algorithms, thereby ensuring that once a transaction is recorded, it is unchangeable. The consensus mechanisms vary depending on the design of the specific blockchain, which controls the addition of a new block to the chain. The consensus algorithm is the process of agreement by all participants in a blockchain network concerning the state of the distributed ledger. The most used consensus mechanism among these is Proof of Work (PoW). Nodes solve difficult algorithms for determining the rightful owner of that data. The first node that solves the problem is given the right to append a new block and is awarded with cryptocurrency. It, however, does involve a lot of energy consumption. Typically, the Proof of Stake (PoS) is considered more efficient than PoW, as a node’s probability of successfully adding a new block correlates with the number of tokens under its control. This work is said to consume minimal energy and is considered highly scalable.

Furthermore, Delegated Proof of Stakes enhances operation substantially since token holders get to nominate a certain cadre of delegates who will take responsibility for authenticating transactions and adding new blocks. Much quicker than PoW and PoS, the Delegated Proof of Stakes process has additional benefits. It applies mechanisms such as Byzantine Fault Tolerance to ensure that a blockchain can successfully operate, even with nodes resisted or erratic behaviour of nodes. This property is particularly important for private or consortium-like blockchains. In general, the features of cryptography, decentralised databases, transparency, and stability render blockchain technology superbly ideal for the industry’s areas where credit is valued and where data integrity and security are most important, being biotechnology. The fundamental principles of cryptography, decentralised databases, transparency, and structural foundation make blockchain especially appropriate for industries where the integrity of credit is paramount, together with data authenticity and security, such as biotechnology.

186 

### 15.2.1 Blockchain Types and Platforms

Three main categories of blockchain technology are mostly classified as public, private, and consortium. Examples of public blockchains include Bitcoin and Ethereum. These blockchains are open to everyone and completely decentralised. These types of blockchains may serve a significant role in biotechnology because their characteristics are critical for the transparent verification of information, especially when it comes to data from clinical trials. However, private blockchains are restricted to only one entity or a collection of organisations. They are mostly found in industries like banking and those that rely on supply chains, where privacy and efficiency are issues. In biotechnology, these private blockchains can be used to store sensitive patient information or move proprietary research data securely. Consortium blockchains are halfway decentralised and managed by a group of institutions and not by a single institution. Thus, they greatly balance openness and privacy and are pertinent to addressing collaboration challenges in biotechnology, like multi-centre clinical trials and consortium-based research initiatives.

Blockchain platforms have emerged to cater to specific needs in the biotechnology sector. For instance, Ethereum is known for its smart contract capability and is used to build decentralised applications across multiple industries, including biotechnology. It thus has the agility to automate even complex processes such as IP management and clinical trial workflows. Hyperledger Fabric is a modular, permissioned blockchain platform developed by the Linux Foundation; its current adoption in enterprises is due to factors such as scalability, privacy features, and plug-and-play components. Hyperledger Fabric can facilitate secure data sharing and supply chain management in biotechnology. Corda, initially tailored for the financial sector, stands out as a permissioned blockchain platform that prioritises interoperability and privacy. It is particularly adept at handling scenarios where multiple organisations need to share data without exposing it to all participants. This unique architecture makes Corda an invaluable tool in the biotechnology field, especially when it comes to managing contracts, safeguarding intellectual properties, and ensuring regulatory compliance.

### 15.2.2 Key Characteristics Relevant to Biotechnology

Some unique properties of blockchain technology make it an essential application in biotechnology. Above all, it immutably means that once data in a blockchain have been entered, they cannot be altered in any way. This peculiarity is necessary for maintaining data integrity in the biotechnology preservation of clinical trials and research, as well as in IP/documentation. Another very important advantage is a high degree of transparency; implementation of a blockchain system makes it possible for all the stakeholders in an organisation to be able to access the same information, thus increasing transparency and diminishing possible errors or fraudulent conduct. This is highly relevant in both clinical trials and supply chain management contexts. A prominent feature of blockchain technology is its decentralised quality.

It wipes out vulnerability due to weak links and eliminates single-point control, all by eliminating central authority. This feature proves to be very beneficial in global 187supply chain and collaborative research environments. The other characteristic of blockchain technology is that it is highly secure since the cryptographic methods used by blockchain technology are resistant to data tampering as well as unauthorised access. Blockchain technology, in the field of biotechnology, where data protection is of utmost importance, serves as a viable approach to protecting sensitive data. Furthermore, self-executing agreements with terms directly encoded into programming code are termed smart contracts, which offer great advantages. Smart contracts minimise dependence on intermediaries, increase efficiency in operation, and enforce agreements without requiring third-party involvement. This can be useful in the biotechnology processes of licensing IP, management of clinical trials, and supply chain transactions.

## 15.3 Applications of Blockchain in Biotechnology

### 15.3.1 Supply Chain Management

This indicates that a supply chain for biotechnology benefits extensively from using blockchain in that it provides an open, timestamped record of transactions. Typical members of a biotechnological item manufacturing network include suppliers of unrefined substances, manufacturers, regulators, and distributors. Products must be traceable and authentic in the pharmaceutical industry because fake drugs will have dreadful implications. Modern studies have proved that blockchain technology can indeed increase the productive efficiency of production networks through its inalterable record of all supply chain transactions. One good example is the MediLedger project \[[9](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_9)\], which, by using blockchain, is tracing pharmaceutical drugs such that only genuine ones reach the end-users. Further research has been conducted using blockchain technology in agriculture, tracing and ensuring that the origin and integrity of Genetically Modified Organism (GMOs), among other biotechnology products, are verified.

The utilisation of VeChain \[[10](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_10)\] in the pharmaceutical industry is an outstanding application of blockchain technology in the biotechnology production network. VeChain’s blockchain platform enables pharmaceutical companies to track the entire lifecycle of drugs, from production to distribution. This prevents counterfeit goods from being distributed and ensures that regulatory standards are adhered to. The incorporation of blockchain with existing inventory network frameworks and the versatility of blockchain arrangements remain obstructions, even though blockchain offers critical benefits for inventory networks. Future assessments could explore hybrid models that unite blockchain with various advances, such as the Internet of Things, to further develop the creation of network-detectable quality and capability.

### 15.3.2 Data Management and Security

The biotechnology sector generates vast data, from genomic sequences \[[11](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_11)\] to clinical trial results. Managing and securing this data is a significant challenge, particularly given the sensitive nature of this information. Blockchain’s decentralised and secure architecture offers a promising solution for data management in biotechnology. 188Several studies have explored the use of blockchain for managing and securing biomedical data. For example, blockchain has been proposed to manage genomic data, where privacy and data ownership are critical concerns.

Projects such as the Personal Genome Project have experimented with using blockchain to give individuals control over their genomic data while ensuring it remains secure and private. Another project that is based on blockchain technology is the Iryo Network \[[12](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_12)\], which is an example of a platform that can implement the management of healthcare data. Through blockchain implementation, Iryo gives patients full control over their data—only individuals with appropriate rights can read the patient’s records. It can be generalised to manage genomic and other sensitive biotechnological information. However, as with most solutions, blockchain is not without problems and issues, including the problem of storage and issues of compliance. Further studies might identify how such solutions can be integrated into large biotechnological systems and how proper legal regulations supporting blockchain technology in managing big data can be implemented.

### 15.3.3 Intellectual Property and Licensing

A well-protected area of a biotechnological business is mainly based on an IP measure since the growth of genetics, pharmaceuticals, and other related ideas can only be protected through patents and licenses. IP administration and protection is not an easy thing since it requires the assistance of many parties, as well as compliance with several different legal systems. Applying the principles of blockchain to the realisation of IP in biotechnology can be an effective method of enhancing the security of the procedures and the easy record of ownership and other transactions in the field \[[11](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_11)\]. Some investigations revealed that blockchain technology is efficient and can be applied to IP rights such as patents, copyrights, and licenses. The idea of IP transactions captured on a blockchain will help reduce theft incidences and help track licenses and enforce them.

Other examples implemented to leverage blockchain include the Intellectual Property Office of Singapore (IPOS), which shows how blockchain could bring more efficiency to IP processes. The poet strategy \[[13](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_13)\] is an example of a blockchain-based solution for controlling computerised content freedoms. Even though Poet’s strategy was initially designed with digital media in mind, it could be applied to the management of IP in biotechnology to ensure that patents and licenses are transparently recorded and enforced. The intricacy of patent regulation, which changes fundamentally from one ward to another, is one of the principal hindrances to utilising blockchain for IP on the board. Blockchain, combined with existing patent and permitting frameworks and the production of normalised blockchain structures for IP boards, could be the subject of future exploration.

### 15.3.4 Clinical Trials and Research Integrity

Clinical preliminary studies are a fundamental component of biotechnology, particularly in developing novel drugs and treatments. However, concerns regarding the integrity of clinical trials are frequently sparked by issues such as manipulation of 189data, lack of transparency, and conflicts of interest. The unchanging and straightforward nature of blockchain makes it an ideal solution for ensuring the trustworthiness of examination and clinical preliminary information. The effectiveness of clinical trials has been the topic of several studies that have suggested using blockchain technology. For instance, Benchoufi and Ravaud \[[14](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_14)\] conclude that blockchain can timestamp and check each step of the clinical trial, which means that blockchain ensures data integrity and proper trial conduct. Another characteristic can also be traced back to the manipulation of trial results, and another key issue blockchain will assist pharmaceuticals with.

ClinTrial Token is an instance of how blockchain technology can be used to solve the problems of clinical trials. Since trial data is stored on a blockchain through ClinTrial Token, the data is reliable, transparent, and conform to the set standards. Overall, such an approach may go a long way in enhancing the reliability of trials, which in return enhances the investors’ certainty in the pharmaceutical business. Looking at the advantages of blockchain linked with clinical trial transparency, there are still issues, such as data privacy and the problem of its implementation across the industry. For future works, it could be possible to propose studies linking further transparency and privacy that could be implemented with blockchain and studies that could better incentivise blockchain usage in clinical trials.

### 15.3.5 Personalised Medicine and Genomics

One rapidly developing field of biotechnology is personalised medicine, which focuses on targeted therapies considering a patient’s genetic makeup. However, there are significant challenges related to the secure management and sharing of genetic data. Blockchain technology offers a promising solution specifically designed to protect sensitive information and facilitate the secure sharing of large data sets, such as genomic data. Multiple initiatives have been implemented in the application of blockchain in the field of Ph 4400. For instance, EncrypGen \[[15](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_15)\] uses blockchain to enable human beings to contribute markers of their genetics with researchers safely and of their own volition. Likewise, Nebula Genomics utilises blockchain to share genomics data, which people can sell to researchers or pharma companies.

The Shivom project is the blockchain technology system for genomic data sharing. Through blockchain, Shivom enables customers to share genomic data with researchers and pharmaceutical companies and simultaneously decide who should be able to use the information. What makes this approach beneficial is the possibility of propelling the research in personalised medicine without violating the individual’s rights to privacy. However, one of the major issues that need to be addressed when applying blockchain in the context of personal medicine is data protection on the one hand and the ability to share data for research purposes on the other. Further study can be done in the direction of adopting blockchain with privacy techniques so that the data can be shared securely without compromising the privacy of the individuals. However, it is also known that the applicability of blockchain solutions for handling massive genomic data is one of the essential concerns for the future.

190 

## 15.4 Challenges and Limitations

### 15.4.1 Technical Challenges

Identifying the benefits that blockchain brings to the field of biotechnology is crucial. Researchers also encounter numerous technical issues in using blockchain technology, which include issues on scalability, interoperability, and energy use. As of now, scalability remains the most prominent technical problem in blockchain. Even public blockchains such as Bitcoin and Ethereum can only process a number of transactions in a certain amount of time, leading to wait times and high fees per transaction. This limitation is most pronounced for practical applications in biotechnology that need huge amounts of computation or involve thousands of transactions. Another major problem with blockchain technology is interoperability.

Data sharing and collaboration, which are imperative concepts in the field of biotechnology, suffer due to the inability of different blockchain systems to cooperate. For instance, data transfer between different types of blockchains is usually not possible, and hence, the application of blockchain technology is complex. Finally, energy consumption is one of the significant challenges as it severely hampers the functioning of PoW-based blockchain networks. The heavy energy requirement for the functioning of PoW-based blockchain networks has raised concerns about the environmental impact and sustainability of such systems over time. This is particularly relevant for industries like biotechnology, which is increasingly looking towards sustainable approaches.

### 15.4.2 Regulatory and Ethical Issues

Although there is a trend of prohibiting certain uses of blockchain, particularly for biotechnology-related applications, it is still tightly regulated, and various issues will have to be settled before they can be utilised. It matters that legal uncertainty threatens the whole application area, especially regarding the situation with blockchain-based transactions or smart contracts applied by it. It may particularly act as a deterrent in highly regulated industries such as biotechnology, where the stakes are really high. Data privacy is another major challenge, especially when it comes to the use of big data analytics.

The immutability of blockchain records raises ownership concerns over data. Again, in biotechnology, where sensitive data, especially genomic data, are collected, compliance with data privacy laws such as the General Data Protection Regulation becomes very important. Moreover, there are also ethical issues at this point in using blockchain technology in biotechnology. For instance, issues of data ownership and consent come into play. For example, personalised medicine raises questions about data ownership and the potential exploitation of genomic information for economic purposes, where sensitive data commercialisation goes beyond its ethical limits. These are ethical concerns that must be addressed to ensure that this technology is responsibly utilised within biotechnology.

191 

### 15.4.3 Adoption and Implementation Challenges

Many issues have to be dealt with and bridged to catalyse the adoption of blockchain technology in the biotechnology industry and its application. Resistance to change will be the biggest challenge that the adoption of blockchain technology will face. Most of the stakeholders here have spent decades within organisations relying much on conventional technologies, making it hard for them to shift to blockchain systems. High expense or sometimes even exorbitantly extravagant cost is a considerable barrier. The cost of implementing and maintaining the solutions using blockchain is really extremely high.

The financial advantages might accrue from its use over time, but in the end, investment in development and integration would be extremely limited due to its short-term understanding. Complexity does not facilitate adoption into blockchain as well. The chains in technology are complex, while the skills required in developing blockchain solutions are very specialised. This can be a big barrier for organisations without the relevant human resource profile because they cannot adopt and effectively use the technology.

## 15.5 Comparative Analysis of Existing Solutions

Based on the potential uses and varying characteristics of the blockchain in the biotechnology sector, various blockchain platforms are available. In this section, the authors describe the most promising blockchain solutions to be applied in biotechnology and compare them with advantages and disadvantages for specific tasks.

**Ethereum vs. Hyperledger Fabric:** Ethereum is very flexible and allows the support of smart contracts, making it suitable for use in IP management and clinical trial processes. It is a public platform, however, with scalability issues that make it less appropriate for dealing with sensitive data. On the other hand, Hyperledger Fabric is an open-source Blockchain Consortium (BCC) that is privacy-enhancing and flexible to use while implementing smart contracts; this suits large-scale organisations that use it in a supply chain and secure information exchange.

**Corda vs Quorum:** Corda is permissioned and also open, and therefore, it is well suited for establishing platforms through which different organisations can safely share data. Like Quorum, which is based on Ethereum but has granted access only to certain participants, the provision of anonymity in Vital is combined with the possibility of using a smart contract. It would be highly appropriate for both platforms to handle the various contracts and comply with biotechnology regulations; however, the colour choice will depend on the needs of each application.

This section also discusses several real-world cases of the successful application of blockchain in biotechnology, as well as an assessment of the effectiveness of such approaches and their potential consequences.

**MediLedger Project:** One of the projects that prove blockchain improves the supply chain and prevents the distribution of counterfeit products is MediLedger, which employs blockchain in tracking pharmaceutical products. This, therefore, 192shows how the use of blockchain enhances the safety and compliance with the standards of biotechnological products.

**EncrypGen:** Blockchain-based applications such as EncrypGen, which is used to manage genomic data through the blockchain, have described how blockchain can help people control how their data and information are used and shared securely. These sentiments apprise the platform’s effectiveness and the potential facilitated by blockchain to overhaul data in personalised medication.

**IPOS Blockchain Patent Platform:** The IPOS has implemented blockchain technology to manage patents. The danger of patent disputes has been eliminated, and the patenting process has been accelerated, proving the viability of the application of blockchain platforms for managing all forms of IP in biotechnology.

## 15.6 Future Directions and Research Opportunities

Integrating blockchain technology with other relatively modern technologies, artificial intelligence \[[16](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_16),[17](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_17),[18](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_18),[19](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_19),[20](#C_021_c15.xhtml#r15_20)\], the Internet of Things, and big data presents great opportunities for developing biotechnology. For instance, integrating blockchain with artificial intelligence can make treatment planning and patient data analysis more effective in personalised medicine. Likewise, integrating blockchain with the Internet of Things may improve accountability in the supply chain for biotechnological products by allowing for tracking services. Although progress has been made in applying blockchain technology to biotechnology, there are still a number of research obstacles. These include developing standard structures for blockchain reception in biotechnology, working on the adaptability and interoperability of blockchain arrangements, and developing protection-saving blockchain advancements. Blockchain could affect biotechnology by making it conceivable to oversee information in a safer and viable way, expanding the straightforwardness of supply chains and shielding protected innovation. Notwithstanding, using this potential will require progressing blockchain innovation examination, joint effort, and speculation.

## 15.7 Conclusion

Blockchain technology can address some of the most pressing issues in biotechnology, such as supply chain transparency, IP management, and data security. In any case, a couple of particular regulatory and gathering difficulties should be addressed to grasp this potential. The primary focus of subsequent research should be overcoming these obstacles and examining the emerging opportunities at the intersection of blockchain and biotechnology.

# 16 Blockchain and Artificial Intelligence in Financial Industry 4.0 Enter the World of Digital Transformation

Annu and Ravindra Tripathi

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-21](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-21)

## 16.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) is the ability of a computer or machine to mimic human intelligence and conduct. Trend analysis, stock price predictions, fraud detection, and routine work automation are all possible with AI in finance. In recent years, significant advances have been made in AI technology. Google Inc.’s Alpha-Go AI software defeated human Go players in 2016, placing machine learning at the top of the rankings. After that, machine learning, neural networks, and other terms became common in financial markets and stock exchanges. Technology like AI and finance Industry 4.0 are changing the financial services business. Programming computers to learn, make judgments, and recognise patterns from past lessons is called “artificial intelligence” (AI). However, financial Industry 4.0 refers to adopting modern technologies like AI, big data, and blockchain in financial services \[[1](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_1),[2](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_2)\].

Blockchain technology has transformed financial transactions by enhancing security and speed \[[3](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_3)\]. Asset tokenisation, cross-border payments, and smart contracts are further blockchain finance applications. The transaction will determine the ideal blockchain for finance. Private blockchains are better for private transactions, while public blockchains are transparent. Transaction speed, security, and regulatory compliance are critical when choosing financial blockchain models. Financial activity type also affects this choice. Blockchain records transactions transparently and unchangeably, improving financial operations. Due to its decentralised nature, fraud and manipulation are considerably reduced \[[4](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_4),[5](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_5)\]. Fintech companies use AI and its many applications to create digital trading platforms that allow low-income women and young and small businesses to access the mainstream financial market.

AI and blockchain in the finance Industry 4.0 are solving risk management, fraud detection, and portfolio optimisation problems. AI also helps financial institutions offer real-time financial data and 24/7 client service. AI and blockchain in the financial Industry 4.0 are launching a new age of innovation and growth in financial services that might improve efficiency, accuracy, and accessibility for businesses and customers.

198 

### 16.1.1 Industries Revolution Background

*   **Coal in 1765**: Steam power and manufacturing mechanisation began in the 18th century, starting the First Industrial Revolution. Mechanised spinning wheels produced eight times as much thread as conventional ones in the same period. Steam was noted for its efficacy. Its industrial use was the most significant productivity boost. Without humans, steam engines could power weaving looms. Technology like the steamship and (100 years later) the steam-powered locomotive allowed people and goods to travel farther in less time, leading to further advances.
*   **Gas in 1870**: With the rise of electricity and the production line in the 19th century, the Second Industrial Revolution began. Henry Ford (1863–1947) invented mass manufacturing in a Chicago abattoir where pigs were hung from conveyor belts, and each butcher only slaughtered a piece. “Henry Ford (1863–1947) implemented these concepts into automotive manufacturing, transforming the industry.” Previously, an entire car was assembled at one location; now, small batches on a conveyor belt are more efficient and cost-effective.
*   **Electronics and Nuclear in 1969**: The decade of the 1970s marked the beginning of the 20th century’s Third Industrial Revolution with the use of computers and memory-programmable controls. Since these technologies were introduced, production can be automated without human interaction. Well-known examples are robots that run pre-programmed sequences without human interaction.
*   **Internet and Renewable Energy in 2000**: *“Industry 4.0”* refers to corporate use of information and communication technology, building on advancements from the Third Industrial Revolution. Using a network link, *“*an online digital twin can be established and expanded for computer-based manufacturing processes*.”* These allow facilities to communicate with one another and transmit information about themselves \[[6](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_6), [3](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_3)\]. Smart factories are created by cyber-physical production systems that connect all systems and require minimal human interaction. When these enablers come together, Industry 4.0 could revolutionise manufacturing. Examples are machines that foresee problems and start repair operations or self-organised logistics that adjust to production variations. [Figure 16.1](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_1) depicts the Industrial Revolution.
    
    ![Figure 16.1 Industries revolution hierarchy. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_01.jpg)
    
    Figure 16.1 Industries revolution hierarchy. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_1b)
    

199 

### 16.1.2 Involvement of Technologies

[Figure 16.2](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_2) shows technological advances like cashless payments, digital payments, and Internet banking affect everyone daily. Investment technology is evolving, as shown by online trading apps. AI and machine learning improve digital market administration.

![Figure 16.2 Involved technologies in Industry 4.0. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_02.jpg)

Figure 16.2 Involved technologies in Industry 4.0. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_2b)

The stock market is complex and dynamic. Selecting an effective investment plan is tough. The challenge is accurate prediction. Stock prices are affected by politics, the market, and the economy. Investor decisions also affect them. Circumstances can significantly affect stock prices. Strict analysis makes finding a simple linear function equation for stock prices and other parameters difficult. Thus, while many basic investing models can recommend investments, they rarely foresee irregular financial goods. AI involves self-organisation, nonlinearity, learning, and self-adaptation.

## 16.2 Theoretical Framework and Conceptual Model

### 16.2.1 Artificial Intelligence and Stock Trading Industry 4.0 Relation

Modern financial markets can simplify market operations and increase trading strategy versatility. Financial institutions now operate better thanks to AI and machine learning \[[7](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_7)\]. From opening an online demat account to trading, everything is electronic. Technology improves usefulness and does not replace intelligence \[[8](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_8)\]. Financial markets today rely on trading software and transaction analysis instruments. Many trading systems allow online trading and demat accounts.

200Stock Trading Industry 4.0 uses cutting-edge technology like AI to improve stock trading efficiency and efficacy. AI’s purpose in Stock Trading Industry 4.0 is to analyse enormous amounts of data in real-time and make predictions and judgments. Additional trading solutions that employ AI to improve your trading experience are available. See how technology generates investor intelligence:

*   **Risk Awareness:** Businesses leverage machine learning, processing speed, and big data to analyse compliance risk-taking. This results from AI technology helping investors recognise complex trading patterns on a large scale. It also occurs in real-time across all marketplaces.
*   **AI Stock Trading:** Real-time financial notes, data, trending firms, and market information enable investors to trade. This is done via speech recognition and Natural Language Processing (NLP). Its primary benefit is saving traders time and effort.
*   **Recommendations and Tips:** Stock recommendations are made available through recognition technology, which helps investors make smart choices and maximise certain trading opportunities.
*   **Less Human Error:** because traders do not have to interact with people to get advice on trading tactics, etc. Data science quickly overcomes investment challenges.

#### 16.2.1.1 Major Steps for the Formation of AI in the Financial Industry

Various steps, including AI in the financial industry, can be considered, as shown in [Figure 16.3](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_3).

![Figure 16.3 Formation steps of AI in the financial industry. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_03.jpg)

Figure 16.3 Formation steps of AI in the financial industry. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_3b)

*   **Data Collection and Preparation**: The first step is to gather and process vast amounts of financial data to feed into AI algorithms.
*   **Model Development**: This involves selecting appropriate AI algorithms and developing models that can analyse financial data and make predictions or recommendations.
*   **Training and Validation:** The models have to be trained in big data so that accuracy improves; after that, these are validated to ensure effectiveness.
*   **Integration and Deployment:** AI models must be integrated with existing financial systems and deployed to end-users.
*   201**Monitoring and Maintenance:** After their development, the AI models are monitored for performance and updated accordingly to ensure effectiveness.
*   **Regulation and Ethical Considerations:** The financial services industry is highly regulated. It should, therefore, develop and deploy AI in full consideration of the existing laws and regulations while exercising concern for ethical considerations.

### 16.2.2 Major Area of Financial Technology in Industry 4.0

FinTech represents one of the economy’s fastest-growing sectors, using technology to improve and automate financial services. Major FinTech applications in Industry 4.0 include:

*   **Mobile and Digital Banking:** Greater penetration of mobiles and internet connectivity has also transformed the level of mobile banking and mobile merchant services.
*   **Blockchain Technology:** Blockchain technology increases financial transaction transparency, security, and efficiency.
*   **Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning:** AI and machine learning will automate most finance processes regarding risk management, fraud detection, and the management of investments.
*   **Robo-Advisors:** Automated investment management services, often called robo-advisors, are becoming increasingly popular because they offer personalised investment advice to individuals.
*   **Digital Currencies**: Digital currencies such as Bitcoin and Ethereum are becoming more mainstream, leading to a growing interest in expanding new financial products and services based on blockchain technology.
*   **Insurtech**: FinTech companies are transforming the insurance sector by offering new products, services, and distribution channels, leveraging digital and data technologies.

#### 16.2.2.1 How Has AI Made Its Mark in Financial Industry 4.0?

The financial industry’s theoretical framework for AI is based on several key concepts and principles. One key concept is using machine learning algorithms that analyse and process large amounts of financial data, such as market trends, customer behaviour, and risk factors. Another important concept is using artificial neural networks to make predictions and decisions based on this data.

As seen in [Figure 16.4](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_4), the conceptual model of AI in the financial sector usually consists of the following elements:

![Figure 16.4 Components of AI in financial industry. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_04.jpg)

Figure 16.4 Components of AI in financial industry. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_4b)

*   **Data Processing and Analysis:** The AI algorithms extract, clean, and process financial data for further use.
*   **Machine Learning Algorithms:** These algorithms use algorithms that have potential, such as decision trees, random forests, and neural networks, to analyse and make predictions on processed financial data.
*   202**Predictive Modelling:** This employed the output from machine learning algorithms to procure prognostications about the future trends in the marketplace, customers’ behaviour, and risk factors.
*   **Decision-Making:** This implies the application of the predictive modelling module in decision-making processes, such as finding the best approach to investments or minimising risks.
*   Overall, the conceptual model of AI in the financial industry aims to use advanced technologies and algorithms to improve decision-making and enhance the efficiency of financial operations.

#### 16.2.2.2 How Has Blockchain Technology Made Its Mark in Financial Industry 4.0?

Blockchain is essential and promising in Industry 4.0 \[[9](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_9),[10](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_10)\], and this technology is believed to transform the world economy. It offers many chances for established businesses to prosper and new ones to start while challenging traditional enterprises. Blockchain is mainly connected with Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies. Its benefits extend beyond cryptocurrency to many commercial and social transactions. 203Thus, blockchain could be used in different business applications \[[11](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_11),[12](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_12)\]. Blockchain’s strong cryptographic encryption, network participants’ ongoing monitoring, and decentralised transaction data make it fraud-resistant. These features make manipulating system transactions nearly impossible \[[13](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_13),[14](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_14)\].

[Figure 16.5](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_5) illustrates how blockchain technology affects numerous facets of the financial industry by offering decentralised, transparent, safe, and efficient solutions.

![Figure 16.5 Several facets of blockchain in the financial sector. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_05.jpg)

Figure 16.5 Several facets of blockchain in the financial sector. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_5b)

## 16.3 Theoretical and Practical Implications

### 16.3.1 Why Should I Feel Confident Investing in Artificial Intelligence?

Face recognition on phones, online shopping recommendations, finding the perfect job, a tailored music list, and Google Maps to handle intricate and unpredictable traffic are all examples of AI making our lives easier. AI affects us unknowingly \[[15](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_15)\]. AI-powered ‘Jarvis Invest creates and manages a portfolio using 12 million financial criteria. We manage risk with this portfolio’s 24 × 7 risk management. It can eliminate standard financial measuring methods’ flaws in complex settings \[[16](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_16)\]. Indeed, quantitative investment using machine learning and AI has advanced swiftly and benefited investors.

204 

### 16.3.2 Applications and Challenges of AI in Stock Market Industry 4.0

AI is employed in the stock trading sector in various applications, as illustrated in [Figure 16.6](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_6).

![Figure 16.6 Employment of AI in stock market Industry 4.0. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_06.jpg)

Figure 16.6 Employment of AI in stock market Industry 4.0. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_6b)

*   **Portfolio Management:** Using parameters like risk tolerance, investment horizon, and performance objectives, AI algorithms can be utilised to optimise the composition of a portfolio of investments.
*   **Development of Trading Strategies:** AI may be used to find relationships and patterns in massive data sets that can be utilised to create trading strategies. These tactics may be based on fundamental analysis, technical analysis, or a mix of the two.
*   **Trade Execution:** AI can automatically execute trades based on preset parameters or rules. Circumstances set in algorithms tracking markets and then executing trades when such circumstances are filled can be used.
*   205**Risk Management:** With real-time monitoring of trades and portfolios, AI analyses and controls risks once they occur. Examples include detecting unusual trading patterns, noticing market conditions, and making any necessary changes in the trades based on them \[[5](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_5)\].
*   **Market Analysis:** With market data analysis, AI can provide insight for traders making the right decisions, like finding patterns and trends from market data, predicting price changes, or even giving real-time market analyses.

AI may increase equity trading efficiency, reduce cost, and enhance accuracy. However, AI is only the most accurate if its data and algorithms are the best; errors or biases in these will lead to errors \[[17](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_17)\]. In actively managed equity funds, AI advisers replace advisers when picking stocks. BlackRock is the most prominent US investment manager stock picker with a self-learning AI-based investment program. AI stock trading employs Robo-Advisors that scan for vast data and make a trade at optimal timing and price. AI traders are more visionary and effectively control a company’s corporate trades, reducing risks and earning more returns \[[18](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_18),[19](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_19)\]. Still, the financial sector faces many challenges in embracing AI technology. One of the main setbacks is the absence of technical people trained and skilled enough to set up AI systems.

The cost of AI is expensive in terms of purchase and large-scale implementation. Privacy issues also arise due to the immense amounts of data AI systems need to function effectively, posing security issues to financial institutions. More automation via AI also leads to job loss and, hence, the redeployment of workers. It is limited to the accuracy because also by the quality of data on which the AI systems have been trained that sometimes will convey biases in the resultant output. Many consumers still doubt AI in the financial sector, as they always distrusted an AI-powered system where they will keep storing their finances and other assets \[[20](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_20)\]. The deployment of an AI system is technically cumbersome as it has to interface with the existing processes. There is also a talent shortage in AI, making it difficult for financial institutions to find qualified professionals to develop and implement these systems. Lastly, the high costs associated with AI development and implementation can make it challenging for institutions to justify the investment.

Among the challenges that emerge from AI in finance are lack of transparency or bias by a financial AI system, data quality, regulation, expertise, risk management, and black box algorithms. Financial AI algorithms are complex, and an investor finds it challenging to evaluate AI-based investment strategies. Biassed AI systems could lead to the wrong financial output. AI algorithms generate forecasts based on the large amounts of data available and quality counts for precision \[[6](#C_023_c16.xhtml#r16_6)\]. It is challenging for investors to analyse AI-powered investment risks because they are not precisely controlled and regulated in finance. The topic of AI is expanding very fast, and many investors cannot appreciate the risks and benefits associated with AI-powered investment techniques. AI algorithms can process large amounts of data quickly, offering real-time financial choices that complicate risk management for investors. AI algorithms can indeed be “black box” systems, making investment decisions incomprehensible or impossible for investors to understand. The above issues thus underpin 206the importance of an informed investor being well-informed about the potential dangers and benefits of AI-powered investment strategies and the deliberative nature of the investment.

## 16.4 Conclusion, Implications, and Future Research Directions

As a contribution to the finance literature, the paper further exposes an analysis of the role of AI within the financial industry as launched by the Fourth Industrial Revolution, Industry 4.0. It portrays the technological evolution from the first industrial revolution and draws insights into AI and stock trading as part of financial technology, or FinTech 4.0. The research shows how AI and blockchain change the financial sector by providing better safety, efficiency, and inclusivity at lower costs and without intermediaries. Given this pattern, technology can further impact the financial sector and provide growth opportunities.

Industry 5.0 would be the shift from technology-centric processes to human–machine collaboration. As such, Industry 5.0 would emphasise the integration of AI with human expertise, in turn, being able to produce highly personal, efficient, and innovative outputs (see [Figure 16.7](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_7)). Therefore, if manufacturing and financial services fail to keep pace with changes in the sector, Industry 5.0 will make them obsolete.

![Figure 16.7 Core values of Industry 5.0. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig16_07.jpg)

Figure 16.7 Core values of Industry 5.0. [Return to text.⏎](#C_023_c16.xhtml#f16_7b)

207While AI brings sundry advantages, high cost remains one of the main barriers. The costs of implementing AI on efficiency and privacy must be overcome so financial institutions can fully adopt and implement AI. Future directions should be to make technologies more accessible and affordable.

Moreover, future research should conduct surveys on investors to assess their knowledge about AI and how complete AI integration can impact investment choices. Further research into AI synergy with new technologies may lead to better decisions that benefit investors and businesses. AI can be fully utilised in the financial sector by addressing concerns over cost and privacy.
