# 17 International Payments Using Blockchain A Comprehensive Analysis of Efficiency, Security, and Feasibility

Arvind Mewada, Mohd. Aquib Ansari, Niharika Keshari, Nagendra Singh, and Amrendra Singh Yadav

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-22](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-22)

## 17.1 Introduction

With the rapid development of internet technology, electronic money transfers or international payments are growing rapidly. Nowadays, the international payment system plays an important role in performing global e-commerce activities. The total value of global international payments has reached USD 11.53 trillion in 2024 and is estimated to increase to USD 21.8 trillion by 2028, as per the World Bank statistics \[[1](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_1)\]. India is a major global trading nation that generates a large volume of international payments due to its broad offline import and export activities. In addition, the demand for international payments in India is increasing day by day due to factors such as e-commerce transactions, studying abroad, and tourism.

Nonetheless, the existing international payment model is suffering from challenges such as cumbersome processes and high transaction fees. Moreover, as these payment systems are exposed to serious security threats, these can expose user privacy as well as data protection risks \[[2](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_2)\]. Even this system has lengthy processing times and inefficient capital usage, which have further hampered the growth of e-commerce. Therefore, addressing these challenges and optimising international payment methods have become an important research area in the international payment system.

The cryptocurrency Bitcoin introduces the groundbreaking concept of blockchain technology. This technology has transformed financial applications, particularly in digital payment systems, both nationally and internationally. The greatest advantage of blockchain is that it has the ability to secure transactions and foster trust at a lower cost. This kind of feature of blockchain has garnered significant attention from government institutions, organisations, technology-based companies, and financial institutions worldwide \[[3](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_3)\]. This is known as the first generation of blockchain technology.

210The second generation of blockchain technology introduced smart contracts, enabling functionalities beyond digital transaction transactions to assets such as stocks, loans, and real estate. The third generation of blockchain technology further expands its scope and offers solutions for industries ahead of banking and finance, such as healthcare, government, and even the science sector \[[4](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_4),[5](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_5)\].

The National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog) is a policy think tank for the government of India. NITI Aayog published a draft discussion paper in January 2020 titled “Blockchain: The India Strategy.” In this report, the Aayog highlighted the journey or development of blockchain technology within the country (India) \[[6](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_6)\]. The Indian government has initiated various blockchain initiatives across sectors to promote the technology and related industries. For instance, in February 2020, the “Maharashtra Blockchain Sandbox” was launched, focusing on blockchain-based applications for e-governance, particularly in supply chain management, land registry, and public record-keeping \[[7](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_7)\]. The IndiaChain project, established in 2017, also aimed to create a blockchain-based platform for exchanging information and services among government agencies \[[8](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_8)\].

In the banking sector, ICICI Bank of India integrated blockchain technology into its Internet e-commerce financing system in August 2016. Furthermore, in December 2022, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) launched the Digital Rupee, a central bank digital currency utilising blockchain technology. Unlike other cryptocurrencies, the Electronic Rupee (ER) has intrinsic value and is regulated by the RBI. The State Bank of India (SBI) Bank of Communications is also actively developing blockchain-based international payment application systems \[[9](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_9)\].

## 17.2 Related Work

Blockchain is a digital distributed ledger that employs cryptographic techniques to secure and link digital records known as “blocks.” These blocks are interconnected in sequential order through cryptographic “hashes,” which form a secure chain of blocks. The foundation principle of blockchain has evolved since the 1980s but has been popularised worldwide after the advent of Bitcoin. Chaum \[[10](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_10)\] performed an early innovation in which he proposed blind signatures. It is a cryptographic method that is used for automated payments while ensuring the payee, transaction amount, and timing remain anonymous to third parties. Performing advancements in the area of cryptography, Haber and Stornetta \[[11](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_11)\] presented the concept of timestamping digital documents. It enhances security and authenticity by using timestamps embedded in records.

The author Finney \[[12](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_12)\] performed further extended advanced research in this field with the Reusable Proof of Work concept. This represents a groundwork for a secure and transparent digital currency payment system that could operate independently of traditional banks or central authorities. This research contribution shows a conclusion in the form of the first Bitcoin transaction performed in 2009. In this transaction, the researcher transferred ten Bitcoin **(**BTC) to a friend, and this marks the appearance of blockchain technology.

Bhaskar and Chuen \[[13](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_13)\] established the first Bitcoin exchange named “Bitcoin Market.” It is marked a significant milestone for the blockchain development. 211Buterin \[[14](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_14)\] follows this and introduces the concept known as a smart contract, which is integral to Ethereum based on a blockchain platform designed for decentralised applications and transactions beyond digital currencies.

Swan \[[15](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_15)\] performed a study based on the application scope of blockchain technology. This study highlighted the potential of blockchain in the financial sector. Further ahead, Patki and Sople \[[16](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_16)\] analysed the impact of blockchain on traditional financial institutions. They find the influence of blockchain on Indian commercial banks. Zhang \[[17](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_17)\] explored the application of blockchain in international payments systems. The author notices the potential of blockchain to create a globally integrated clearing system and to reduce transaction costs.

Deng \[[18](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_18)\] analysed the innovative potential of blockchain in international payment systems. He suggested strategies for blockchain trial and broader promotion. Similarly, Mori \[[19](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_19)\] performed a case study on Ripple. He demonstrated the advantages of blockchain in payment and settlement processes. Li \[[20](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_20)\] examined the limitations of existing traditional international payment models. He provides details on the benefits of blockchain-based payment systems and highlights their challenges.

Shen et al. \[[21](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_21)\] proposed an international payment system based on blockchain. They describe the advantages of blockchain in simplifying transactions. Roy et al. \[[22](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_22)\] compared various blockchain payment models used in international payments. Deng \[[23](#C_024_c17.xhtml#r17_23)\] also highlighted the potential of blockchain to address the bottlenecks in traditional international payment systems. Despite these advantages, Deng warned that risks associated with the implementation of blockchain technology based on international payments still need thorough exploration.

The literature of this chapter reveals that inefficiency, high costs, and security risks are the most prominent challenges associated with traditional international payments. This makes these issues a focus point for researchers in this field of research. The advent of blockchain offers promising solutions to these pain points using the introduction of decentralised, tamper-proof, anonymous, and traceable systems.

This chapter explores the potential of blockchain technology to overcome the limitations of existing international payment systems. Initially, the chapter theoretically examines the advantages of blockchain, which simplifies the payment process, enhances payment efficiency, reduces transaction costs, and ensures the security and authenticity of transactions. Then, designed a case study of an international payment system based on blockchain, which integrates real-world operations. Finally, the chapter presents recommendations for implementing blockchain in international payments, offering valuable insights for developing and commercialising blockchain-based payment systems in India.

## 17.3 Blockchain Technology Overview

Blockchain synthesises distributed data storage, peer-to-peer (P2P) transmission, cryptographic algorithms, consensus mechanisms, timestamps, incentive structures (including rewards and punishments), and smart contracts. At its core, it is a decentralised, collectively maintained, and reliable ledger technology. Due to its decentralised, tamper-proof, anonymous, programmable, and secure nature, blockchain holds significant application value in areas such as digital currencies, international 212payments, and trade finance. Blockchain is composed of multiple blocks linked together to form a chain. Each block is structured with two main components, as depicted in [Figure 17.1](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_1), representing a typical block structure: the block header and the block body.

![Figure 17.1 A block structure. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig17_01.jpg)

Figure 17.1 A block structure. [Return to text.⏎](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_1b)

The block body stores verified transactions, while the block header contains important metadata. This metadata includes the hash of the previous block, the hash of the current block, the timestamp, the nonce, and the Merkle root, all of which ensure the block’s integrity and its connection to the rest of the chain.

The blockchain architecture is divided into six layers, as shown in [Figure 17.2](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_2). Among them, the data layer is used to describe the physical form of the blockchain. It primarily includes data blocks, hash functions, timestamps, Merkle trees, chain structure, public-key encryption, etc. In the network layer, there are distributed networking, communication, and data verification mechanisms to distribute, forward, and verify blockchain transactions, etc. Typically modelled as a P2P network for all nodes that are equal participants. As soon as a transaction occurs, it is sent to nearby nodes, which check whether it is valid or invalid according to the rules set. Valid transactions are forwarded to further nodes, and invalid ones are discarded. In digital signatures, asymmetric cryptography is used to authenticate transactions through a signing and verification phase. It ensures secure interactions and communication among nodes in the blockchain network. The consensus layer contains various algorithms that are designed to achieve efficient agreement among untrustworthy nodes in decentralised environments. Blockchain networks do not have a trusted central node, so a rule (protocol) or process is needed to obtain consensus before a block is added to the blockchain network. So, in this layer, four major (Proof of Work (PoW), Proof of Stake, Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT), and Delegated 213214Proof of Stake (DPoS)) consensus mechanisms are widely used. The PoW is utilised in Bitcoin, which requires nodes to repeatedly run hashing functions to generate a nonce that is difficult to produce yet easy to validate. The PoS is an energy-efficient mechanism that enables nodes with the largest stakes to generate blocks.

![Figure 17.2 Blockchain layer architecture. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig17_02.jpg)

Figure 17.2 Blockchain layer architecture. [Return to text.⏎](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_2b)

The DPoS is a type of blockchain consensus mechanism in which token holders elect delegates who create new blocks and verify transactions on the blockchain. This system is designed to make blockchain operations more democratic and efficient. PBFT is a consensus algorithm designed to reach consistent decisions in blockchains and distributed networks even when some nodes behave erratically or maliciously. It is specifically designed to solve the Byzantine Fault Tolerance problem of keeping the system secure and stable despite fraud or malfunctions in the network. The incentive layer contains issuance and distribution mechanisms, which are mainly used to encourage nodes to participate in the security verification of blockchain transactions. The contract layer contains various algorithms, such as script codes and smart contracts. These are used to realise programmable features. The application layer includes application scenarios and cases of blockchain in various fields.

The processes of recording, transmitting, verifying, and storing blockchain data do not require the credit endorsement of a third-party institution. Instead, they rely solely on mathematical methods that enable distributed nodes to achieve a consensus of trust. Blockchain systems employ P2P networks without a central server. This network structure comprises many highly autonomous nodes that are equal in status and freely connected in a flat topology, allowing for resource sharing. In blockchain, decentralisation reduces transaction complexity, saves costs, and alleviates issues (connection interruptions and information delays), which ensures efficient value transfer without intermediary (organisation or middleman) links or servers.

Blockchain is a key technology that uses cryptographic methods to ensure the security and tamper-freeness of data. Hash functions serve as a unique tool under this security layer, which plays a vital role in maintaining the authenticity and integrity of data. This hash function works as a strong collision resistance, which is used to link blocks. It ensures that transaction information remains intact. An individual node attempts to tamper with transaction data already uploaded to the blockchain. So, when this happens, the hash value changes, which makes the block with that transaction invalid. Other nodes reject any attempts to tamper with the data unless someone manages to control 51% of the entire network, which would make it possible to manipulate the blockchain. Otherwise, tampering with a single node has no effect due to the integrity of the blockchain. Strong computational protection against attacks is provided to the authorisation and the verification of transactions in the blockchain through a mechanism such as PoW based on public key cryptography.

Moreover, timestamp technology is used to add a time dimension to data, which ensures the traceability and authenticity of each transaction record. Smart contracts work as digital commitments that make blockchain data programmable, which is used to enhance the management and trading of traditional financial assets and lay the foundation for programmable financial systems. They are characterised by decentralisation and autonomy. Decentralisation means that smart contracts are executed by decentralised stored and verified program codes, ensuring fairness and equity in fund transfers. Autonomy implies that once specific conditions are met, the contract executes automatically without external interference, ensuring the security 215and accuracy of transaction processing. By leveraging the programmability of blockchain smart contracts, complex centralised clearing processes can be eliminated, significantly reducing costs, improving efficiency, and enabling the quick and convenient trading of financial products.

Although transaction information in the blockchain is broadcast to the entire network, the identities of the transaction nodes remain anonymous. Each node does not need to trust one another, meaning identity information must not be publicly disclosed. This allows for anonymity, protecting user privacy. Each account identity in the blockchain system is represented by a wallet address that can be changed at will. This wallet address is generated through cryptographic technology, making it impossible to infer the user’s identity or private key. No one can determine the identity of the transaction user corresponding to an account, ensuring that each transaction is carried out anonymously.

Blockchain is a distributed shared ledger maintained collectively by all nodes. The distributed data storage and transaction recording enable all users to participate in this bookkeeping system. The data on the ledger is public and transparent, allowing anyone to check, trace, and audit information through a public interface. This public transparency ensures the tamper-proof nature of blockchain data and allows for public oversight of compliance with operational behaviour. Consequently, the transaction information within the blockchain system is open and transparent, preventing one node from deceiving another. The transparency of blockchain promotes open and effective operations in international trade activities.

## 17.4 Addressing Challenges in International Payments with Blockchain Technology

Common international payment methods include bank wire transfers (as shown in [Figure 17.3](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_3)), international credit card payments, and third-party payment services, with bank wire transfers being the primary remittance option. These traditional payment settlements operate in a centralised architecture involving multiple intermediary entities. As a result, the payment-clearing process can be cumbersome, 216leading to slow business processing speeds, low payment efficiency, and high costs. Furthermore, the lengthy and complex payment procedures place significant demands on bank personnel, and operational errors can have serious consequences for both parties involved in the trade. The emergence of blockchain technology offers potential solutions to the challenges of traditional international payments. Integrating blockchain into this process makes it possible to eliminate intermediaries, simplify settlement procedures, enhance payment efficiency, and reduce costs, making international payments faster and more cost-effective. Moreover, blockchain reduces risks in operations when performing international payments and protects user privacy.

![Figure 17.3 Wire transfer using society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunications. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig17_03.jpg)

Figure 17.3 Wire transfer using society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunications. [Return to text.⏎](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_3b)

### 17.4.1 Payment Efficiency in International Transactions

Payment efficiency mainly refers to processes and the associated time costs. Simplifying these transaction processes and reducing time costs is a very important point for international trade, as it ensures a smooth flow of money and helps reduce foreign exchange risks. Traditional wire transfer systems have multiple steps, such as remitting banks, correspondent banks, payment systems, and receiving banks. Each organisation or institution operates its own independent accounting system, which results in complex procedures such as fund recording, clearing, and reconciliation with the counterparty bank. Subsequently, this structure or framework contains lengthy processing times and low payment efficiency for international transactions. For example, a bank which performs wire transfers for international remittances generally takes 3–5 working days. However, international credit cards or third-party payment services take 3–7 days to process. In the case of large-scale transactions in which a manufacturer purchases raw materials from a supplier, the opportunity cost of waiting several days for payment clearance is significant. These delays can hinder production schedules, impact a company’s ability to fulfil orders, and potentially jeopardise business relationships and financial stability.

Blockchain technology eliminates intermediatory mediators or organisations, reduces payment times in parallel, and maintains proper security. This allows remitters and recipients to make direct P2P payments and settlements. Furthermore, payments can be processed 24/7, regardless of holidays, enabling instant arrivals. In addition, withdrawals are convenient and free of hidden costs.

Stellar (XLM), Ripple (XRP), Corda, and IBM are blockchain platforms used worldwide to create a blockchain-based international payment system. Ripple is a popular platform due to its speed and low transaction costs, which facilitate real-time international transactions. Ripple is a decentralised consensus mechanism that has the ability to perform a transaction in short times to within 3 seconds, significantly improving settlement efficiency (see [Figure 17.4](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_4)).

![Figure 17.4 Ripple transfer system. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig17_04.jpg)

Figure 17.4 Ripple transfer system. [Return to text.⏎](#C_024_c17.xhtml#f17_4b)

The efficiency of international payments is of great significance to traders. In a competitive international market, fluctuations in foreign exchange rates significantly impact trading operations. International businesses reduce the exchange rate risk associated with long-term capital occupation while simultaneously accelerating capital turnover, contributing to market prosperity by shortening settlement times.

217 

### 17.4.2 Cost Comparison of International Payment Systems

The traditional international payment system is very complex in India, and transaction costs are also quite high. The Indian banks charge a fee of approximately INR 500 to INR 1,500 for remittance amounts, along with additional charges such as telecommunications fees. For example, SBI or HDFC Bank charges transaction fees based on the remittance amount, which depends on the transaction size and destination. Subsequently, the total cost of each remittance falls in the range of INR 800 to INR 2,000.

Blockchain technology has the potential to significantly simplify the international payment process by reducing payment costs and saving transaction fees for traders. A McKinsey report assesses the introduction of blockchain technology in international payments. It globally decreases transaction costs from nearly $26 per transaction to $15 per transaction, which saves approximately 42% of the amount of both parties involved after the adoption of Ripple blockchain technology in an international payment system. A prominent Indian bank reduced fees from INR 400 per transaction to as low as INR 50. This technology saves users up to 87.5% in fees and greatly minimises payment costs.

Furthermore, a traditional international payment system requires trusted central banks to facilitate payments and settlements between different banks. They are also required to maintain separate margin accounts to minimise transaction risks. Due to the old payment method, a large amount of capital gets stuck for some time, and subsequently, the growth of foreign trade is adversely affected due to the low rate of utilisation of funds. This new method has particularly benefited small and medium-sized enterprises. However, platforms like Ripple eliminate the need for intermediary banks in a blockchain-based international payment system by enabling real-time payments. This approach requires only a single margin account, significantly reducing the amount of capital tied up and enhancing the utilisation rate of funds.

### 17.4.3 Enhancing Security and Trust in International Transactions

The inherent characteristics of blockchain technology ensure the security, transparency, and reduced risks associated with international payments. Blockchain-based payment methods operate on a push-based model, meaning that when a user sends a payment to a merchant, they do not need to provide their personal information or 218bank account details. Instead, the system uses a secure hash function to transmit a hashed value of the encrypted personal information to the merchant. Due to the one-way nature of the hash function, personal information remains protected and is not leaked. Furthermore, Ripple employs anonymous transactions, which offer additional privacy protection for users.

In contrast, traditional payment systems utilise a centralised pull-based method that requires real-name transactions. To complete a transaction, users must provide their personal information and bank account details to the merchant online. The merchant uses this information to withdraw funds. Consequently, the merchant accumulates many users’ sensitive data, such as bank account numbers and other personal transaction details. This private information is vulnerable to being leaked or sold by the merchant and may also be at risk of theft by criminals, leading to substantial losses for users. Thus, adopting a blockchain-based payment system can enhance user privacy and improve the security of online transactions.

In addition, traditional international payments have significant risks due to relying on commercial credit without guarantees. The importers and exporters themselves must manage trade risks. One of the primary challenges is ensuring that the importer’s payment and the exporter’s shipping information are accurate and reliable. Once the importer remits the funds, they cannot track or control the intermediate links in the payment process, nor do they have visibility into the exporter’s shipping status. The trading parties must rely solely on commercial credit for transactions, which leaves them vulnerable to commercial fraud. Even in the absence of fraud, poor business practices or sudden market changes can create bad faith, exacerbating these risks and leading to significant losses for traders. Such issues can erode trust in international trade and severely hinder its growth.

Blockchain technology can mitigate these trade risks in international payments. Participants in a transaction do not require the endorsement of intermediary institutions. Instead, they upload the transaction contract information to the blockchain, where all parties involved in the trade jointly maintain the transaction details and verify consistency. If one party does not confirm the transaction information, the transaction is rendered ineffective. The blockchain system facilitates automatic payments once both the importer and exporter validate the transaction information.

The payment process begins with the importer making a prepayment to the exporter per the contract. The exporter then ships the goods via a logistics company. Upon verifying the cargo information, the logistics company uploads the delivery note to the blockchain network. Once the importer confirms the transaction details on the blockchain, the remaining balance is settled. Therefore, blockchain-based international payments can effectively resolve trust crises, reduce payment risks, save time, expedite corporate funds’ return, and promote the rapid and sustainable development of international trade.

## 17.5 Challenges and Recommendations for Blockchain Implementation

The primary obstacle to implementing blockchain technology in international payment settlements is the inherent limitations of its core technology. Significant challenges still need to be addressed, including energy consumption, credit issues, 219security of encryption algorithms, block capacity, and lengthy transaction confirmation times. These technical hurdles impede the broader adoption of blockchain solutions. As a major trading nation, our country must enhance its efforts in independent research and development of blockchain core technology. Financial technology companies should particularly focus on the research outcomes of related domestic and international industries, investing more human and financial resources to actively develop key technologies and business models for blockchain in international payments. This proactive approach will enable us to establish technical reserves and position ourselves as leaders in the technology era.

The decentralised nature of blockchain presents a stark contrast to the current centralised financial model. Consequently, we incorporate blockchain technology into the international payment system, and it is crucial to establish a corresponding regulatory framework. As we know, no single country or institution independently regulates blockchain technology. Therefore, all financial institutions and regulatory authorities worldwide must collaborate together to formulate a blockchain regulatory strategy that suits global trade settlements. This technology ought to balance supervision, innovation, and practical application.

In order to establish a blockchain-based international payments system, we need to benefit from the UK-based regulatory sandbox mechanism. It allows pilot projects to be run across various sectors. For example, selecting blockchain for the transaction settlement banks within free trade zones for trial runs could provide valuable information. This approach enables researchers or developers to refine technology and regulatory policies before rolling out their larger-scale implementations.

Blockchain technology is inherently multidisciplinary, which requires expertise in mathematics, computer science, cybersecurity, communications engineering, and finance. Nevertheless, India presently requires more high-end talent with this composite skill set. To address this gap, leading universities in India are introducing specialised blockchain courses within their mathematics, computer science, or cybersecurity programs. It is recommended that reputable domestic and international blockchain experts from different organisations be engaged to support talent development. In addition, working together with financial technology companies helps prepare blockchain experts for international business transactions.

In addition, it is necessary to establish a uniform system in industry guidelines for implementing blockchain technology in international trade transactions.

The establishment of these standards fosters healthy competition among enterprises, which improves customer satisfaction. It enhances the effectiveness of financial markets and mitigates potential application risks. In addition, uniform industry standards are necessary to monitor the market and formulate better policies. For this, countries and financial institutions should agree on basic aspects such as blockchain terminology, coding, technical development, and standardisation of products and services.

## 17.6 Conclusion

A blockchain has the potential to enhance processing and reduce inefficiencies in international payments. Blockchain exists as a trusted and decentralised system free of intermediaries. The result is that the costs of transactions, processing time, and the security of financial transactions are also reduced. Blockchain uses cryptography to 220protect user’s privacy and turn to reduce the risk of data breaches and fraud. In addition, blockchain is transparent and immutable; therefore, all transactions are secure and trackable. This also increases the trust in international payment systems, which is quite important when considering international trade and global e-commerce, where banks and payment systems must serve fast and secure payments. However, more research, collaboration, and investment are still needed to fully expose the true power of blockchain in international payment systems. The solutions to the problem of scalability and security in the blockchain space must be done in collaboration between governments and other financial institutions and technology companies to cater for global financial needs. It is a technical challenge that necessitates solving for energy efficiency, compliance with regulations, and a willingness to use innovation to push beyond how the blockchain can be used on a wide and global basis in international payments. But, as it turns out, blockchain is not just a technological change. It is a powerful force behind the future of international payments, bringing greater efficiency and security to the world’s financial systems at a lower cost.

# 18 Blockchain-Driven Solutions for Energy Trading and Sustainability in Microgrids

Mohd Bilal, Ahsan Waseem, Imdadullah, Salman Hameed, and Zenab Elgamal

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-23](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-23)

## 18.1 Introduction

The increase in global energy demand is directly proportional to urbanization and industrialization. Traditional energy systems use fossil fuel-based energy-generating sources, which cause a large emission of greenhouse gases. Rapid increases in demand can only be met by centralized power plants, which are environmentally friendly \[[1](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_1)\]. Generating power plants are situated far from the utilization zone, which causes large transmission losses, making the system less efficient. In many regions, the ageing infrastructure and inefficiency of the transmission system cause higher costs and environmental degradation.

Climate change and international agreements like the Paris Agreement emphasize developing a more renewable energy-based system. Renewable energy sources are intermittent, and their integration is a major concern for transitioning from a centralized energy system to a decentralized energy system. Renewable source-based energy generation depends on the geographical location, wind speed, availability of biomass fuel, and solar irradiance. Integration of these generating units effectively into the grid is a challenge. Secure energy trading from microgrid to grid is also a challenge. This is where decentralized energy systems and blockchain energy trading can revolutionize how energy is traded and used globally \[[2](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_2)\].

Microgrids are the solution to address the rapidly increasing demand while incorporating renewable energy sources. In decentralized systems, various energy sources such as solar photovoltaic (PV), wind turbines, biogas plants, hydropower systems, and energy storage systems like batteries, supercapacitors, and fuel cells are used. Energy generated by these systems is utilized locally, which reduces dependency on the centralized system. The microgrid can be operated on a grid or standalone. Standalone microgrids are important when the power supply to a place is very costly or when energy resilience is critical, such as natural disasters or grid failure. The microgrid can make the island from a utility grid when the central grid is down. The self-sufficiency in microgrids provides energy security to ensure uninterrupted supply during emergencies \[[3](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_3)\].

223Advanced control systems are used in microgrids to allow real-time monitoring and optimization of energy flow and sufficient energy generation to match electrical load demand patterns. When renewable energy sources are insufficient to supply the load demand, energy storage devices must provide the energy needed to meet this deficiency \[[4](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_4)\]. Conversely, when energy production exceeds local consumption, grid-connected microgrids can feed excess power back into the larger grid, contributing to overall grid stability and energy efficiency.

To fully understand the technical aspects of microgrids, it is important to break down their core components.

### 18.1.1 Energy Generation Units

These include renewable sources like solar panels, wind turbines, and sometimes, backup generators that run on fossil fuels or biofuels for reliability. Including diverse energy sources within a microgrid allows for continuous power generation, even when renewable sources experience downtime.

### 18.1.2 Energy Storage Systems

Batteries are the most common form of energy storage in microgrids, allowing excess energy generated during peak production times (e.g., during the day for solar power) to be stored and used later when generation is low or demand is high. Technologies like lithium–ion batteries and emerging energy storage systems (e.g., solid-state and flow batteries) enhance the capacity and durability of energy storage solutions.

### 18.1.3 Power Distribution and Management

The energy generated and stored in a microgrid must be distributed effectively. This is accomplished through a sophisticated control system that monitors energy flows, ensures reliability, and balances supply and demand. Power electronic converters are utilized for AC–DC conversion in PV and batteries for AC loads. [Figure 18.1](#C_025_c18.xhtml#f18_1) shows the energy trading in microgrid based on blockchain technology.

![Figure 18.1 Blockchain-based energy trading in microgrids. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig18_01.jpg)

Figure 18.1 Blockchain-based energy trading in microgrids. [Return to text.⏎](#C_025_c18.xhtml#f18_1b)

Utilizing peer-to-peer (P2P)-based energy trading in microgrids is revolutionary. Traditional consumers purchase power from the utility grid, which gets power from fossil fuel-based power-generating power plants \[[4](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_4)\]. Consumers who produce excess energy and sell it back to the grid are prosumers, as they consume and make the power. Decentralized energy trading can lead to a more dynamic and efficient market. It will lead to the optimization of local energy and promote renewable energy-based generation, which is environmentally friendly.

Blockchain leads to a secure, transparent, and decentralized ledger; transactions in energy trading between prosumers can be done without any intermediary \[[5](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_5)\]. Smart contracts are self-executing contracts in which terms are written directly into the code. These automatically handle the trading of energy when certain predefined conditions arise. Smart contracts offer seamless and low-cost transactions between participants, reducing dependency on traditional utility grid operators \[[6](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_6)\].

224One of the good examples of P2P energy trading in a microgrid is Brooklyn Microgrid, New York. This project has attracted international interest because of the innovative system that is being used to allow local people to buy excess solar energy from each other using a blockchain. People who have installed PV systems on their rooftops can sell excess electricity to their fellow citizens without the involvement of power utilities \[[7](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_7)\]. The Brooklyn Microgrid develops an organized multiplayer energy market that will give numerous communities throughout New York City access to their energy ingestion and help decrease their carbon footprints.

Other successful early implementations exist besides the Brooklyn Microgrid, including Power Ledger of Australia. These systems promote the generation and utilization of renewable energy, less centralization of power generation, and hence inferior inefficiency as well as expenditures of conventional power generation and transmission \[[8](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_8)\].

Nevertheless, numerous studies recognize that utility-scale integration of microgrids is not without problems. First, there are high fixed costs when installing renewable energy power generation equipment like solar/Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) panels, windmills, and energy storage system equipment. However, these technologies have come down in the recent past; the initial costs of infrastructure, control systems, and periodic maintenance could still add up and pose a significant cost burden, often to developing regions or small communities.

Government subsidies, Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs), and new innovative concepts like energy as a service platforms have assisted in some of these financial 225costs. However, to achieve broad penetration of microgrids, further reductions in the price of microgrid technologies are necessary, alongside improvement in awareness about the available financing options \[[9](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_9)\].

Second, there are such barriers as geographical and environmental factors. Some areas could be more conducive to renewable energy production. For instance, areas close to the seashore are likely to record high wind speeds, hence the suitability of wind turbines. At the same time, the mountainous or strongly wooded significantly lack direct sunlight, making solar panels suitable. In these cases, feasibility studies must be conducted to determine more appropriate energy generation methods on the particular sites.

Furthermore, using microgrids connected to the conventional centralized grid can pose legal issues and policies. Utilities and grid operators are typically slow to utilize decentralized systems because they threaten their current approaches. There is an increasing need for regulatory changes to allow new approaches in energy trading to cater to microgrids and P2P trading platforms.

Another challenge is the technological factor: the complexity of microgrids’ technological systems and the technical know-how needed to run and support microgrids. Microgrid operators must have sufficient technical background to monitor the immediate dispatch of power, recharging, and grid synchronization \[[10](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_10)\]. For example, coastal areas might experience high wind speeds, making wind turbines ideal, but mountainous or heavily forested areas may have limited sunlight exposure, hindering solar panel efficiency. In these cases, site-specific assessments must be conducted to determine the best energy generation options.

Integrating microgrids with the existing centralized grid can create regulatory and policy challenges. Utilities and grid operators are often reluctant to embrace decentralized energy systems because they disrupt traditional business models. Regulatory frameworks must be updated to encourage innovation and accommodate new energy trading models that enable microgrids and P2P trading platforms to thrive.

Microgrids have certain challenges, and though they degrade their current and near-future picture, the future is incredibly bright for these systems. As costs for renewable energy technologies reduce and issues related to climate change become more well-known, microgrids are likely to emerge as one of the primary infrastructures for the future power system. Microgrids, providing localized generation and consumption of power, are a sustainable and low-vulnerability option to traditional centralized systems. The microgrid offers added advantages, including reduced greenhouse emissions, minimal transmission losses, and enhanced air quality, meeting the global objectives of combating climate change. Moreover, microgrids could be a source of new investment opportunities and fresh revenue generation for rural or other areas that experience energy deficits, thereby stimulating local energy generation and commerce.

The remaining chapter is organized as follows: [Section 18.2](#C_025_c18.xhtml#h18_2) describes P2P energy trading, [Section 18.3](#C_025_c18.xhtml#h18_3) highlights the working methodology of blockchain technology, [Section 18.4](#C_025_c18.xhtml#h18_4) explores advancing sustainability through cryptocurrency-enabled energy trading, and [Section 18.5](#C_025_c18.xhtml#h18_5) concludes the chapter.

226 

## 18.2 Peer-to-Peer Energy Trading: Blockchain and Cryptocurrency

### 18.2.1 Blockchain and Cryptocurrency in Energy

Blockchain is another technology that has gained prominence with the popular use of coins such as bitcoins, and it has inherent characteristics that best fit into energy markets. A blockchain is a distributed database framework that establishes secure and transparent digital transactions without intermediaries \[[11](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_11)\]. It is also important in energy markets, where intermediaries like utilities or grid operators control the flow of electricity and money.

In a decentralized energy system, blockchain can provide the platform to perform energy trading through P2P where prosumers, which means the producer and consumer, can easily trade excess electricity with consumers \[[12](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_12)\]. Smart contracts, with programmed and self-implementing terms, accomplish these transactions to make energy transferred and paid instantaneously. Lastly, energy trading is protected and transparent due to the decentralized nature of blockchain \[[13](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_13)\]. Transaction data’s immutability is secured from manipulation by cryptographic hashing and consensus protocols. However, through the minimization of intermediaries, blockchain has been able to minimize transaction discounts cost and optimizes the operational aspects of energy trading.

### 18.2.2 Role of Smart Contracts in Energy Markets

One of the most important elements of blockchain technology in energy trading is smart contracts. These smart contracts are calculated or agreed to be performed automatically once specific pre-stipulated conditions are met. For instance, a smart contract can sell extra electricity produced by the prosumer’s solar panel and store it in a battery once it is full. Likewise, for instance, a consumer can sell electricity to them using the smart contract when the prices drops below a certain level \[[14](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_14)\]. Smart contracts remove the need for human intervention in energy trading, making transactions more efficient and less prone to error. In addition, because smart contracts are self-executing, they reduce the potential for disputes between parties, as the blockchain automatically enforces the contract terms. This automation can dramatically reduce the operational costs associated with energy trading \[[15](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_15)\]. [Table 18.1](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_1) summarizes the key aspects of energy trading in microgrids and the role of blockchain technology, highlighting its benefits (e.g., decentralization, cost reduction) and challenges (e.g., scalability, regulatory barriers).

Table 18.1 Key Aspects of Energy Trading in Microgrids and the Role of Blockchain Technology [Return to text.⏎](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_1b)

 
| Aspect || Description || Applications || Limitations || Uniqueness |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Decentralization || Microgrids operate locally, independently, or connected to the main grid. || Localized energy generation and consumption. || Coordination with the central grid can be complex. || Reduces dependence on central utilities. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Localized energy generation || Uses local renewable sources like solar and wind. || Lower transmission losses and enhanced energy autonomy. || Variability in renewable energy production || Promotes sustainable energy practices |
| Flexibility and resilience || Adapts to changes in energy needs and conditions and provides backup power. || Enhances grid reliability and rapid response || Requires robust infrastructure and management. || Improves grid stability and response to outages |
| Peer-to-peer trading || Direct transactions between participants without intermediaries. || Personalized and efficient energy transactions. || Regulatory and technical challenges in scaling. || Empower local energy markets and prosumers. |
| Secure transactions (blockchain) || Blockchain provides a decentralized, tamper-proof ledger || Ensures security and transparency in trading. || Potential for scalability and energy consumption issues. || Enhances trust and reduces fraud. |
| Smart contracts (blockchain) || Automated, self-executing contracts with terms written into code. || Automates and enforces trading agreements. || Complexity in designing and managing contracts. || Reduces administrative overhead and intermediary costs |
| Transparency and trust (blockchain) || Immutable records enhance transparency and verifiability. || Builds trust among participants and ensures fairness. || Requires comprehensive data management. || Provides an auditable and verifiable trading environment |
| Efficient settlement (blockchain) || Real-time transaction settlement reduces delays. || Improves cash flow and transaction efficiency. || Dependence on blockchain infrastructure stability. || Accelerates financial transactions and liquidity. |
| Cost reduction (blockchain) || Lower transaction fees and operational costs by eliminating intermediaries. || Reduces overall costs for energy trading. || Initial implementation and integration costs || It makes energy trading more economically viable. |
| Enhanced scalability (blockchain) || Blockchain supports scalable trading systems as the network grows. || Handles increased transaction volumes || Scaling can lead to increased energy consumption || Adapts to expanding microgrid networks |

### 18.2.3 Energy Tokenization and the Emergence of Energy Cryptocurrencies

Tokenization is one of the important parts of blockchain regarding energy trading. It converts the physical energy (such as Kilowatt-hours) into digital tokens, which can be used for trading in blockchain. These tokens represent a quantity of electricity and can be exchanged by users in the decentralized market. It enables the creation of energy-specific cryptocurrency, which can be used for energy trading \[[16](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_16)\].

227228Power Ledger and major innovations enable users to buy and sell energy tokens directly without using traditional exchange techniques. These energy tokens can also be stored or transferred globally, allowing users to trade energy across borders seamlessly \[[17](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_17)\].

Moreover, tokenization provides a new investment opportunity for individuals and businesses. By purchasing energy tokens, investors can effectively fund renewable energy projects and benefit from the future sale of energy generated by those projects. This promotes the expansion of renewable energy and democratizes access to energy investments, allowing every day consumers to participate in energy markets \[[18](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_18)\].

### 18.2.4 Currency Mining and Energy Consumption

While blockchain technology offers numerous benefits for energy markets, it is important to acknowledge some of the challenges, particularly the energy-intensive nature of crypto currency mining. Mining is the process by which new transactions are verified and added to the blockchain. In networks like Bitcoin, mining involves solving complex mathematical problems, a process known as “proof-of-work,” which requires significant computational power and, consequently, a substantial amount of electricity.

The high energy consumption of proof-of-work mining has raised concerns about the environmental impact of blockchain technology \[[19](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_19)\]. Take the example of Bitcoin, which alone consumes more energy over the years than some small nations, leading to the criticism that cryptocurrency mining contributes to the global energy demand instead of reducing it.

Since the blockchain is a leading technology and is helping in exploring more energy-efficient consensus mechanisms to address issues, Proof-of-Stake is one of the mechanisms that is significantly reducing the energy consumption by the user to “stake” a portion of their cryptocurrency holdings to confirm the transactions, instead of relying on energy-intensive consumption \[[18](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_18)\]. Ethereum’s second-largest blockchain platform has recently transitioned to a proof-of-stake model by reducing energy consumption by more than 99%.

Furthermore, some blockchain projects are even exploring the idea of “green mining,” using renewable energy sources to run their mining operations. These projects aim to reduce the environmental impact of cryptocurrency mining by utilizing solar, wind, or hydro-powered sources in combination with mining infrastructure to combine blockchain sustainability with wider sustainability objectives, security, and privacy in blockchain-based energy markets \[[19](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_19)\].

One of the essential benefits of blockchain technology in energy markets is that it affords elevated security—existing energy systems store transaction records in centralized databases and servers, which are susceptible to hacks and breaches. However, the distributed ledger of blockchain decentralizes the storage of transaction data across different nodes, which eliminates any single point of failure risk. For this reason, energy markets based on blockchain are, by nature, more secure.

In addition, blockchain employs state-of-the-art cryptography to secure data, making all transactions encrypted and human-verifiable. This maintains the integrity 229of the energy trading process and helps users improve their privacy. In legacy energy markets, customer data are usually centralized, and many players have access to it, opening the door to possible unauthorized data access. Blockchain provides a method where users can have more ownership and control of their data: only those with the correct cryptographic keys can access the information.

### 18.2.5 Role in Driving Energy Market Transparency

Another important aspect of blockchain in energy markets is transparency. Information regarding electricity generation, distribution, and pricing exists in silos and is controlled by centralized entities in traditional energy systems; thus, consumers find it difficult to access accurate information in real-time. So, this opacity triggers price manipulation and price dysfunctions and creates a trust gap in the energy market. By its nature, blockchain is decentralized, meaning all the participants have the same information simultaneously. This transparency allows consumers to make informed decisions regarding energy consumption and purchases \[[20](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_20)\]. For instance, consumers can observe the origin of the electricity they consume, whether from renewable or fossil energy sources, and the price of electricity in real-time.

## 18.3 Working with Blockchain in Microgrid Energy Trading

### 18.3.1 How Blockchain Works in Trading

Blockchain technology profoundly affects the operation of energy markets. In its most basic form, blockchain is a decentralized ledger that records transactions openly and securely across multiple computers \[[21](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_21)\]. Applying this to energy trading, blockchain facilitates the direct exchange of electricity between prosumers and consumers, removing intermediaries such as utility companies. Every transaction in a blockchain energy market is documented on a distributed ledger, which is open to all parties. This transparency makes it possible for all parties to trust that transactions are done correctly and protected by cryptographic hashing, making them unchangeable and tamper-proof. Smart contracts are an important part of energy transaction automation on blockchain platforms. These contracts are self-executing and get executed automatically when the predefined conditions are met \[[22](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_22)\]. For instance, if a prosumer has excess electricity and another consumer needs energy, a smart contract can facilitate the transaction automatically, transferring the energy and payment simultaneously.

### 18.3.2 Peer-to-Peer Trading Models

P2P energy trading is an innovative model in which individuals exchange electricity among themselves by buying and selling directly, bypassing the traditional utility companies. Several blockchain-based platforms have pioneered the idea of P2P energy trading, such as LO3 Energy and Power Ledger \[[23](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_23)\]. These allow prosumers to monetize their surplus energy through a blockchain-based marketplace, whereby they sell directly to nearby consumers.

230Several potential benefits can be realized by P2P energy trading as opposed to the traditional energy markets, such as low transaction costs, high efficiency, and transparency improvements. It provides for more locally based energy production and consumption; thus, transmission losses are reduced while the grid’s resilience increases. [Table 18.2](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_2) provides a comparative analysis of blockchain-based technologies in energy trading, showcasing their applications (e.g., Power Ledger for P2P trading), limitations (e.g., regulatory barriers), and unique contributions to sustainability (e.g., smart contract automation).

Table 18.2 Blockchain-Based Technologies in Energy Trading and Sustainability \[[22](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_22), [24](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_24), [25](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_25), [26](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_26), [27](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_27), [28](#C_025_c18.xhtml#r18_28)\] [Return to text.⏎](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_2b)

 
| Technology Name || Application || Limitation || Unique Aspect |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Power Ledger || The P2P energy trading platform for microgrids allows prosumers to trade excess renewable energy. || Regulatory barriers in some regions and scalability concerns due to blockchain energy usage. || Smart contract-based automated trading; transparent energy transactions. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Brooklyn Microgrid || Community-based microgrid using blockchain for local solar energy trading between neighbours. || It is limited to small-scale communities; regulatory challenges in scaling. || Local energy independence promotes the adoption of renewable energy sources. |
| Grid+ || Decentralized and Autonomous (DeA) centralized energy market enables real-time energy pricing and trading for microgrid participants. || Relatively high operational costs for blockchain networks. || Real-time energy pricing; efficient energy use within microgrids. |
| WePower || A renewable energy trading platform connecting green energy producers with consumers uses blockchain. || Integrating with traditional grids is complex; adoption could be faster in some regions. || Tokenization of energy increases liquidity and accessibility of renewable energy. |
| SunContract || The P2P energy marketplace enables users to buy and sell renewable energy using blockchain. || Dependent on national regulatory frameworks and policies. || Direct connection between renewable producers and consumers; no middlemen. |
| Electrify || Facilitates energy trading between distributed energy resources within microgrids. || Limited pilot programs need broader integration into national grids. || Enables competition in energy pricing through blockchain; scalable for multiple energy sources. |
| TransActive Grid || Blockchain-based energy platform focusing on localized energy sharing in microgrids. || Needs advanced infrastructure like smart meters for full functionality. || Real-time energy distribution and trading; community-driven sustainability model. |
| Energy Web Chain || Open-source blockchain platform to accelerate low-carbon energy transition and decentralized trading. || High computational power requirements for blockchain operations. || Designed specifically for the energy sector, it focuses on regulatory compliance and decarbonization. |
| Share&Charge || Blockchain technology enables P2P trading of energy for electric vehicle charging within microgrids. || Limited to electric vehicle-related infrastructure; still emerging. || Facilitates the integration of electric vehicles with renewable microgrids. |
| LO3 Energy || Develops blockchain-based platforms for decentralized energy exchanges in community microgrids. || Early-stage development; challenges with scalability across large grids. || Community-driven energy trading; decentralized control over energy sources. |

## 18.4 Advancing Sustainability Through Cryptocurrency-Enabled Energy Trading

Cryptocurrency-enabled trading, powered by blockchain, big data, and artificial intelligence, represents a revolutionary approach to sustainability by facilitating the trading of surplus renewable energy in their preferred between customers—organizations that produce and use energy this alternative, such as solar and wind. Renewable energy adoption encourages and reduces reliance on fossil fuels and increases CO[2] emissions. Real-time energy trading improves the integration and quality of renewable energy, reduces energy waste, and contributes to grid efficiency. Case studies from India and Africa highlight the impact of blockchain-based microgrids in providing electricity, reducing carbon footprints, and generating economic growth in underserved areas. Furthermore, strategies of green mining and decentralized blockchain systems balance the production and consumption of energy and encourage the use of energy so that our environment and surroundings reinforce the role of cryptocurrency in sustainability. [Table 18.3](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_3) describes the environmental impacts of cryptocurrency-enabled energy trading.

Table 18.3 Environmental Impacts of Cryptocurrency-Enabled Energy Trading [Return to text.⏎](#C_025_c18.xhtml#t18_3b)

 
| Aspect || Description || Impact on Sustainability |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Carbon emissions reduction || Facilitates the use of renewable energy, reducing reliance on fossil fuels. || Decreases greenhouse gas emissions and carbon footprint. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Renewable energy integration || Optimizes the use of renewable energy sources through real-time trading. || Enhances the efficiency of renewable energy use. |
| Decentralization of energy production || Encourages localized energy production and consumption. || Reduces the need for centralized fossil-fuel-based generation. |
| Energy access (Africa) || Blockchain-based systems in Africa support renewable energy adoption and local trading. || Contributes to local economic development and sustainability. |
| Green mining || Utilizes renewable energy for crypto currency mining to reduce environmental impact. || Lowers the carbon footprint of mining operations. |

## 18.5 Conclusion

In this chapter, we have reviewed vast literature on the use of blockchain in microgrid decentralized energy trading. The study investigated how blockchain allows for P2P energy trading, improving the transparency, efficiency, and automation of transactions with smart contracts. This emphasized blockchain in renewable energy integration, providing grid flexibility and distributed energy autonomy. It also explored the advantages of time-of-use pricing and integration of variable renewable energy resources at the microgrid level.

It also points out the major issues, such as the scalability of blockchain networks, the energy consumption of traditional consensus mechanisms, and the regulatory complexities. It does so by critically examining these topics, emphasizing the need for more energy-efficient blockchain models, and inviting regulatory frameworks that will allow for decentralized trading. The work establishes a basis for future studies to investigate the technology of artificial intelligence and Internet of Things used in conjunction with blockchain to optimize the microgrid system and to evolve sustainability and the energy market.231
