# 19 Blockchain for Social Impact Shaping Future Management Practices

Richa Verma, Utkarsh Kumar Verma, Kalpana Rawat, Richa Srivastava, Arvind Mewada, Nagendra Singh, and Mohd. Aquib Ansari

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-24](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-24)

## 19.1 Introduction

Blockchain technology, known for its decentralised digital ledger, has brought significant changes to various industries since the beginning of the Internet era. It offers a secure method for recording and verifying transactions across a network without third-party intermediaries, enhancing transparency and security \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\]. Using cryptographic principles, blockchain creates a verified transaction history distributed across multiple network nodes. Recently, this technology has seen increasing application in social impact projects, prompting a reassessment of traditional governance, finance, and corporate social responsibility practices.

Blockchain is a distributed ledger technology that works on a peer-to-peer network where people can record and verify information safely and securely \[[2](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_2)\]. Among these features, decentralisation and immutability are the outstanding features. Decentralisation is defined as no central authority body giving control to a network of nodes. It prevents any entity from changing data and assures participant trust \[[3](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_3)\]. Over these raw themes, blockchain promotes transparency because each peer on the network is equipped with the same latest updated information at a real-time level. This openness can facilitate creating trust in systems that used to involve abstruse processes, which left avenues open for inefficiency or even some kind of abuse in the past \[[4](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_4)\].

### 19.1.1 Understanding Social Impact

Social impact can be described as the relative impact of any innovation or venture on society and its institutions- the relative effects on social welfare, equality, or justice. Technology’s social impact looks into how blockchain technology results in positive social outcomes that manifest from greater openness, more decentralised control, and enhanced access to necessary services \[[5](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_5)\]. Furthermore, it allows everyone 236access to full-fledged financial services in a traditionally bankless economic system by ensuring all can transfer money securely from peer to peer \[[6](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_6)\]. It can also raise the transparency and accessibility of services in sectors like health and education. For instance, blockchain technology used in healthcare can hasten patient data management; thereby, patients can easily manage their medical records while helping healthcare providers deliver better care \[[7](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_7)\]. Similarly, using blockchain to validate academic credentials in education can make it easier to share and authenticate qualifications across global networks.

### 19.1.2 Blockchain Meets Management Practice

Blockchain implementation into management practice has radical implications. Traditionally, the management system tended to be hierarchical, with commands on information flow and decision-making given by centralised authorities. Blockchain re-exposes all these traditional models with a decentralised peer-to-peer network for delegating control \[[2](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_2)\]. This decentralisation allows for more democratic management frameworks, enabling stakeholders to better view the goings-on in the decision-making processes and operations \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\]. Within corporate settings, blockchain technology can improve the transparency of supply chains, strengthen CSR efforts, and ensure accountability across business operations. For example, blockchain-based supply chains will be able to track products from the starting points to the end, thus ensuring ethical sourcing practices and reducing inefficiencies in distribution \[[8](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_8)\]. Other than the above, blockchain provides transparency regarding CSR activities. The reason is that the activity records are maintained to check for any company initiatives that enhance the stakeholders’ trust \[[9](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_9)\].

## 19.2 Objectives and Outline

The chapter highlights the following objectives:

*   To explain the blockchain, elucidate its fundamental principles, and outline its most crucial issues.
*   To discuss how blockchain can make a social impact in the governance, healthcare, finance, and education sectors.
*   To analyse the transformative potential of blockchain in reframing the management attitude towards more openness, decentralisation, and inclusiveness in decision-making systems.
*   To analyse issues and ethical dilemmas around integrating the technology of blockchain.

## 19.3 Blockchain's Social Impact

*   **A Governance Transformation:** Blockchain technology has significantly changed financial systems and, thus, introduced new governance approaches towards empowering individuals and communities through decentralised power structures. By establishing a distributed control model and 237institutionalising transparency, blockchain enables a structure for increased accountability and equity. The section discusses how blockchain’s distributed nature transforms traditional governance structures and pushes social empowerment through inclusion, trust, fraud prevention, and access to essential services.

### 19.3.1 Decentralisation and Its Role in Social Empowerment

Decentralisation is the most transformative aspect of blockchain technology. Traditionally, centralised institutions have held power and jurisdiction, often leading to inefficiencies, corruption, and exclusion \[[10](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_10)\].

### 19.3.2 Recasting Tradition Governance Mechanisms

Decentralisation inherently changes governance mechanisms as no intermediaries or central authorities are required to make decisions. According to \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\], because blockchain is inherently a network that makes decisions collectively, no one will be allowed to throttle the control mechanism in the decision-making process. From a top-down governance model, through a peer-to-peer network model, people and their groups are vested with much broader influence in the decisions that most impact them.

### 19.3.3 Inclusivity in Decision-Making

Blockchain technology allows all participants equal decision-making shares. It removes middlemen and offers unfettered access that other groups would not have had because of financial or bureaucratic constraints, according to \[[5](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_5)\].

### 19.3.4 Enhancing Transparency and Accountability

Blockchain technology improves transparency and accountability in many sectors. It does this by providing immutable and verifiable records of transactions and decisions. Blockchain is distributed to a network where every transaction is chronologically recorded and available to all. This feature makes alteration nearly impossible without majority consensus. Therefore, blockchain technology fosters greater accountability \[[4](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_4)\].

### 19.3.5 Blockchain as a Mechanism for Trust Building

Trust can be characterised as one of the most fundamental challenges of modern governance and management structures. Blockchain technology comes in handy by generating an immutable record of transactions that is visible to all network members. This makes it impossible to cheat or corrupt since any transaction gets an open and irreversible record. It is from such low-trust environments that public services consider; in them, opaque processes have often led to the erosion of public trust within governance structures. Fraud and corruption reduction through 238decentralised ledgers has been pivotal, with blockchain’s decentralised ledger ensuring that transactions are transparent and verifiable \[[8](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_8)\]. All the above, any such fraudulent parties can detect and curb fraudulent actions since they must agree on validating a transaction before it hits the blockchain. For this reason, blockchain not only deters corrupt behaviours but also gives rise to transparent and accountable governance. This integrity is essential for the public to be confident in governmental and corporate systems \[[2](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_2)\].

### 19.3.6 Role of Blockchain in Alleviation of Social Inequality

Blockchain technology can significantly reduce social inequities by democratising access to financial systems and essential services. Traditional financial and governance models often exclude specific societal segments, affecting economic and social development. With its decentralised and all-inclusive methodology, blockchain offers new avenues for financial inclusion and egalitarian service access.

### 19.3.7 Financial Inclusion

Blockchain innovation financial inclusion is another area where blockchain plays a significant role. Since blockchain allows peers to transact with each other without banks directly, it also provides exposure to immense benefits offered to unbanked individuals, particularly in developing countries \[[11](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_11)\]. By affording a safe digital space to facilitate transactions, allowing those without access to traditional banking facilities to participate in economic activities becomes possible, leading to total financial inclusion \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\].

### 19.3.8 Enabling Equal Access to Services in Underserved Communities

Blockchain technology promotes financial inclusion and enables access to essential services in underserved communities. For example, it can securely keep and verify personal identification, land titles, and learning documents. That, in turn, assists in one of the problems of acquiring said documentation in cases with difficulty in logistics or corruption problem issues \[[12](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_12)\]. Furthermore, blockchain technology enables patients to maintain and share health information securely; therefore, it increases the effectiveness of healthcare delivery while reducing health access inequities \[[7](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_7)\].

### 19.3.9 Blockchain and Management Practice Integration

Blockchain technology quickly rearranges technical and financial systems and fundamental business management processes. Its decentralised nature, transparency, accountability, and security allow one to rethink traditional management frameworks. Integrating blockchain into management practice transforms organisational governance and stakeholder management, contributing to corporate social responsibility and social impact investing.

239 

### 19.3.10 Reconsidering Organisational Governance

Current forms of organisational governance are typified by structures where authority is centralised at the topmost level. Such frameworks are often riddled with problems of inefficiency, transparency problems, and decision-making processes that could be more convenient and take ages. However, the decentralised method of blockchain breaks the mould of conventional models where decisions may be dispersed over nodes or participants.

### 19.3.11 Distributed Decision-Making

In traditional corporate settings, decision-making is time-consuming, cumbersome, and involves approval from various hierarchical levels. The blockchain distributes decision-making among stakeholders instead. Through decentralised decisions, blockchain technology enables employees and partners, among other interested persons, to be actively involved in the governance process. The shift towards democratised decisions leads to inclusiveness and fairness in the organisational hierarchy, giving participants a say in crucial matters. Moreover, it decreases the probability of having a few individuals decide and decreases conflict of interest \[[13](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_13)\].

### 19.3.12 Stakeholder Management and the Blockchain

Stakeholder management typically involves the sensitive balancing of interests among numerous parties, such as employees, customers, investors, and regulators. Blockchain enhances stakeholder management by providing a transparent and immutable record of all engagement and transactions. That way, through transparency, all the stakeholders will have the same information, reducing the chance of miscommunication or tussles \[[8](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_8)\]. Furthermore, blockchain technology enhances stakeholder participation. In a decentralised system, stakeholders can voice their apprehensions about essential decisions by voting or presenting views on critical issues to ensure their interest is addressed. Shareholders may be able to participate at the policy-making level to determine company-level decisions or how a resource would be allocated in a blockchain network, which is then recorded on the blockchain \[[14](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_14)\].

This increases transparency and builds trust among stakeholders, who can verify that their contributions have been recognised. In addition, blockchain hastens stakeholder involvement through the automatic handling of the dividend disbursal process or profit-sharing processes. Automated discharge of dividends or profit-sharing functions can be capitalised on through smart contracts to automatically transfer funds to the stakeholders under certain conditions, thus facilitating accurate and timely payments \[[2](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_2)\]. With this automation in place, there is a reduction in administrative burdens because the processes relating to the management of stakeholders become effective and efficient \[[15](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_15)\].

### 19.3.13 Blockchain and CSR

In most CSR initiatives, issues with transparency and accountability arise as more stakeholders require extensive proof of a firm’s impact on society and the environment.

240Transparency is the foundation of successful CSR initiatives. Companies should prove the authenticity of their CSR activities instead of just treating them as promotional tools. Blockchain can be used in this regard. Blockchain can help a company trace and authenticate its entire products or service life cycle and thus authenticate CSR claims with verifiable data \[[8](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_8)\]. This transparency instils trust among consumers, investors, and other stakeholders by providing the means to verify the truth behind a company’s claims visibly. In addition, the application of blockchain technology can also prevent such incidents as greenwashing, which involves falsely exaggerating or even fabricating environmental credentials by a company, thus sustaining the legitimacy of a CSR program, according to \[[9](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_9)\]. Blockchain can prove the money distribution in the CSR programs. For instance, if a company states that it will distribute a proportion of its profit to charity, blockchain can document the money flow and ensure that the money ends up in the target recipient’s pockets \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\]. The record in a blockchain places more legitimacy on the CSR activities, hence making companies responsible for their assertions \[[16](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_16)\].

### 19.3.14 Enhancing CSR Reporting Accountability

Investing has undergone significant transformation in recent years, with an increasing focus on creating social and environmental benefits alongside financial returns. Blockchain technology has become pivotal in ensuring transparency, accountability, and efficiency in managing social impact investments \[[17](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_17)\]. Investors can directly and securely support project-level or company-level initiatives by leveraging blockchain-based platforms. These platforms enable decentralised investments, often using cryptocurrencies or tokens to issue shares in projects or fund specific social initiatives \[[18](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_18)\]. For example, blockchain platforms can finance renewable energy projects by providing detailed, immutable records of each investment \[[19](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_19)\]. Blockchain integration addresses challenges often present in traditional investment frameworks, introducing unparalleled transparency and security. This transparency fosters trust among stakeholders and ensures that the impact of investments is accurately measured and verified \[[1](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_1)\]. Blockchain’s ability to create an immutable history of transactions allows investors to track the impact of their contributions over time \[[18](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_18)\].

Furthermore, automating impact assessment and reporting processes reduces administrative costs, enhancing efficiency \[[15](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_15)\]. The success of such systems hinges on the quality of data input and storage within the blockchain, enabling data-driven decisions to refine strategies where necessary. Ultimately, the transparency and accountability facilitated by blockchain can ensure that social impact investments achieve their intended outcomes, delivering measurable and sustainable benefits \[[16](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_16)\].

## 19.4 Benefits of Blockchain Technology

In this part, we discuss the benefits of blockchain technology across industries.

### 19.4.1 Blockchain in SCM

Supply Chain Management (SCM) comprises complex networks with numerous mediators. Such a process has been chiefly inefficient, delayed, and non-transparent. 241Blockchain tries overhauling supply chains by using live track records, guaranteed authentication, and building stakeholder trust. By providing unalterable registers, blockchain ensures equal access to information for any participant in the supply chain by reducing fraud, counterfeit, and unscrupulous sourcing.

### 19.4.2 Traceability and Ethical Sourcing

Blockchain has proven valid in offering traceability of products and ethically sourced products. This is of significant importance, and the challenge is based on consumer trust, making several ample food and retail companies embrace it. For instance, in collaboration with Walmart, IBM developed a blockchain-based food platform called the Food Trust. In 2018, the company made all suppliers in the leafy green category sign on to its blockchain network to strengthen food safety. The move helped the company trace contaminated products within seconds rather than days, reducing the risks associated with foodborne illnesses \[[20](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_20)\].

In addition, the collaboration brought about transparency as the customers can obtain truthful information concerning the history of the product; in this case, it is sourced from an ethical perspective. Nestlé also applied blockchain to its supply chain management. The company launched a blockchain system with Open SC that tracks from New Zealand farms and further down to the warehouses in the Middle East. This enables Nestlé to ensure that safety standards and ethical sourcing requirements are met at every point in the supply chain \[[21](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_21)\]. These examples illustrate how blockchain technology improves transparency in supply chains, ensuring responsible sourcing and meeting the ever-growing demand by consumers for ethically sourced products.

### 19.4.3 Cost Savings and Supply Chain Efficiency

Blockchain technology benefits business sectors as it becomes more transparent and efficient. For example, Maersk and IBM collaborated to develop Trade Lens, a real-time container tracking system. This enables players in the supply chain to collaborate. With automated documentation processes and digitisation of paperwork, Maerske was able to reduce costs while simultaneously improving operational efficiency in shipping. De Beers applied technology with Tracr, too, in its diamonds’ traceability so that they appear authentic and sourced ethically. This program provides transparency and functional improvements by tracing diamond origin in real-time. These illustrations explain how the future of blockchain innovation could reduce cost, expedite processes, and improve safety in supply chain procedures.

### 19.4.4 Blockchain Impact on Management of Security in Health Records

Health is characterised by the volume of information that should be managed securely and effectively. The most essential healthcare issue is the clear assurance about properly managing healthcare records. Blockchain technology allows healthcare patients to safely manage their health data while allowing authorised providers access to records. A representative example in the healthcare industry is the Medi Ledger 242Project, an initiative undertaken by drug majors such as Pfizer and Genentech that uses blockchain technology to authenticate and track drugs so that spurious medications cannot find their way into the supply chain \[[22](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_22)\].

### 19.4.5 Blockchain Ensuring Better Security to Health Records and Patient Confidentiality

Blockchain alleviates these issues by providing a decentralised and secure method of managing health records, significantly limiting the chance of unauthorised access or data tampering. Estonia has used a blockchain-based system headed by the e-Health Foundation to secure health records for its citizens. This blockchain framework ensures that all interactions between patients and providers are recorded providently in an immutable ledger, ensuring full transparency and accountability \[[22](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_22)\]. Estonia’s blockchain solution ensures that any particular health record is accessible only to authorised parties, significantly improving patient privacy without compromising transparency. The firm based in Estonia, specialising in blockchain technology, Guard Time, has developed the KSI Blockchain, which is used to protect medical records and the integrity of information in most healthcare systems. The blockchain product from Guard Time ensures that medical records are secure by preventing data breaches and ensuring information about patients is kept as secret.

### 19.4.6 Education: Blockchain-Based Credentials and Accessibility

Blockchain technology has much to say for the education sector, primarily verifying academic credentials and enhancing accessibility in education. This technology ensures integrity via a blockchain-based secure ledger of educational accomplishments, resisting tampering and allowing easy verification processes. This not only eases procedures intended to reduce fraud but also cuts down on the hassle quotient entailed by verifications by employers or institutions of education.

### 19.4.7 Verification of Educational Documents with Blockchain Technology

Verifying educational credentials is lengthy, costly, and, in most cases, inaccurate. Blockchain technologies will soon end this situation when academic institutions can verify the authenticity of digital diplomas and certificates on an irreversible ledger. MIT has pioneered this innovation by introducing its Digital Diploma Initiative, which allows graduates to obtain blockchain-backed digital diplomas. This technological advancement enables the recruiter to verify qualifications easily and guarantees the proper hiring of people with legal certificates. Sony Global Education ventured into blockchain by creating a platform that, among other things, would assist in protecting and transferring academic data. This ground-breaking platform enables students to share their official, authenticated academic achievements with relevant organisations and employers across the globe to ease the processing of admissions and recruitment \[[23](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_23)\].

243 

### 19.4.8 Blockchain as the Answer to Expanding Access to Education in Developing Regions

Blockchain verifies the authenticity of documents and significantly widens educational opportunities, especially in far-flung places restricted from accessing regular education systems. Blockchain allows students to be part of international educational structures, and this is made possible through decentralised systems for checking students’ records and authenticating attendance and other grades. This adoption is seen through African Broadcasting Union (ABU) using blockchain to track students’ files and provide academic materials to students across African nations. Students can showcase their credentials to prospective employers and educational bodies through the blockchain-based platform accessible through ABU, thus equipping them with the resources necessary to gain a suitable advantage on global playing fields \[[24](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_24)\].

### 19.4.9 Design of Social Business Models through Blockchain

Blockchain technology helps social entrepreneurs design decentralised business models that empower communities and amplify the sense of transparency. Blockchain technology ensures efficient allocation of resources and transparent operations, thus creating trust and accountable stakeholders. For example, BanQu is a blockchain technology that allows poor people to build an economic identity based on recorded transactions. BanQu enables marginalised groups to enhance their livelihoods through access to financial services and global supply chains without being involved in corrupt practices in handling resources; instead, such practice is transparent \[[19](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_19)\]. This platform connects smallholder farmers and farmers with global markets, facilitating fair trade practices and economic opportunities. In addition, the blockchain-based-provenance platform Provenance supports social entrepreneurs by making transparent and traceable supply chain solutions available for producing socially beneficial products. Through Provenance, consumers can trace the product origin and the product while confirming positive impact support for the ethical businesses assured to be supported \[[25](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_25)\].

### 19.4.10 Crowdfunding and Impact Tracking via Decentralised Platforms

The social entrepreneur can access an investor community globally via decentralised crowdfunding, whereas full transparency in funds use can be guaranteed. Using such a website, donors can see where their money goes through a public, decentralised ledger; that way, all distributions of funds meet the goals of donors \[[6](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_6)\]. This is meant to create trust and transparency on the part of the donor, who gets to view the projects they are funding in real-time. Most importantly, blockchain technology helps track the social and environmental impact behind every undertaking with tremendous accuracy. This facilitates accurate data being provided to patrons and stakeholders, elevating credibility.

244 

### 19.4.11 Challenges and Ethical Considerations in Blockchain Implementation

Blockchain technology has revolutionised many industries with decentralised, transparent, and secure systems. Besides its promising benefits, blockchain implementation faces significant challenges and ethical issues. These include privacy concerns, the challenge of legal and regulatory problems, ecological impacts, and the ethical use of blockchain in vulnerable societies.

### 19.4.12 Balancing Transparency with Data Privacy Issue

Transparency is one of its core strengths; all transactions are visible on the ledger. However, this quality sometimes conflicts with privacy requirements, especially with sensitive personal or financial data. In cases like public blockchains, entire transaction histories are open to everyone, potentially revealing personal information inadvertently. The dilemma here causes a significant issue, especially in health care and finance industries where privacy is an issue.

### 19.4.13 Solutions for Protecting Sensitive Information on Blockchains

Using the most advanced encryption and privacy-enhancing technologies to protect sensitive information is crucial. Zero-knowledge proofs and secure multi-party computation are emerging solutions. Zero-knowledge proofs enable a party to verify that a statement is true without revealing the underlying data, thereby preventing the leakage of secret information.

### 19.4.14 Discovery of Sustainable Alternatives

The effort to minimise the environmental footprint of blockchain technology led to the discovery of energy-efficient variants for consensus mechanisms and hybrid approaches. For instance, Ethereum is the blockchain network that mostly migrates from PoW to PoS, with the Ethereum 2.0 upgrade reducing its energy consumption by up to 99.95% \[[8](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_8)\]. Other environment-friendly options involve Proof of Authority and delegated Proof of Stake, adopted by platforms like VeChain and EOS, to offer reduced resource demand.

### 19.4.15 Ensuring Ethical Use in Vulnerable Communities

Blockchain solutions must be guided by responsible technology that will help assist vulnerable groups, such as refugees, unbanked accounts, etc. However, such endeavours must address surveillance risks, data exploitation, and misapplication by governmental or other forces. Ethical issues align with members of vulnerable communities maintaining control over their data, while blockchain applications must not enhance current inequalities.

245 

## 19.5 Future Directions

Blockchain technology is an evolving innovation with substantial potential to transform sectors by advancing social impact, governance, and management practices. Its integration with cutting-edge technologies such as artificial intelligence and Internet of Things (IoT) enhances its application, enabling automation, real-time data collection, and secure decision-making processes. For example, blockchain and IoT ensure supply chain transparency, particularly in agriculture and healthcare, by tracking products’ origins and ensuring compliance with ethical standards \[[26](#C_026_c19.xhtml#r19_26)\]. Estonia’s adoption of blockchain in e-governance exemplifies its capacity to simplify administration and ensure transparency. Decentralised autonomous organisations further foster participatory governance by enabling decentralised decision-making. In business, blockchain promotes sustainable and ethical practices by enhancing trust through immutable records. Platforms like Provenance demonstrate how blockchain can verify supply chain claims, reinforcing corporate social responsibility. Its functionality in automating operations through smart contracts boosts efficiency, while shared ledgers support collaboration, as seen in IBM’s blockchain application in global supply chains. By aligning ethical standards with operational efficiency, blockchain technology has the potential to reshape governance and management practices, ensuring greater transparency and fostering sustainable growth.

## 19.6 Conclusion

Decentralised, transparent, and accountable, blockchain offers an excellent force for social impact. Through blockchain, real-time data monitoring and validation in support of ethical sourcing, the disarming of fraud, and the ability of businesses and organisations to take the sustainability game an extra step further in demonstrating it is possible. The immutability of records in blockchain lends credibility and assurance among various stakeholders towards implementing CSR initiatives and sustainability goals. More than this, blockchain will change governance and management practices when innovative solutions focus on absolute transparency, fight corruption, and empower the marginalised. Decentralised governance structures enable individuals and communities to participate in decision-making for inclusivity and accountability. Blockchain has the power to support sustainable business models and ethical behaviours; hence, it could be something that businesses and governments look to grapple with issues in society and the environment seriously. So, it can steer the pathway towards a fairer and more sustainable future in this way through its progress and adoption.

# 20 Planting the Seeds of Reform The Effect of Blockchain on Seed Management

Sumit Kumar Sharma, Bhawna, Arvind Mewada, and Mohd. Aquib Ansari

DOI: [10.1201/9781003545620-25](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003545620-25)

## 20.1 Introduction

Food security is a critical pillar of any economy and it depends on a food supply chain that is connected to the management of farming resources. This means that the ineffective management of farming resources not only impacts yield but also impacts food security in any nation. Seeds are invaluable assets in farming; their quality and availability are key parameters for evaluating the farming process. Seed accessibility to farmers not only improves yield but also helps farmers to make informed decisions. Traditional supply chain methods hamper the operation of processes due to the involvement of external parties like middlemen; the addition of fake seeds in the supply chain is a reason for making this process unreliable and opaque.

In addition, malpractices of using researchers’ work without their permission, which leads to intellectual property rights infringement cases, add delay and choices in the supply chain. These challenges open the door for the integration of emerging technology solutions in this space that improve the seed supply chain management and increase the farmer’s confidence in the quality of the seeds in supply. Technology seems to be a transformative technology in space that has the ability to revolutionize various processes, including agriculture. Its immutable and decentralized nature offers a safe and transparent platform for verifying and recording transactions. By leveraging blockchain’s resources, it is possible to tackle the challenges faced in established seed management systems, such as traceability, validity, and intellectual property rights \[[1](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_1)\]. The international industry is responsible for managing the supply of high-quality seeds that help in the production of crops in order to ensure food security; however, there have been common challenges in this industry since its inception: those are intellectual property, issues in the supply chain and quality, damages due to transportation, and malpractices in the availability of high-quality seeds in general \[[2](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_2),[3](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_3)\].

Another factor that impacts the smooth delivery of the seeds is the complicated nature of the factors, such as changing the preferences of the farmers from quality seeds to high-producing seeds, regulations, and changes in climate that impact the 249soil and ultimately affect the seed supplies. These fast changes that impact seed supplies create a need for the implementation of new-age technology solutions in managing the supply of seeds that make them traceable, add transparency, and also make the actors in the supply chain accountable for their role in the chain \[[4](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_4),[5](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_5)\]. As blockchain makes the process distributed via assigning a hash to every actor when an actor performs any action in the chain in order to record the transactions performed, as blockchain technology creates the decentralized digital ledger, it manages the transactions with accountability, traceability, and reliability over the responses, which makes this technology enhance the trust, efficiency, and traceability in processes such as supply chain management \[[6](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_6)\].

## 20.2 Understanding the Seed Supply Chain

Various stakeholders are involved in the management of the complex process of supply chain management of seeds from manufacturers to farmers, including the following actors with the challenges particular actors are suffering from \[[7](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_7)\]. Seed producers are primarily institution-owned laboratories, whether private or public, that use various research and development techniques to produce high-quality seeds, sometimes also known as seed breeders. Challenges include competition with other firms and organizations, stealing of their intellectual property rights (IPR) \[[8](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_8),[9](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_9)\], and high costs associated with the research in producing various new breeds of crop seeds based on the problems that arise or solving under any project. Certification institutions play a critical role whenever new seed developed by producers needs to be approved by a certifying government body that gives that certification for regular consumer market selling or distributing, and the problem-fighting certification authority is doping in samples submitted for testing, parallel balancing the high quality and standards of grading the seeds. Seeds are certified and then collected by wholesalers, who are responsible for distributing them to retailers or farmers \[[10](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_10)\].

Challenges faced by wholesalers include eliminating the middleman in the procurement of seeds from producers, which increases the cost of seeds at the source only and further impacts the margins of the wholesaler and retailers in the chain. Retailers are major distributors from which direct farmers or generic customers purchase the seeds for sowing in the crop fields. Inventory management and storing the seeds in the required storage type and cost in that required stage are the primary challenges these retailers are continuously fighting \[[11](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_11)\].

The farmer is an actor who is a major consumer of the seeds for harvesting desired products based on the location and climate of crop fields, frequent changes in climate, soil properties, and the price of the seeds in the supply chain system of seeds are the major issues farmers are suffering from. Another major challenge that impacts the complete supply management of the seed industry is the bookkeeping of entries of seeds \[[5](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_5)\], which includes data fragmentation, which means data related to the transaction of seeds is stored in various storages and even different formats that make it difficult to process collectively. Data reliability and precision over the data stored are other concerns that make the evaluation metric unreliable, as standard metrics in this 250case. Gap and traceability are not practically possible without a reliable source of entries at every point from where seeds pass through, which ultimately impacts the quality of the seeds.

Data security is a major issue that impacts the supply chain most, apart from the data recording challenges faced by the current supply chain management process. One solution for all current challenges, as mentioned above in seeds supply chain management \[[12](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_12),[13](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_13)\], is blockchain technology. It provides various features for automatically recording each step in a distributed manner, ensuring that every actor in the supply chain is informed and able to track the status of each batch of seeds as it moves through the supply process. It tracks the distribution of crops in real time, helping government agencies manage demand and supply balance, control prices for major crops, and oversee the harvesting and procurement systems in the specified areas \[[14](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_14),[15](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_15)\].

This chapter aims to explore the application of blockchain technology in seed management. It will investigate how blockchain can improve seed traceability, guaranteeing that seeds are traced from legitimate suppliers and sustain quality standards. The proposed study summarizes the perspective of blockchain to protect the IPRs of stakeholders such as seed breeders and producers. By analysing these aspects, the study provides insight into seed management, where blockchain technology plays a crucial role in adding reliability to supply chain management.

## 20.3 Challenges in Seed Supply Chain Management

Seed supply management has a complex network of stakeholders, incorporating seed breeders, regulatory bodies, distributors, retailers, and farmers. Each stakeholder plays a vital role at different stages in confirming the authenticity, quality, and timely delivery of seeds \[[16](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_16),[17](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_17)\]. However, the established seed supply chain encounters numerous challenges that can hamper its efficiency and effectiveness. Key challenges faced by stakeholders include the scarcity of traceability, which is one of the prime concerns. There is a significant need for robust traceability systems to track the source, journey, and quality of seeds throughout the supply chain \[[15](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_15)\].

Data fragmentation is another issue, where information about seed quality, production, and distribution is often scattered and fragmented across several systems. This fragmentation obstructs data analysis and decision-making. Quality issues also present a major concern, as confirming seed quality is essential. Contamination, counterfeit seeds, and low-quality seeds can drastically impact crop yields and farmer earnings. IPR safety is another critical challenge, as protecting IPRs associated with seed varieties is crucial for innovation and security. However, challenges in enforcing IPRs can lead to piracy and illegal seed production. The challenges mentioned above can have substantial negative impacts on the seed supply chain. Lack of traceability can hinder deliveries, increase costs, and erode trust among stakeholders \[[18](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_18),[19](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_19)\]. Data fragmentation impedes data-driven decision-making and blocks the identification of trends and patterns. Quality issues can lead to crop failures, financial losses for farmers, and damage to the reputation of seed manufacturers \[[20](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_20)\]. Furthermore, inadequate IPR protection can suppress innovation and restrict investment in seed research and development.

251 

## 20.4 Blockchain as a Solution for Seed Management

Blockchain is an innovative technology that permits secure and evident recording of transactions among multiple computers. It acts as a distributed record, where data is collected in blocks that are linked simultaneously using cryptography. This produces a chain of cell blocks, hence the name “Blockchain.” This technology is an immutable, decentralized, and transparent digital ledger that documents transactions across multiple computers. Every transaction is inserted into a block, linked to preceding blocks, creating a chain \[[16](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_16),[21](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_21)\].

This chain of blocks is distributed throughout a network of computers, making it substantially secure and resistant to tampering. The aim for seed management through blockchain’s key highlights consists of immutability, ensuring that once data is authenticated on a blockchain, it cannot be amended or deleted, thereby safeguarding the integrity of seed records. Transparency ensures that each transaction on the blockchain is noticeable to all participants, progressing accountability and transparency. Traceability in blockchain enables the tracking of seeds from production to utilization, reducing the risk of counterfeit or low-quality seeds and enhancing trust in the supply chain. The primary flow of seeds begins with the breeder as the first actor and extends to the farmer as the end consumer.

This process can be effectively monitored using several key methods. Contract administration involves maintaining all information linked to the licensee, roles, and the logging of actions. Payment administration ensures that every transaction is recorded and that stock books are updated accurately. Seed quality management focuses on tracking high-demand seeds, enabling efficient real-time supply chain management.

In addition, verification and IPR management ensure that all transactions are validated by actors on the blockchain. At the same time, IPRs are securely preserved for the author, who retains hash ownership within the chain. A classified blockchain network can be inaugurated to facilitate secure and efficient seed management \[[22](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_22)\]. This network can engage key stakeholders such as seed producers, retailers, wholesalers, certification authorities, and farmers. Smart contracts, or self-executing contracts with the terms of the agreement directly transcribed into code, can automate several processes, such as payment transactions, quality constraint checks, and IPR licensing.

Key benefits of blockchain for seed management include improved traceability, which is achieved through blockchain, facilitating the tracking of seed extraction from the source to the end-user. Each step of the seed journey, including production, processing, packing, and allocation, can be documented on the blockchain \[[23](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_23)\]. This enhances transparency and responsibility while reducing the risk of counterfeit or inferior seeds. Superior IPR protection is another advantage, as blockchain can better safeguard IPRs associated with seed varieties. By recording ownership details, licensing contracts, and royalty payments on the blockchain, it becomes significantly harder to infringe on IPRs.

In addition, the distributed and immutable nature of blockchain ensures the security and reliability of seed data. All transactions are recorded on the blockchain, making it difficult to manipulate or alter records \[[24](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_24)\]. This strengthens accountability among all stakeholders and fosters trust in the seed supply chain.

252 

## 20.5 Implementation Framework

The implementation of blockchain that is able to handle the primary problems associated with seed supply management requires the below-defined steps and execution logics, which might vary for each use case and environment. In order to implement blockchain, several steps are required for blockchain integration. The process begins with the identification of actors/stakeholders and their roles, including farmers, seed producers, regulatory bodies, distributors, retailers, and technology support providers. Next, the type of blockchain needs to be defined, and a private blockchain, suitable for stakeholders within a single organization, or a public blockchain, which accommodates a large number of stakeholders and ensures greater transparency, needs to be selected. Smart contracts are then set up, establishing the rules required for actions such as seed certification, financial transactions, and quality checks.

A compatibility check follows, ensuring that the system is designed in a way that does not affect the existing workflow while seamlessly connecting with the current system. Additional steps include designing the system architecture and defining all actors, actions, and data layers for both on-chain and off-chain data points. Key data points such as seed ID, variety, quality attributes, expiry date, source details, consumer details, and location should be defined, along with tracking standards like GS1 and tokenization, which may involve fungible or non-fungible tokens for each seed batch.

Smart contract functions are developed for various actions, such as registering, storing, certifying, and validating, along with the logic for triggering events that update the hash whenever executed. Infrastructure implementation involves deploying the network, adding/removing nodes with permissions, and integrating wallets for digital identity. Frontend development is required for user interactions, while the backend enables communication between the front end and the core functionalities of the application. Finally, the system undergoes testing, monitoring, and maintenance, with regular contract updates as needed.

The technical components of the blockchain-based system include smart contracts, which automate and enforce the rules for certification, payments, tracing, and compliance checks. A key component of this is the ERC-721 token, which serves as a template that developers must follow when creating the authentication mechanism for the blockchain system \[[25](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_25)\]. For example, a smart contract processes payment only for verified seed deliveries. Tokenization involves assigning unique codes to seed batches, enabling the tracing of these batches and ensuring security. Cryptographic methods are used to manage seed data integrity and security through techniques like hashing and digital signatures, which allow the safe movement of data within the environment.

Distributed storage ensures that all data points associated with seeds, such as certifications, Internet of Things (IoT) monitoring logs, and transaction records, are safely stored in a distributed system, protecting them from data loss due to failures. Other significant attributes of the system include the ledger, which must be immutable to prevent any tampering or changes to the records. Access control is implemented to assign permissions based on roles, such as regulators, who are the only ones authorized to assign certificate hash keys. The integration environment may utilize platforms like Oracle or SAP to fetch the data required for the process.

253The workflow begins with the registration of the seed in the system, followed by the uploading of data attributes associated with it onto distributed storage as shown in [Figure 20.1](#C_027_c20.xhtml#f20_1). Tracing of all stages starts once the seed is entered into the blockchain network, with verification occurring at each stage as data points move through the system. Whenever a new purchase is made, transactions associated with payment settlement are created for each node in the system. Above all, steps and flow guides for the implementation of a blockchain-based seed management system that enables every stakeholder to perform the required actions without affecting their physical process in a virtual model as well, with more security and a seamless management process.

![Figure 20.1 System workflow for blockchain-based seed management system. Return to text.⏎](images/Fig20_01.jpg)

Figure 20.1 System workflow for blockchain-based seed management system. [Return to text.⏎](#C_027_c20.xhtml#f20_1b)

## 20.6 Case Studies or Hypothetical Scenarios

Following are simulated or real-time examples of the utilization of blockchain technology for seed management. In order to improve farmer confidence in purchasing new seed breeds, a project is currently being tested in India that uses blockchain to verify seed certification with traceability during supply chain movements \[[26](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_26),[27](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_27)\]. In this method, a hash-based QR code is given to users with the seed batch, allowing them to track the origin and stages of distribution, along with verifying the certification associated with that particular seed.

254Analysing the outcomes of this pilot project reveals several optimizations achieved through the use of blockchain for seed certification and tracing. First, it reduces expenditure and time by minimizing human resources involved in the process and eliminating delays caused by paperwork. Second, the immutable nature of blockchain ensures that only high-quality seeds, which meet the standards maintained by regulatory bodies, are passed to farmers. Finally, the accessibility of seed tracing data to farmers improves transparency in the process, enabling them to make informed decisions, which helps improve crop yields. By analysing this real-time application, it becomes clear that the integration of blockchain technology for seed management optimizes the farming process from various angles.

## 20.7 Future Trends and Research Directions

Blockchain technology revolutionizes seed management systems by integrating with emerging technologies such as artificial intelligence (AI) and the IoT \[[28](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_28),[29](#C_027_c20.xhtml#r20_29)\]. The following methods provide an overview of how these integrations can work. AI for predictive analytics in a blockchain network records large amounts of real-time data and stores data points for logging transactions that occur on the network. AI algorithms then analyse these large chunks of data, helping to develop predictive systems that assist farmers in making informed decisions on various processes such as resource allocation and seed breed selection, ultimately improving production. For example, an AI model trained on data generated from the blockchain network for seed management can identify patterns between production and seed utilization. This identified pattern can recommend the best-suited seeds for specific soil types, weather, or climate conditions.

Real-time data collection through IoT devices, such as soil sensors, moisture sensors, and trackers, helps in collecting data points on soil conditions, weather, and soil moisture levels from crop fields. The integration of blockchain ensures secure access to these data with proper access control mechanisms based on the role specified for each stakeholder, reducing the risk of data contamination shared between various users. The proposed blockchain network application in seed management has significant potential for regulatory compliance and quality assurance in seed management. Regulatory compliance within a blockchain network provides immutable records for seed production, certification, and distribution processes, ensuring that all local and global regulations are followed throughout the supply cycle.

This not only simplifies the audit process but also improves trust among stakeholders involved in the supply system. Quality assurance, on the other hand, is achieved through end-to-end tracing of seeds from production to purchase, managed by blockchain. Smart contracts for each node ensure automatic quality measures and guarantee that only certified, high-quality seeds reach the end farmers. However, despite these benefits, the implementation of blockchain in seed management faces major issues. One of the main challenges is the high expense of implementation, as creating, setting up, and maintaining blockchain is an expensive venture, especially for resource-intensive processes like seed management in the agricultural sector.

255In order to overcome this issue, government bodies must encourage the implementation of subsidies. In addition, adoption barriers exist, as various stakeholders involved in seed supply management, such as small-scale suppliers and farmers, lack the technical skills and knowledge necessary to adapt to solutions like blockchain. This barrier can be overcome by offering mass education campaigns to provide skills and knowledge to these stakeholders.

## 20.8 Conclusion

The agricultural sector, burdened by concerns such as soil degradation, resource scarcity, and supply chain ineffectiveness, necessitates innovative solutions. Blockchain technology, with its intrinsic properties of security and immutability, offers a promising possibility to address these challenges. By allowing the creation of a devolved and auditable record of agricultural methods, from seed to shelf, blockchain can substantially enhance the sector’s efficiency, consumer trust, and sustainability. The broader effects of blockchain for agriculture are far-reaching. It can encourage greater precision and accountability, reduce food fraud and forging, and enhance traceability. By optimizing supply chains, blockchain can lessen food waste and guarantee the timely delivery of fresh goods. Moreover, it can encourage farmers by granting them fair benefits and access to market statistics. In order to realize the capability of blockchain in agriculture entirely, a collective effort is required from various investors. Governments, industry leaders, policymakers, and technology providers must work collectively to develop strong blockchain solutions, verify regulatory frameworks, and encourage widespread acceptance. By adopting this transformative technology, we can make a more efficient, sustainable, and unbiased agricultural future.
